Law and Religion in Indonesia
Understanding and managing inter-religious relations, particularly between Muslims and Christians, presents a challenge for states around the world. This book investigates legal disputes between religious communities in the world’s largest majority-Muslim, democratic country, Indonesia. It examines how the interaction between state and religion has influenced relations between religious communities in the transition to democracy.
The book presents original case studies based on socio-legal field research of court disputes in West Java, a majority-Muslim province with a history of radical Islam. These include criminal court cases, as well as cases of judicial review, relating to disputes concerning religious education, permits for religious buildings and the crime of blasphemy.
The book argues that the introduction of democracy has increased the politicization of religion in local contexts. It highlights the way in which religious disputes since 1998 have been localized through the decentralization of power and exacerbated by the central government’s ambivalent attitude towards radical Islamists who disregard the rule of law.
Through a focused examination of Indonesia, as the largest majority-Muslim country in the world, the book examines the challenges facing governments to accommodate minorities and manage religious pluralism, and in doing so furthers understanding of state-religion relations in the Muslim world. This book is of interest to students and scholars of law and society in Southeast Asia; Islam and the state; politics of the courts; and the legal regulation of religious diversity.
Melissa Crouch is Postdoctoral Fellow in the Law Faculty at the National University of Singapore. Her research focuses on law and religion, Islamic law, legal anthropology, and public law.

Preface
The field research for this project was conducted over 10 months between 2009 and 2011 in Depok, Indramayu and Tasikmalaya (West Java), and Jakarta.
All translations from Indonesian to English in this book are the author’s own, unless otherwise indicated. The titles of all laws and court decisions have been translated for ease of reference.
The glossary is based on common Indonesian spelling (with reference to Stevens and Schmidgall-Tellings, 2004; Federspiel, 1995; Esposito, 2003), and Islamic words derived from Arabic are specified with (A).
An earlier version of some sections of Chapter 5 of the book have appeared in Melissa Crouch (2010) ‘Implementing the Regulation on Places of Worship in Indonesia: New Problems, Local Politics and Court Action,’ Asian Studies Review 34 (December): 403–19. Some parts of Chapter 6 of the book have appeared in Melissa Crouch (2012) ‘Law and Religion in Indonesia: The Constitutional Court and the Blasphemy Law,’ Asian Journal of Comparative Law 7(1) (May): 1–46. The case study in Chapter 4 is also discussed in Melissa Crouch (2013) ‘Proselytisation, Religious Diversity and the state in Indonesia: The Offence of Deceiving a Child to Change Religion, in M. Feener and J. Finucane (eds) Proselytising and the Limits of Religious Pluralism in Contemporary Asia. Singapore: ARI Springer Asia Series.

Acknowledgements
I came to the study of Islam and West Java from the margins. Prior to commencing my PhD, I had spent most of my time in Indonesia on the outer islands, outside Java. I stayed with Hindus in Bali, Christians in North Sulawesi and North Maluku, and Muslims in South Sulawesi and South Kalimantan. I experienced the deep tradition of religious tolerance and diversity that runs through the Indonesian archipelago, where Christians are commonly invited to Eid celebrations and Muslims attend festive Christmas parties. Yet I had also seen the refugee camps of internally displaced communities in North Sulawesi and the burnt out ruins of places of worship in North Maluku. This was just one indicator of the threat intolerance and violence posed in the absence of a strong democratic state. As a lawyer, I questioned the role of the law in this context, whether it can help to prevent such situations or only exacerbates inter-religious conflict. Along the quest for answers, I have perhaps only discovered new questions.
This journey, and the book, would not have been possible without the generous assistance of many people and the support of many institutions. My sincere thanks and appreciation go to my Indonesian host-country supervisors. In 2009, Dr Fatimah Husein of the State Islamic University Sunan Kalijaga and the International Consortium for Religious Studies based at Gadjah Mada University, Yogyakarta, graciously hosted me during my field research. In 2010–11, Dr Amelia Fauzia of the Centre for the Study of Culture and Religion, and Dr Dina Affrianty of the Centre for the Study of Islam and Society (PPIM), both of the State Islamic University Syarif Hidayatullah, Jakarta, acted as wonderful hosts during my field research.
Obtaining my field research visa, a feat in its own right, would not have been possible without the generous assistance of the Indonesian Consular General and staff at the Indonesian Consulate in Melbourne. I am also very appreciative of the assistance of Ibu Fatimah and Ibu Amelia in this regard.

1  Introduction
The initial years of the precarious transition to democracy in Indonesia that began in 1998 coincided with some of the worst violence between Muslims and Christians in the history of that country. Violence broke out across the archipelago, dividing communities according to religious affiliation in areas such as Maluku and Central Sulawesi. The arrival of reinforcements, particularly radical Islamic groups, from other islands such as Java, escalated the conflict in these areas beyond local differences. These civil conflicts tore apart neighborhoods and communities that had existed peacefully side by side for many years. They resulted in thousands of casualties, and tens of thousands of people displaced. These clashes were marked by attacks on places of religious significance, such as churches and mosques.
At the same time as these conflicts raged on the peripheral islands of the archipelago, religious minorities on the nation’s most populous island, Java, were also the target of brutal vigilante action. In the years just prior to the fall of President Soeharto (1966–98), large-scale attacks and burnings of churches occurred in cities such as Surabaya and Situbondo (East Java), and Tasikmalaya (West Java).1 At the end of 2000, the Christmas Eve services of over 30 churches across Indonesia were shattered by bombings coordinated by the radical Islamic group Jemaah Islamiyah, leaving 19 dead (Fealy, 2005b: 6). Every Christmas since, thousands of churches across Indonesia have been guarded by sympathetic local Islamic groups to prevent the risk of future attacks.
Islamist perpetrators of violence against Christians have rarely been prosecuted, or have received light sentences in proportion to their crime, while many Christians perceive that violence instigated by Christians against Muslims has been prosecuted more earnestly. When such cases proceed to court, they are often highly contested and controversial, such as the allegations made against three illiterate Catholic farmers from Poso, Central Sulawesi. These men were found guilty of masterminding violent attacks against three Muslim villages in Poso in 1999, and were subsequently executed in 2006.2 The executions went ahead despite legal appeals for presidential clemency, calls from Christian political parties and Pope Benedict XVI for the case to be retried, and harsh criticism from human rights groups. Their deaths led to further protests and demonstrations, as well as triggering incidents of retaliation by Christians angered at the perceived injustice of the outcome (Jakarta Post, 24 September 2006; 2 October 2006).
Although the violence in Maluku and Central Sulawesi largely died down by 2002, intermittent attacks persist. In October 2005, for example, three Christian school girls were cruelly beheaded in an attack by six Muslim men, one of whom was affiliated with the radical Islamist group Jemaah Islamiyah in Sulawesi (Jakarta Post, 22 March 2007). Attacks and campaigns to close Christian churches persist at a rate far exceeding the number of attacks during the New Order (1966–98), particularly in the densely populated, majority-Muslim province of West Java. Conflict over church permits further deteriorated in August 2010, when a pastor and church elder were brutally stabbed by radical Islamists in a confrontation over the construction of a Batak church in Bekasi (West Java) (ICG, 2010). This series of events indicates that although large-scale violence between Muslims and Christians in the reformasi era peaked between 1999 and 2001, sporadic campaigns to close Christian churches or prevent proselytization activities continue to occur at the initiation of a minority of Muslims who are willing to use violence to achieve their goals.
A wide range of factors contribute to these violent incidents, from issues of ethnicity, socio-economic disparity, religious sentiment and local politics. Yet among these various reasons, the issue of proselytization – that is, perceived or real efforts by Christian groups to propagate Christianity – has received little attention. Proselytization is often a central reason behind religious conflict worldwide (An-Na’im, 2009: 2). The lack of attention to this issue is therefore surprising given Indonesia’s religious makeup and the history of Muslim-Christian relations.
Indonesia is the largest Muslim-majority country in the world, and the third largest democratic state. The transition to democracy in 1998 was followed by significant legislative reforms including amendments to the Constitution that inserted a wide range of international human rights, as well as remarkable steps forward politically, with free and fair elections. It has led to the affirmation of judicial independence and the establishment of freedom of the press. It has seen the notorious ‘dual function’ of the military dismantled, with the abolition of seats reserved for the military in the legislature. It has also led to the strengthening of non-judicial mechanisms of review of government action, such as the National Human Rights Commission, as well as the creation of other avenues as a check on the power of government officials, such as the office of the Ombudsman. There has also been a remarkable transfer of power to the regional level due to a widespread process of decentralization.
The change brought about by the introduction of democracy and decentralization has opened up greater freedom of expression and practice for religious communities. Religion is deeply embedded in Indonesian culture, and almost 90 per cent of Indonesians follow Islam. The remaining population is 5.7 per cent Protestant, 3 per cent Catholic, 1.7 per cent Hindu, 0.06 per cent Buddhist, and 0.01 per cent Confucian (Ministry of Religion, 2010b). The state is based on the Pancasila ideology, which includes the belief in Almighty God. Officially, the state recognizes and gives preferential treatment to six religions (agama): Islam, Protestantism, Catholicism, Hinduism, Buddhism and Confucianism.3 A range of religious communities and local beliefs exist outside the state-sanctioned religions, and there is also great diversity within religious traditions (see Chapter 3). In the past, this diversity has been the cause of both social pluralism and conflict.
This book is about the two major religions in Indonesia: Islam and Christianity. While a traditional understanding of the relationship between these communities casts the debate as one of majority versus minority, this book seeks to add nuance and explore the complexity of this relationship. First, Christians are a majority in some parts of Indonesia, while Muslims are a minority in other provinces. Status as a minority, therefore, depends on context. Second, Christianity has historically occupied a very privileged and central position since independence and many Christians exert significant influence in sectors such as the military, the police, politics, the judiciary and the legal profession. Even if Christianity is the religion of a minority in some areas, at the national level it has enjoyed far greater representation due to its recognition as an official religion by the state, in comparison to other religious minorities that fall outside the six recognized religions.
This book primarily focuses on West Java, where Muslims are a majority and Christians a minority. Yet the developments in West Java will be situated in broader national, historical and social context. The findings of this book are therefore significant not only for other areas of Indonesia, but also speak into ongoing debates on religion-state relations in countries around the globe. First, it relates to the challenge of governments worldwide to accommodate minorities, and to manage religious pluralism and diversity. Second, it addresses the issues specific to developing and consolidating democracies where, in the absence of a strong state and law enforcement agencies, the use of violence threatens to trump the resolution of disputes through the legal system. Further, this book focuses particularly on the minority of Muslims in Indonesia who support the establishment of Islamic law. This is a diverse group, who I will refer to as ‘Islamists,’ or, as they are known in Indonesia, garis keras, ‘hardliners’ (see further explanation in Chapter 3). A small proportion of this group use violence to achieve their goals, who I will refer to as ‘radical Islamists.’ This book focuses on this diverse group of actors in order to further understanding of state-religion relations and religious plurality in the Muslim world.
Faith in the law? Boundaries of proselytization
In Indonesia, it is assumed that the two largest religious communities, Christianity and Islam, are missionary religions that aim to proselytize (Ricklefs, 2008a: 334). There is no direct translation of the term ‘proselytization’ in Bahasa Indonesia. Indonesian Christians often refer to the term misi, mission, while Muslims use the Arabic term dakwah (lit. to call). Proselytization includes all efforts to attempt to convert or persuade a person to a particular religion and religious community. Proselytization can be characterized as any effort towards conversion. Conversion out of Islam is, of course, considered to be apostasy (see Chapter 6).
When conversion does occur, it has two main consequences. First, conversion implies a shift in allegiance from one religious tradition and community to another. Efforts to convert a person are also about seeking new members of an institution or community. The shift in religious boundaries and membership brought about by conversion potentially disrupts religious communities. This destabilizes the role of world religions in their capacity to assert ‘authoritative control of identity’ (Hefner, 1993: 122). Ultimately, conversions between religions threaten the power of both political parties and religious leaders in contexts where popularity depends on the favor of a stable religious majority. Conversions may lead to the insult of a religion, where converts demonize or denigrate the religion they left behind, or challenge the teachings of the religion they have entered. As both Christianity and Islam are recognized as religions with a missionary agenda in Indonesia, the issue of conversions between religions is a cause of real tension and conflict between their adherents.
Within Islam it is generally considered acceptable for Muslims to engage in religious proselytizing (dakwah). In the twentieth century, the dakwah efforts of Muslims worldwide were largely driven by concerns of encroaching Christianization (Saeed and Saeed, 2004: 117). Indonesia was no exception. This is because conversions from Islam to Christianity are the most common religious conversions in Indonesia (Saeed and Saeed, 2004: 115). Christian religious activities raise fears and suspicions of a conversion agenda, particularly for Islamists. There is a high level of intolerance of conversion from Islam, that is, apostasy, which is perceived as a real threat to Islam and to the Muslim community. Nevertheless, there are no legal restrictions imposed by the state in Indonesia that prevent a person from converting to another religion, unlike in neighboring Malaysia.
One point of contention that often arises is the motives behind proselytization efforts. A basic assumption of many who proselytize is that their religion offers a better alternative, implying religious and often cultural superiority. In the context of Christian proselytization towards Muslims, it is often taken as suggesting or implying that Christianity is somehow ‘better’ than Islam. Indeed, conversion from one religion to another involves a conscious rejection of the former religion. Conversion from Islam to Christianity may therefore be perceived as the outright rejection of Islam (Ricklefs, 2008b: 133). The ultimate goal of proselytization – conversion – presents a risk and a challenge to the identity and stability of a Muslim community (An-Na’im, 2009: 6).
On the other hand, common objections to proselytization are often based on problematic assumptions; for example, it may be assumed that proselytization is always coercive and cannot occur on equal terms because such efforts are only successful if the proselytizers have a material advantage over the target group (An-Na’im, 2009: 6). This is partly because of the competitive nature of proselytization which, at its extreme, may result in a ‘war for souls,’ where mutual defamation occurs and laws are manipulated for the religious advantage of the majority (ibid.: 4).
In Indonesia, attitudes towards Christian proselytization vary both within and between Islam and Christianity. Most Indonesian Muslims are not necessarily against Christian proselytization. Rather, they contest the kind of proselytization that is permissible or acceptable. Two concepts have been increasingly reinforced by government policy since independence: the distinction between religions and local beliefs, and the idea of a fixed religious identity as a way of preventing conversions between religions (see Chapter 3).
The debate over proselytization between Muslims and Christians in Indonesia is focused on who is ‘fair game’ for efforts of proselytization and what are acceptable terms of engagement. Indonesian Muslims generally argue that a person who already has a religion must not be the target of religious proselytization. Since independence, Islamists have demanded that Christian proselytization should be limited to those who do not yet have a religion – that is, who do not have one of the six official religions listed on their national identity card (particularly ‘Islam’). This conviction suggests religious affiliation and identity with a world religion such as Islam should be relatively fixed, certain and permanent. It also implies that once a person enters a religious community, there is little possibility of moving to another religion.
In addition, Muslims contest and oppose methods of proselytization that are perceived to be ‘aggressive’ (agresif) or situations where a person is suspected of being ‘coerced’ (dipaksa) or ‘deceived’ (ditipu) into converting. Protestant and Catholic Christians are also generally in agreement that deceptive tactics should not be used, although the definition of what amounts to deceptive proselytization may differ. The concept of proselytization is complicated by notions of ‘legitimate’ and ‘illegitimate’ proselytization, which in Indonesia are often determined by who the ‘target’ is and the methods used to proselytize. The purpose of this book is not to determine what are or should be legitimate forms of proselytization, but rather to explore the effects or influence of local understandings and methods of proselytization on the regulation of religion in the Indonesian context.
The perpetuation of suspicions and fears between Muslims and Christians has in part been caused by stereotypes of Kristenisasi (Christianization). This term is generally used by Indonesian Muslims to refer to Christian missionary practices or activities that aim to convert Muslims by using unfair methods to prey on vulnerable groups, such as children, the poor and the sick. Examples of proselytization methods that Islamists cite as ‘deceptive’ include the building of Christian hospitals, schools and churches (particularly in Muslim-majority areas), or giving aid or material assistance to non-Muslims with the aim of converting them to Christianity.
It is not just Muslims who oppose manipulative Christianization, but also Protestants and Catholics, because these churches often lose members to the newer, independent Evangelical and Pentecostal congregations (see Chapter 3). In Indonesia, most concerns over Christianization are in relation to Protestant, Evangelical or Pentecostal Christianity, as opposed to Catholicism, which has historically been distinguished from Protestantism in Indonesia (see Chapter 3). Established Christian organizations seek to distance themselves from the ‘aggressive’ practices of newer, evangelical organizations in order to maintain good relations with the Muslim community.
There will always be fear of proselytization between members of Islam and Christianity because a key aspect of both religions is a missionary nature. Democracy creates greater challenges for religious tolerance, acceptance of pluralism and inter-religious relations due to the decrease in government control over religious groups, as opposed to authoritarian forms of government. This book focuses on legal responses to proselytization because of the increasing importance of legal reforms in an era of democratization. It will build on the literature on Muslim-Christian relations in New Order Indonesia (1966–98), particularly Ropi (1998), Husein (2005) and Mujiburrahman (2008). It is, however, distinctive because it provides an analysis of how Islamists oppose proselytization by other faiths, why this opposition has increased, and the influence this has had on legal disputes between Muslims and Christians.
In doing so, it will argue that Islamist responses – including violence against Christians, initiating law reform based on Islamic law, and pursuing prosecutions against Christians – have been made possible due to the politicization of religion under democracy and the decentralization of power to the local level, underlined by ongoing, long-held fears Muslims have of Christianization. This affects the extent to which the resolution of legal disputes can accommodate religious pluralism. It also causes religious minorities to question whether they can have faith in the legal system.
Negotiating Muslim-Christian conflict
This book examines how Islamists have contested the perceived or real threat of Christian proselytization in democratic Indonesia. First, radical Islamists have responded with threats, intimidation campaigns and violent attacks on churches they believe have violated the law or have overstepped ‘Islamic’ standards of acceptability. Second, they have advocated for national and local laws that reflect aspects of Islamic law, and have participated in the creation and drafting of Bills, primarily through the Indonesian Ulama Council and the Ministry of Religion.
A third response is that they have instigated investigations, filed reports with police, influenced public prosecutors and pressured judges to convict Christians who have allegedly committed offences against Islam, such as blasphemy. Radical Islamists have petitioned local authorities to cancel building permits for some churches because the construction of the church is seen to overstep the boundaries of permissible proselytization. Local Islamic religious leaders have also actively lodged criminal allegations with the police concerning issues of blasphemy. Combined with the intimidation and vigilante tactics of radical Islamic groups, this has contributed to the convictions of the accused in court. In cases of judicial review of laws concerning religious issues, the judiciary has overwhelmingly upheld the validity of limitations on the right to proselytize. The issue of radical Islamic groups taking the law into their own hands is not new. There is, however, an increasing process of judicialization taking place as groups resort to legal means – via the legislature and judiciary – to justify and legalize their position.
This book examines how Muslim opposition to Christianization has increased since 1998 due to the twin processes of democracy and decentralization, combined with an historical narrative of resentment towards Christian mission activities. It then focuses on the resolution of legal disputes between Muslims and Christians at the local level through three case studies in West Java, a majority-Muslim province with a high level of attacks on Christian churches. It demonstrates that the courts, independent institutions intended to protect the democratic rights of citizens and uphold the rule of law, but which have always been weak in Indonesia, are prone to corruption and intimidation by radical Islamists at the local level. Trials are vocally supported by demonstrations or campaigns by radical Islamic groups during the court hearing. This atmosphere of animosity raises questions about the impartiality and independence of judicial decisions.
In response, many Christians are intimidated in the face of violence without the protection of state enforcement agencies and choose not to pursue legal action in defense. Others, however, have begun to test the waters of their democratic rights by appealing to independent human rights bodies, both national and international. Christians have participated in the political process through debates, advocacy and political parties. Others have vigorously defended criminal allegations, initiated appeals and cases of judicial review of laws or challenging the administrative decisions of local governments that they consider to be beyond the powers of executive agencies. Part of the motivation for taking such actions may be fears of Islamisasi. Islamization, however, is understood by Indonesian Christians as efforts by Islamists to establish Indonesia as an Islamic state under Islamic law. This concept emphasizes the potential threat to religious freedom and the fear of being a religious minority under an Islamic state, rather than a fear of Islamic dakwah efforts to convert individuals. It is largely fears and suspicions of Christianization, not Islamization, that have inspired and been articulated in government regulations, first under the New Order and, with greater frequency, during the reformasi era.
In assessing the responses of Christians since 1998, this book explores why Christians are becoming more active in asserting rights and opposing restrictions on proselytization, and what effect this is having on the resolution of inter-religious disputes. Central to this book is the dilemma of negotiating tensions between the rights of religious communities in a democracy. In West Java, Islamists have dominated the agenda and the rights of the Muslim majority are being exercised in a way that deprives minorities, such as Christians, of their rights. Overall, this book contributes to a deeper understanding of how the dynamics of legal disputes between Muslims and Christians are shaped and negotiated by the complex issue of proselytization.
Outline of the book
Part I sets the broader context for this book. Chapter 2 begins by examining the historical origins for opposition to Christian proselytization in Indonesia and the policies that developed under the New Order. It provides a brief overview of the colonial era and the favoritism, real and perceived, that colonial authorities showed to Christianity. This chapter then focuses on the crucial period of the 1960s, when the perceived threat of communism saw many suspected communists, or those who did not adhere to a world religion, convert to Christianity. At the insistence of Islamists, the president introduced the criminal offence of blasphemy, and the Minister of Religion issued regulations on matters such as segregated religious education in schools, permits for places of worship, and limitations on religious proselytization.
Chapter 3 analyses how religious communities and their leaders, particularly Muslims and Christians as adherents of the two largest religions in Indonesia, use state processes and institutions to reform or introduce laws on religion that apply to all religious communities. It argues that efforts at legal reform since 1998 reflect the growing fears Muslims have of Christianization – that is, of efforts by Christians to convert Muslims to Christianity. By identifying key religious institutions within the bureaucracy, it demonstrates how these bodies are being influenced by Islamists to advocate policies favorable to their agenda of implementing Islamic law. It also looks at one of the most significant changes since decentralization – the proliferation of religious regulations at the regional level – and the symbolic influence of this trend on legal disputes between Muslims and Christians in West Java.
In Part II this book examines three legal case studies. According to former Minister of Religion Taher Tarmizi (1993–98), the potential for conflict between religions in Indonesia mainly occurs due to one of the following three situations: 1 when proselytization is aimed at gaining converts from adherents of another religion; 2 when places of worship are built without permits; and, 3 when a person insults another person’s religion (Taher, 1997: 16–17). These fears – of conversions between religions, of unlicensed places of worship and of insults to a religion – are at the heart of the three case studies. These three issues are also of central importance to countries across the globe, as they grapple with the challenges of religious pluralism.
The first case study (Chapter 4) raises issues of religious education and child protection. This chapter focuses on the introduction of Law 20/2003 on Education and the implementation and interpretation of religious instruction policies in both public and private schools. It analyses the Indramayu Religious Education Case, in which three Christian religious education teachers were charged with deceiving Muslim primary school children to change their religion. This is a criminal offence of deceiving a child to change his or her religion in Law 23/2002 on Child Protection (art. 86) and is related to fears of conversion through religious education. This book provides a fresh analysis of the controversy over child conversions and unfair proselytization. It argues that reforms to the law and court cases on issues of religious education and child protection have validated and further entrenched the fears, stereotypes and suspicions Muslims have of efforts to proselytize through Christian schools. This is because perceived or real attempts to convert children are seen as a threat to the next generation and the future of religious communities more broadly.
The second case study (Chapter 5) addresses the regulation of permits for a place of worship and the symbolic value of religious buildings as sites of proselytization. This book considers the regulation of permits as part of the broader discourse on proselytization and accusations of Christianization. It uses the example of the Depok Church Permit Case, in which a church challenged a decision of the mayor to cancel its building permit. The chapter canvasses the problems related to the process to obtain a permit for a place of worship, introduced by the national Joint Regulation 8 & 9/2006. It argues that churches are often sites of conflict because they are seen as platforms for Christianization, rather than merely as sites of worship and instruction for the Christian community. This has the potential to be used by Islamists to destabilize Muslim-majority communities. Permit disputes highlight the acute limits of legal mechanisms, in the absence of local political will, to protect the rights of religious minorities. This includes both the limited influence of the judiciary and non-judicial mechanisms of review.
The third case study (Chapter 6) raises issues of conversion, apostasy and blasphemy, particularly conversions from Islam to Christianity. This chapter examines the specific meaning and application of blasphemy in the Indonesian context. While issues of blasphemy and heresy have a long history within Christianity worldwide,4 this chapter centers on contemporary understandings of the offence of ‘insulting a religion,’ particularly Islam. This chapter is concerned with cases in which Christians have been accused of insulting Islam, such as the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, in which a Muslim convert to Christianity was convicted for insulting Islam. The enforcement of the criminal offence of blasphemy, as set out in article 156a of the Indonesian Criminal Code, is linked to cases of conversions from Islam to Christianity. It will be shown that prosecutions of Christians for blasphemy function like an apostasy law, allowing Islamists publicly to condemn conversions from Islam to Christianity.
In conclusion, Chapter 7 draws together this discussion of law and religion in Indonesia, highlighting how Islamists have used legal means to oppose Christian proselytization, and questioning whether minorities can assert their rights in fair and impartial legal forums. It demonstrates that hostility to Christian proselytization has intensified, taking the form of vigilante violence as well as campaigns for laws based on Islam and efforts to convict Christians for overstepping the boundaries of proselytization. This is because of the greater freedoms and opportunities for religious communities to influence law reforms under democracy, combined with a competitive local political environment in a decentralized Indonesia. It asserts that real and perceived fears of Christian proselytization continue to fuel tension between Muslims and Christians in Indonesia. Sporadic campaigns of intimidation by radical Islamists against Christians have persisted, laws have been introduced in line with syariah, and Christians have been convicted and imprisoned for offences against Islam. This is because the combination of democratic freedoms, the decentralization of power and a history of opposition to Christian proselytization has led to religious majoritarianism at the local level, at the expense of the rights of religious minorities. Christians have exercised their democratic rights in court but, even if a case is resolved in their favor, such decisions have little practical effect in the face of state ambivalence towards the vigilante action of radical Islamists who disregard the rule of law.
Notes
1  For an analysis of this religious violence between 1995 and 1997, in the lead-up to the transition to democracy, see Sidel, 2006: 68–105.
2  For an analysis of the procedural shortcomings in the investigation and trial, see McRae, 2007.
3  According to the Elucidation to Presidential Decision 1/1965 on the Prevention of Misuse and/or Disrespect of Religion (explained in Chapter 2). The explanation to article 1 states: ‘This does not mean that other religions or beliefs, such as Judaism, Zoroastrianism, Shintoism or Taoism, are banned.’ As long as they do not disturb the community, adherents of other religions are also free to practice their religion in principle (Ministry of Religion, 2008a: 181–85). As this book shows, however, this right is limited in practice.
4  There is a large body of scholarship on heresy and blasphemy in Christianity, for example, see Levy, 1993, 1981.

Part I
Context

2  Hostility to Christian activities
Origins and policies
State policies on religious affairs are partly a reflection of the struggles and dynamics between religious communities in the past. Indonesian Muslims’ attitudes towards Christians and Christian activities have been shaped by colonialism and have influenced the development of policies on religion under the New Order. This chapter examines the links between colonialism and Christianization, particularly through institutions such as Christian schools. It questions how and why colonialism raised concerns among Muslim leaders that the Muslim community was being unfairly targeted for Christian proselytization.
After the demise of colonialism, the 1960s became a crucial part of this narrative, as the threat of communism and a sense of crisis prompted many suspected communists to convert to Christianity. These events motivated some Muslims to lobby the government to introduce a series of policies or restrictions on religious activities. This chapter demonstrates how Islamists, particularly from the Indonesian Ulama Council (Majelis Ulama Indonesia, MUI), successfully campaigned for the introduction of policies limiting religious activities under the New Order. This included segregated religious instruction in schools, the criminal offence of insulting a religion, the regulation of church permits, and limitations on religious proselytization. Each of these policies has had a lingering impact on the way the government manages religious communities and regulates religious affairs.
Colonialism and Christian missions
As in many countries, prejudices and stereotypes of Christian proselytization in Indonesia are overshadowed by the legacy of colonialism. In the early 1500s, the Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive in Indonesia and with them the Roman Catholic Church.1 In the 1600s, the Dutch took over as the dominant foreign power in Indonesia and under its control foreign Christian missionary efforts were initially restricted (see generally Steenbrink, 2008). During the entire period of the Dutch East Indies Company (Vereenigde Oost Indische Compagnie, VOC, 1602–1799), Catholic workers were not allowed (Steenbrink, 2003: 7). In contrast, the Protestant Church was one of the departments of the VOC (Klinken, 2003: 9, 13). This had the effect of polarizing Protestantism and Catholicism, as noted in more detail in Chapter 3.
Further, up until the 1900s, the Dutch consciously excluded Christian mission organizations and workers from areas of the archipelago that were ‘economically important or religiously sensitive’ (Nyhus, 1987: 20). This sentiment was echoed in reports to the Dutch government arguing for restrictions on Christian missions. For example, Governor-General Baud (1883–86) was of the opinion that ‘it is in the interests of the Dutch government to restrict the [Christian] mission as much as possible.’2 This implied that religion, particularly a foreign Western religion such as Christianity, was perceived as a force that must be consciously controlled in order to maintain and advance political power in a majority-Muslim country.
Limitations on proselytization were also set out in the Constitution of the Netherlands Indies (Regerings-Reglement) of 1854. According to article 123, all Christian teachers, priests and ministers were required to obtain permission from the governor-general to work in a particular area (Steenbrink, 2003: 208). In addition to permits for all individuals, this policy reinforced the distinction between Protestants and Catholics by giving churches exclusive rights to work in certain areas (Nyhus, 1987: 20). For example, the island of Flores and the provinces of South Kalimantan and South Sulawesi were only open to Catholic missionaries, while Sumba (East Nusa Tenggara) and Batak areas such as Tapanuli (North Sumatra) were restricted to Protestants (KWI, 1975:25). Klinken (2003: 11) notes that ‘most important among the results achieved by these private missions was the large Batak Protestant Church.’ Under a policy introduced in 1854, parts of Indonesia were divided up into exclusive Christian religious domains, although Protestant Christians usually had the upper hand.
Despite these formal restrictions, Christian missionaries were not only permitted but encouraged and supported by the Dutch colonial government to pursue a missionary agenda (Shihab, 1995: 273). Evidence cited for this favoritism includes educational advantages, financial resources and other facilities provided by the government to Christians (Shihab, 1995: 275). For example, the colonial government paid the salaries of some religious leaders, and the disproportionately ‘high salaries of Catholic priests and Protestant ministers caused many critical remarks from Muslim deputies, who concluded that Christianity was a privileged religion’ (Steenbrink, 2007: 6). This stirred feelings of resentment and jealousy towards converts to Christianity who enjoyed the same privileges as the Dutch and were believed to be ‘protected’ by them (Akkeren, 1970: 113). This led to a strong association between Dutch colonialism and Christianity, and those who converted to Christianity were seen as belonging to the Dutch, the ‘other side’ (Boland, 1982: 224).
Concerns over foreign and aggressive Christian proselytization did not abate after independence from colonial rule in 1945, partly because the colonial restrictions on mission no longer applied and arguably led to greater freedom for Christian proselytization (Hasyim, 1979: 306). This perpetuated the concern that Christianity was competing with Islam for control of the country (Shihab, 1995: 246). In the mid-2000s, the Islamic Prosperous Justice Party (Partai Keadilan Sejahtera, PKS) blamed missionary efforts under colonialism for inciting tensions between religions:
The colonial invasion began with the arrival of the Portuguese (who brought Catholicism) and the Dutch (who brought Protestantism), which caused conflict and local opposition.
(PKS, 2007: 162)
Apprehension over Christian missionary activities and conversions, combined with an awareness of past Christian Crusades and European colonialism, produced defensive reactions from radical Islamists in Indonesia (Taher, 1997: 116). The former Minister of Religion (1993–98), Tarmizi Taher, articulated the consciousness of past injustices committed by Christians against Muslims (Taher, 1997: 66):
Throughout history, Muslim and Christian (or Western) relations have been colored by the fierce battles of the Crusades, Western colonial expansion and domination, and bitter rivalry between Christian and Muslim missionaries.
In particular, he linked the challenges facing Indonesia with the broader issues confronting Muslim-majority countries. According to Taher (1997: 139):
There has been much concern in the Muslim world about an aggressive campaign to missionize and convert Muslims to Christianity, more so because the campaign was seen as funded and backed by foreign powers and the means and methods used to preach Christianity were hardly Christian.
This agenda was believed to be linked to the construction and establishment of organizations such as churches, Christian hospitals and schools. The extent to which colonial authorities supported the expansion of Christian schools is debated, but clearly by the end of the nineteenth century the government openly supported Christian schools.
Christian education in Indonesia began in the 1540s in the Maluku islands, where Christian teaching was intertwined with missionary activities (Kelabora, 1976: 231). The initial policy of the Dutch government on religious education during the colonial era declared that religion was not to be taught as a school subject in government schools, avoiding the issue of religious instruction in public schools entirely (Kelabora, 1976: 233). Further, prior to 1890, the Dutch denied requests for subsidies by Christian mission schools to avoid attracting any suspicion from the Muslim community (Shihab, 1995: 245).
The change came in the late nineteenth century when the Dutch government began actively to support Christian-based education through the provision of funding, which was not available to the same extent for Islamic schools (Husein, 2005: 66). For example, between 1936 and 1939, a disproportionate sum of money was allocated to Protestant and Catholic schools compared to Islamic schools (Saerozi, 2004: 134). Christian schools enjoyed this privileged position in Indonesia until the Japanese occupation in 1942 when some private Christian schools were forced to close (Sairin, 1996b: 258). Aside from this, the financial support of the Dutch colonial government, which also extended to other Christian endeavors such as the establishment of hospitals, was perceived to give Christians an unfair advantage over Muslims in terms of their ability to attract converts to Christianity (Taher, 1997: 36). Christian education and health services were unable to be matched by Muslim schools or medical treatment at the time (Noer, 1973: 23). This led to complaints that ‘Christian missionaries invaded [Muslim] areas and converted their children through Western education, which promoted Christian values’ (Shihab, 1995: 312).
In addition to the provision of funding to Christian schools, former Minister of Religion (1993–98) Tarmizi Taher argued that under the colonial government, policies were introduced that impeded the development of Islamic schools, such as restricting independent Islamic schools and religious teachers that Dutch authorities considered difficult to control (Taher, 1997: 36). Islamic religious leaders were under the close scrutiny of the Dutch authorities because they were perceived as a threat to colonial powers (Steenbrink, 2003: 228).
One consequence of the shift in policy was that from 1909 to 1912, Christian mission schools in Indonesia increased by 40 per cent (Shihab, 1995: 245). This additional support for Christian schools is part of the reason that these institutions are able to provide, and have earned a reputation for, high-quality education. A Christian education offered a sense of security for parents who were keen to send their children to mission schools not only for the education but also for other benefits such as medical services (Reid, 1993: 169–70). The popularity and high quality of Christian education also means that there has been a disproportionate number of Christians represented in the professions and middle class because of the advantage Christians gained to higher education and state resources over Muslims (Hefner, 2000: 140).
In addition, many high-profile Indonesian Muslim parents began sending their children to Christian schools. These parents were accused of allowing their children to be converted to Christianity, even though many of the teachers at these schools were Indonesian Muslims (Noer, 1973: 7–8). One example of a high-profile Muslim who attended a Christian school as a child is Hadji Agus Salim (1884–1954), who played a prominent role in the creation of the 1945 Constitution and served as Indonesia’s foreign minister (1947–49). His parents encountered strong opposition from family and friends over the fact that their child Agus Salim attended a Christian school, because it was thought that he would convert to Christianity (Noer, 1973: 7). Noer notes that it was also not uncommon for a kiai (Islamic religious leader) or group of ulama to issue a fatwa declaring it haram (forbidden) for Muslim parents to send their child to a Christian (that is, Dutch) school (Noer, 1973: 8). Many Muslim parents still send their children to Christian schools today, however.
It was not only the legacy of colonialism, mission and the establishment of Christian schools that raised suspicions amongst Muslim leaders of a Christianization agenda, but also the ideological and religious crisis created by the attempted communist coup in 1965.
Communism and conversions
In the 1960s, pamphlets distributed by Muhammadiyah, the largest modernist Islamic organization in Indonesia (see Chapter 3), claimed that Christians had a scheme to Christianize Indonesia. The pamphlets warned of a joint conference that took place in Malang and made plans to Christianize Java in 20 years and all of Indonesia in 50 years.3 Sources are unclear whether the conference was held in 1962 or 1963, and some even dispute whether it was held at all.4 According to Hasyim (1979: 271), the methods the Christian conference participants were advocating to achieve this aim include: inter-religious marriage; building churches in majority-Muslim areas; financial assistance for poor Muslims; using Arabic to relate the Christian message to Muslims; and building Christian schools. Hasyim (1979: 274) also claims that in July 1970 another Christian conference took place in Magelang (Central Java), where plans were made to ensure a church is built in every regency in Indonesia.
From the 1950s to the early 1970s, as rumors of these pamphlets circulated, there was a significant increase in reported conversions to Christianity. Table 2.1 demonstrates that the total number of Christians grew from less than 3 per cent of the population in 1933, to almost 8 per cent of the population in 1971 and over 9 per cent in 2005. This growth was particularly concentrated in East and Central Java, where an unexpected and unprecedented conversion phenomenon to Christianity occurred.5
Figures relied on by Christians suggest a large number of conversions, evidenced by baptism, in a very short space of time.6 For example, in Kalimantan between 1965 and 1974, the Protestant Church baptized 300,000 people; in East Java from July to August 1966 alone, the East Java Christian Church baptized 10,000 people; and from 1966 to 1967, the Karo Baptist Protestant Church recorded 26,000 baptisms (Barrett, 2001: 382–84). Hefner captures the reaction of shock and disbelief in the Muslim community by noting that ‘the Muslim leadership was caught off guard by this rapid Christian advance’ (Hefner 2000: 108). These reported conversions have, however, been criticized or discounted as overnight and temporary. Others, such as Catholic priest and scholar Father Frans Magnis-Suseno, claim that the growth was more incremental and long-lasting, at least in the Catholic Church, which requires a person to undergo a training course before they can be baptized into the church.8
Table 2.1 Indonesian population according to religion7
Overall, there appears to have been significant growth in the number of Christians (both Catholics and Protestants) from 1930s to 2000s. Numerous reasons have been offered to explain these large-scale conversions. First, many converts are believed to have been former communists who wished to deny any former association with communism after the attempted communist coup in 1965 and the banning of the Indonesian Communist Party in 1966 (Aritonang, 2004: 365). In the 1960s, the Indonesian Communist Party was the biggest communist party aside from China and Russia (Sihombing, 2008: 28–30). After 1965, even association with communists led to arrest, imprisonment or even death.9 One of the reasons communists, and Chinese Indonesians, turned to one of the world religions is because a lack of religious affiliation raised fatal suspicions that a person may be connected with communism. For example, if a village did not have a mosque or a church, then it was at risk of being suspected of being communist.10
The government was at the forefront of a public campaign against communism and in favor of religion, encouraging atheists and animist believers to adopt one of the world religions (Smith-Kipp, 1995: 876). The approach of the government was essentially that ‘the making of believers was the unmaking of Communists’ (Thomson, 1968: 8). This policy was evident in government rhetoric. In the late 1960s, President Soeharto encouraged the people of Indonesia to embrace a religion, ‘otherwise we will give PKI [the Indonesian Communist Party] the opportunity to grow again,’ and he appealed for help from established religions to ‘unite against PKI’ (Presidential Speech, 1968: 29–32; 1969: 7–8). The inherent danger of being associated with communism, combined with changes in government policy on matters of religion, ‘drove millions of Indonesians to seek refuge in religious identity, institutions and faith to avoid fatal charges of atheism …’ (Sidel, 2006: 51). The pressure on Chinese Indonesians to adhere to a religion was particularly acute. For example, Fox (1980: 243) links the significant number of conversions that took place in Kupang (East Nusa Tenggara) to a military officer in Kupang who ‘instructed the Chinese that they had two weeks to choose a religion.’ It was in this atmosphere of crisis that many former communists converted to Christianity in the face of death.
Aside from the fears of former or suspected communists who wished to avoid being the subject of attack, a second reason offered for these mass conversions is that ‘churches offered protection to those who had been suspected of involvement in Communist activities’ more so than Muslim communities (Shihab, 1995: 304). The church became a shelter or haven for communists. The sympathy extended to suspected communists by Christians was crucial for many families that were torn apart by the violence against suspected communists (Hefner, 2000: 108). By contrast, Muslims were generally hostile towards ex-communists and Islamic organizations such as Nahdlatul Ulama (NU) were involved in orchestrating the violent attacks and mass killings of communists.11 In particular, the West Java branch of MUI actively participated in the arrest, detention and killings of communists after 1965 (Mufrodi, 1994: 92).
Religious organizations also received some assistance from the government in spreading their teachings. For example, in the 1970s, the Ministry of Religion sponsored the efforts of the national Protestant and Catholic councils to distribute magazines and paket dakwah (lit. proselytization packets) to prisons, where many suspected communists were detained. In particular, the government sponsored efforts to distribute thousands of brochures and paket dakwah to political prisoners (tahanan G30S/PKI) detained in Tapol Pulau Buru (South Maluku); and RPC Cipinang and RPC Bukit Duri, both prisons in Jakarta (Presidential Speech, 1973: 455–76; 1976: 597–614; 1977: 648–67). The national Protestant Council also helped former communists reintegrate into the community once they were released from jail and these ex-political prisoners often converted to Christianity in the process (Aritonang, 2004: 413). Many churches were also at the forefront of efforts to care for the widows and orphans of political prisoners, providing material aid and shelter (Thomson, 1968). Churches were criticized, however, for their lack of public protest against the killings that took place (Webb, 1986: 110).
In addition to practical assistance provided by the church, some Christian accounts also attribute these conversions to spiritual occurrences such as miracles (Willis, 1978). Not all conversions were the direct result of the efforts of churches, practical or miraculous. During this period, there are stories that some people, and in some cases entire villages, proclaimed themselves Christian without previous contact with any Christians (Boland, 1982: 232).
These conversions may also have been the result of a third contributing factor – that is, the new government policies on religion that were introduced, which reinforced the fixed nature of religious identity. Such strategies formed part of Soeharto’s agenda of maintaining political power by containing sensitive issues, such as religion. This policy was exemplified by the use of the acronym ‘SARA’: ethnicity (suku), religion (agama), race (ras), and inter-group relations (antargolongan). These were prohibited as matters for discussion under the New Order (Taher, 1997: 30–31). Partly in response to these developments, the Soeharto government began to consider and introduce various policies on religion, including on religious education, criminal offences against religion, permits for churches and guidelines on the boundaries of proselytization.
Segregated religious instruction
One area in which government policies on religion divided religious communities is education. In 1946, the Ministry of Education set up an Education Commission to design a new national education system for Indonesia. One of its responsibilities was to address the issue of religious instruction in schools (Kelabora, 1976: 238). The outcome of the Commission’s investigation was a new law, which included the following policy on religious instruction in public schools: ‘Religious instruction shall be provided in government schools and parents shall decide whether their children are to attend such instruction’ (art. 20(i)).12
This meant that religious education was to be provided in all public schools, though it did not apply to private schools, and participation was optional, depending on the wishes of the parents. This enshrined a sectarian approach to religious education in Indonesia – that is, children were separated according to religion and given instruction according to their religion. A Joint Regulation of the Minister of Education and the Minister of Religion13 further clarified that religious education only need be provided by a school if there were at least ten students of that religion (Taher, 1997: 60).
In 1950, due to concerns that Muslim children were not receiving Islamic education, the Minister of Religion and the Minister of Education and Culture issued a letter to public schools instructing them to provide Islamic education (Adams, 2004: 141). By 1960, concerns that Muslim children in Christian schools were being ‘forced’ to learn about Christianity were addressed by the People’s Consultative Counsel (Majelis Permusyarawatan Rakyat), which decided that parents can choose for a child to opt out of religious education classes.14
In 1966, the government issued a decision that made religious education compulsory for all students.15 This decision specifically deleted the previous provision which provided that parents can choose for their child not to participate in religious education, and replaced it with the sentence ‘religious education is a compulsory subject in all schools, from primary school through to university.’ This decision effectively nullified the right of parents to withdraw their child from a religious education class and forced parents to identify their child as ‘belonging’ to one of the six religions.
Throughout the New Order, Islamists sought to restrict the perceived influence Christian schools had exerted over Muslim students since colonialism. In 1967, an inter-religious conference (explained further below) debated the issue of religious education. According to Hasyim, the Muslim delegates expressed concern that ‘if Muslim children attend a Christian school, they will be tricked into becoming Christian because they are easily deceived,’ although the Christian delegates disagreed (Hasyim, 1979: 279). Differences of opinion between Muslims and Christians on this issue continued.
In March 1975, in a speech at the Commemoration of the Birthday of the Prophet Muhammad in Jakarta, President Soeharto declared that: ‘In the implementation of religious education, it is best to provide religious education for each religion’ (Krissantono, 1976: 37). He emphasized that religious education was necessary not only in public schools at all levels, but also in private education institutions (Krissantono, 1976: 38). The government continued to warn schools that students should not be targets of proselytization. In June 1978, Minister of Religion Alamsyah delivered a speech to a group of ulama (Islamic religious leaders) in Bandung, urging religious schools not to proselytize their students (Hasyim, 1979: 373).
By the 1980s, almost 20 years after the attempted communist coup and the introduction of the new religious education policy, concerns were still being expressed by some Muslim leaders anxious about the risk of conversion. In particular, the Minister of Religion Munawir Syadzali (1983–93) publically expressed his concern that Muslim parents in Indonesia still prefer to send their children to public schools or private Christian schools, rather than Islamic schools (Antara News Bulletin, 29 October 1985), because of the reputation of Christian schools for higher-quality education. In 1985, the minister for education provided further clarification on religious education, reinforcing that every student must attend religious education according to their religion (art. 2(2)), and that schools must provide religious education for at least two hours per week if there were more than ten students of that religion, although this did not extend to private schools.16
It was not until 1989 that the entire education system was revised through Law 2/1989 on the National Education System.17 This law required schools to provide religious education to equip students to understand their religion based on the assumption that every child has a fixed religious identity. The significant change here was that religious education was given equal status in the curriculum alongside the Pancasila and citizenship (art. 39(2)). The right to education was qualified in the Elucidation to a regulation, which stated that schools based on a particular religion were not obliged to provide religious education for religions other than the religion of that school.18 This was effectively an exemption for private Christian schools.
This qualification was significant for Christian schools because it relieved the pressure to provide Islamic religious education for Muslim students. The pressure felt by Christian schools was expressed by the Indonesian Christian Education Council, a division of the Indonesian Communion of Churches (Persekutuan Gereja-gereja Indonesia, PGI) which develops the curriculum for Protestant religious education. It went to great lengths to emphasize that the regulations passed in 1990 did not require religious-based schools to provide religious education different from the religion on which the school was based, thus justifying the absence of Islamic religious education in Christian schools (Sairin, 2006a: 87).
The Indonesian Ulama Council, however, maintained the position that Muslim children should not attend a school that does not provide Islamic religious education. This view was expressed by participants at the National Working Committee in 1984, which noted that ‘children who follow one religion but attend a school based on another religion still need to have the opportunity to learn about their own religion’ (MUI, 1995: 126). This is a reference to the large number of Muslim students who attend Christian schools. This opinion was again expressed at the National Working Committee in 1990 (MUI, 1995: 73), and has persisted since 1998, as I will demonstrate in Chapter 5.
Government policies on religious instruction since independence have gradually shifted from an emphasis on the rights of the parents to choose whether their child participates in religious education, to compulsory religious education post-1965 due to Muslim concerns over communism and Christianization. In the 1980s, this sectarian model where students only learn about the teachings of their religion was further reinforced. Another area that became the subject of government policy and sanction after independence is the criminal offence of insulting a religion.
The crime of insulting a religion
In January 1965, the offence of ‘insulting a religion’ was established through a decree of President Soekarno.19 This law was introduced just prior to the alleged attempted communist coup in mid-1965. Four years later, the decree was strengthened and made into a statute (undang-undang).20 This formed part of a broader government agenda after 1965 strongly to encourage all citizens to choose one of the six recognized religions. This promoted the idea that citizens should have a fixed and bounded religious identity that could be monitored and controlled by the state. This law ensured religious leaders could protect the status and interpretation of the six religions from criticism through the legal system.
The Blasphemy Law is very brief and yet wide in scope, with just four key provisions. The first three provisions aim to protect the six recognized religions and prevent any ‘deviations’ from the established teachings of these religions. Article 1 contains a prohibition on publicly interpreting a religion or conducting religious activities that deviate from the tenets of one of the six religions. This allows the six religions to set boundaries on what is ‘official’ doctrine and to have the final, authoritative say on interpretations of religion.
The Minister of Religion, the Attorney-General and the Minister of Home Affairs can issue a joint decree to warn a person who has violated article 1 by promoting deviant teachings (art. 2). If a violation is committed by an organization or aliran kepercayaan (mystical belief), the president has the power to ban the group on the recommendation of these three authorities. What constitutes ‘deviancy’ is not defined in the law, though I will show that in practice it is the national religious councils that claim the authority to determine what is ‘deviant’ (see Chapter 6).
Article 3 provides that if a person or organization continues to act in breach of article 1, then they can be imprisoned for a maximum of five years. According to the Elucidation to the decree, if the followers or leaders of a group stop their activities, they cannot be charged. The criminal offence of blasphemy contained in article 4 provides for the insertion of article 156a into the Criminal Code, as follows:
A person who deliberately and publicly expresses feelings or acts in a manner that:
a)
involves hatred, misuse, or insulting of a religion followed in Indonesia; and
b)
has the intention that a person should not practice any religion at all that is based on Belief in Almighty God, shall be sentenced to a maximum of five years jail.
The Indonesian phrase used in the Blasphemy Law is penyalahgunaan dan/atau penodaan agama, which means to misuse, besmirch or insult a religion. In Indonesia, several terms are used by both Muslims and Christians to describe offences, often interchangeably, including penodaan agama (disgracing a religion); penistaan agama (insulting or defaming a religion); and penyalahgunaan agama (misusing or abusing a religion). A person who is accused of blasphemy may be called murtad (apostate), kafir (infidel), aliran sesat (deviant group), sesat (deviant), or aliran kepercayaan (mystical belief). The use of these terms reinforces the distinction between mystical beliefs and religions (explained in Chapter 3), and shows a concern for protecting the orthodox teachings of a religion.
The Elucidation to the Blasphemy Law clarifies that the accused must have intentionally insulted or shown hostility towards a religion (art. 4). Written or verbal statements that are objective and avoid the use of offensive words or phrases are not considered to be a criminal offence under this article. The Elucidation justifies the severity of the punishment by stating that offenders have disturbed a religious community and are considered to have betrayed the state ideology, Pancasila, and its first element, Belief in One God.
This provision does not appear to be based on any modern principle of Islamic law but this may be because in Islam there is no general agreement on a definition of ‘blasphemy.’21 In Islam there are several relevant terms including riddah (apostasy), sabb Allah and sabb al-Rasul (blasphemy against God or against the Prophet), zandaqah (heresy), nifaq (hypocrisy), and kufr (unbelief), although no distinction is made between these terms in practice (Saeed and Saeed, 2004: 36–42). The traditional Islamic penalty for apostasy is death, although some scholars have argued that there is no Qur’anic authority for this penalty (Saeed and Saeed, 2004: 56, 69–87). Only radical Islamists in Indonesia would agree with the implementation of the traditional penalty.22 Cases of blasphemy are explored in greater detail in Chapter 6 and show the increase in the number of prosecutions and convictions for this crime. The third area that the New Order government came under pressure to regulate was the construction of churches.
Church burnings and permits
Several churches were destroyed in the 1960s based on allegations that they were built in Muslim areas or that Christians had insulted Islam. In April 1967, a Methodist church in Melauboh, Aceh, was destroyed (Hasyim, 1979: 291–93). This was one of the first major church incidents to become a matter of national debate. Known as the ‘Melauboh Affair’ (Peristiwa Melauboh), it was reported that objections were made to the church because it was built in a Muslim-majority area. The church was subsequently rebuilt in a different area.
Following this incident Simorangkir, a member of the Christian-based political party Parkindo (see Chapter 3), initiated a meeting with President Soeharto in June 1967 and proposed an interpelasi (official question) to the legislature (Dewan Perwakilan Rakyat) regarding the Melauboh Affair and the right to religious freedom (Hasyim, 1979: 293–98). On 10 June, this was countered by an interpelasi from Lukman Harun of Muhammadiyah, supported by members of parliament affiliated with Islamic-based political parties, questioning the practice of religious organizations receiving foreign funding (Aritonang, 2006: 128; Hasyim, 1979: 298–308). This implied that the church in Melauboh was built through reliance on foreign funds.
In October 1967, further attacks took place on 14 churches, three schools and the office of a Catholic student organization in Makassar, South Sulawesi. Labeled the ‘Makassar Affair’ (Peristiwa Makassar), this incident was the action of Muslim youths who reacted to rumors that a Christian teacher, H.K. Mangunbahan, had insulted Islam by stating that Muhammad was illiterate, and only married nine of his wives and lived in adultery with the others (Hasyim, 1979: 314–20). This was an early example of retaliation and vandalism of church buildings as a result of perceived insults to Islam (see Chapter 6).
Similar to the Melauboh Affair, in 1969 a church in Slipi, Jakarta, was destroyed in an area that was already home to five churches that catered for the 350 Christians living in the area (Hasyim, 1979: 321–22). In addition to claims that yet another church was unnecessary in a Muslim-majority area, complaints were made that the church did not have a permit from local authorities. These incidents led to greater demands for the implementation of a permit process before a church could be constructed.
As a result of the national controversy generated by media coverage of these incidents and by the questions raised in the legislature, a decree was drafted and implemented without consultation with the representatives of the five official religions: Islam, Catholicism, Protestantism, Hinduism and Buddhism. On 13 September 1969, the Minister of Religion Kiai Haji23 Mohammad Dahlan, and the Minister of Home Affairs Amir Machmud, officially introduced Joint Decree 1/1969 to control religious activities and the building of places of worship. The aim of this decree was to ensure that a religious group had obtained a permit from local authorities, including the local government, the Ministry of Religion and local religious leaders, before it proceeded to build a place of worship.
From the beginning many Christians opposed the joint decree. In October 1969, the Indonesian Council of Churches (Dewan Gereja-gereja Indonesia, DGI) and the High Council of Indonesian Bishops (Majelis Agung Wali Gereja Indonesia, MAWI) issued a joint memorandum expressing their opposition to the Joint Decree on the basis that it was discriminatory and violated the right to religious freedom in article 29 of the Constitution (PGI, 2006a: 26–29). These complaints from religious minorities were countered by vocal support from Muslims who kept up a campaign of support for the decree. Violent incidents at churches were used by Muslim leaders as evidence that churches built in ‘Muslim areas’ lead to inter-religious violence. The introduction of the joint decree in 1969 on places of worship and the ongoing campaign by Muslims to limit Christian missionary activity allowed Islamists to garner support for the introduction of regulations on proselytization, as follows.
Restrictions on missionary activities
In 1967, partly in response to Muslim concerns over conversions to Christianity, the Ministry of Religion initiated a gathering of religious leaders in Jakarta with the intention of establishing an inter-religious council and a mutual agreement on the boundaries of religious missionary activities.24 This conference was attended by representatives from the major social and political religious organizations at this time (Sairin, 2000: 97). The reason for the conference, as identified by the Minister of Religion in his opening speech, was the perceived need for a response to what was described as the ‘systematic attempts’ by some religions to convert Muslims to their religion, an indirect reference to Christian proselytization (Sairin, 2000: 205). The government had prepared a draft statement that emphasized the need for inter-religious dialogue. It contained a provision that all religions would agree not to make efforts to convert a person who already had a religion.
The draft statement was never signed, however, because of sharp disagreement between Muslim and Christian delegates. The Muslim representatives refused to take part in inter-religious dialogue with Christians, but supported a ban on missionary activities aimed at people who already had a religion (Mudzhar, 1996: 231). The Christian representatives agreed to participate in inter-religious dialogue but strongly opposed the proposed limitations on proselytization because, in their opinion, it would compromise the freedom of Christians to practice their faith and propagate their religion.
As a result, the conference was considered to be a failure and the blame was directed at Christians. One newspaper reported the outcome as a ‘deadlock’ and the headline identified Christians as to blame: ‘Catholics/Protestants reject draft charter’ (Sairin, 2000: 135). According to Father Frans Magnis Suseno, a Catholic priest whose friend was the English advisor to the president at the time, Soeharto was very angry with the national Protestant and Catholic councils because it was the first instance in which these councils had directly and publicly opposed the position of his government.25
In a later statement issued by five Islamic leaders, including leaders from Muhammadiyah and Nahdlatul Ulama, blame was pointed at Christians for ‘rejecting the government’s idea to stop religious proselytization towards people who already have a religion’ (Sairin, 2000: 206). This was not explicitly based on any religious or Islamic doctrine, but was the pragmatic stance of government officials and Islamic religious leaders at the time. Professor Maskyuri Abdillah, chairperson of the Inter-religious Harmony Division of the Indonesian Ulama Council (2010–15), suggests, however, that the idea might be related to the Islamic concept ‘To you be your religion, and to me my religion’ (Qur’an 109:6).
In the 1970s, concerns again surfaced in public debates that Christians were failing to respect the ‘boundaries’ of the six recognized religious communities, particularly Islam. For example, on 16 August 1974 at a ceremony for the commemoration of the Prophet Muhammad’s journey from Mecca to Medina, Soeharto issued a warning to all religious organizations: ‘So that the feelings of other groups are not disturbed, you must be careful not to direct religious proselytization at people who already have a religion’ (Natsir, 1980: 31).
In September 1975, the chairperson of the Indonesian Ulama Council met with President Soeharto to discuss the issue of ‘Christianization,’ citing examples such as the expansion of Christian hospitals and events designed to convert Muslims to Christianity in return for food and other material provisions (Mudzhar, 1996: 231–32). Then in 1976, a decision of MUI highlighted the speeches of several government ministers over the previous few years which all emphasized the principle that a person should not try to convert someone who already has a religion by offering economic incentives (Sairin, 2000: 207). In particular, this decision highlighted the imperative of not exploiting the weaknesses of the uneducated, the sick or children, and of not approaching a person in his or her home. The decision affirmed an earlier statement by President Soeharto that ‘proselytization should not be conducted towards people who already have a religion’ (Hasyim, 1979: 424). There were also other complaints made to the police at this time, such as those regarding mass distribution of tracts by evangelical Christian organizations in areas such as Bandung (Dixon, 1999). These complaints sought to reinforce the original position of Islamists and the government at the 1967 conference that proselytization should be strictly limited. The agenda to introduce legal regulations that would restrict Christian proselytization therefore continued despite the initial failure of the 1967 conference.
Regulations on proselytization
In May 1978, Minister of Religion Alamsyah issued a decision to the effect that Islamic dakwah activities do not require prior permission from the government.26 This essentially gave free license to Muslims to spread the teachings of Islam. Then, in August 1978, the Ministry issued Regulation No. 70/1978 to set out guidelines for religious proselytization. This regulation echoes the long-held fears and suspicions Muslims have of Christian proselytization. It prohibits religious proselytization that (art. 2):
a)
is directed at a person who already has a religion;
b)
employs the use of bribery (such as gifts, money, clothes, food and drink, medicine, etc.) in order to persuade a person to change his or her religion;
c)
involves the distribution of pamphlets, bulletins, magazines, books or other publications to people who already have a religion;
d)
involves approaching the private residences of people who already have a religion.
This means that religious missionary activities can only be conducted among people who follow animistic or mystical beliefs, because these beliefs are not officially considered to be ‘religions’ (see Chapter 3). This provision is therefore in support of the position of Muslim leaders as expressed at the 1967 conference. If the propagation ‘destabilizes religious groups’ or social harmony, then the government can take legal action to ensure compliance with these rules (art. 3). These four key limits on proselytization represent particular concerns of Muslims regarding Christian missionary activities.
Article 2(a) relates to the idea of a fixed religious identity and the need to prevent apostasy and conversions between religions, mainly from Islam to Christianity. Apostasy (riddah, lit ‘to turn back’) is widely recognized within Islam as the desertion of Islam or the conversion from Islam to another religion (Saeed and Saeed, 2004: 1). Apostasy is a very sensitive issue in Indonesia, but it is not a crime recognized by the state.
