Apostasy from Islam and conversion to Christianity is, however, still a real cause of concern for Islamists in Indonesia (see Chapter 6).
Some Muslim organizations have been founded in part as a reaction to Christian proselytization and efforts to prevent apostasy. One such group is the Indonesian Islamic Preaching Council (Dewan Dakwah Islamiyah Indonesia, DDII), which was founded on 26 February 1967 by Mohammed Natsir, a leader of the Islamic political party Masyumi.27 According to Hefner, DDII ‘chose to work in regions where it competed directly with Christian missionaries’ (Hefner, 2000: 109). Since 1998, several ‘anti-apostasy’ groups have formed. One example is the Alliance of Anti-Apostasy Movements (Aliansi Gerakan Anti-Pemurtadan, AGAP), which was founded in April 2005 by Muhammad Mu’min. AGAP claims to represent 27 radical Islamic organizations, such as the Islamic Defenders Front (Front Pembela Islam, FPI), Barisan Pemuda Persis, Jamaah Tabligh and Hizbut Tahrir, and claims to up to 50,000 supporters. The group organises campaigns on Christian activities and churches that aim to convert Muslims. As one example of its vigilante actions, Mu’min claimed it was responsible for the closure of 23 churches in West Java in 2005 (Tempo Interaktif, 11 September 2005).
A group with similar goals is the Anti-Conversion Movement Forum (Forum Anti-Gerakan Pemurtadan, FAKTA). Members of FAKTA have attempted attacks on Christian pastors who are on its blacklist of converts from Islam to Christianity (ICG, 2009: 7, 8). For example, in 2007, a Christian teacher who allegedly tried to convert his Muslim students was murdered by members of FAKTA in Palembang. In April 2009, ten men were brought to trial and convicted in relation to this incident and for planning other similar attacks (ICG, 2009: 3).
MUI has also expressed concerns that Christians are converting Muslims at ‘an alarming rate,’ lamenting the migration of Christians to predominantly Muslim areas, and complaining about the construction of churches in Islamic-majority provinces (Jakarta Post, 28 July 2005). Some concerned parties have held conferences to address these issues. For example, in 2001, Muhammadiyah held a conference in Jakarta on ‘Facing Apostasy and the Christianization Movement,’ which featured speakers from groups such as the Indonesian Committee for Global Muslim Solidarity (Komite Indonesia untuk Solidaritas Dunia Islam), FPI and Laskar Jihad (Republika, 4 September 2001). In 2004, the Indonesian Ulama Forum organized a seminar on ‘Education and Training on Anticipating Apostasy’ (Republika, 2 March 2004). In June 2010, the Islamic Center of Bekasi issued several recommendations at its Congress, which included a recommendation to educate the Muslim community about the dangers of Christianization. These activities continue to incite wider fears of apostasy as a result of ‘Christianization.’
This is related to the second element of the Regulation, article 2(b), which aims to prevent conversions through bribery or dishonesty. According to KH Said Agil Siradj, chairperson of NU (2009–14), there are many rumors that Christians resort to bribery to ‘win’ converts, offering people money or food if they will become Christian.28 Newspapers such as Republika circulate reports of people converting to Christianity, often through coercion or by deceptive means. For example, in 1999 there were reports of 500 people converting from Islam to Christianity in West Sumatra (Republika, 16 August 1999). In December 1999, the Indonesian Ulama Council reportedly discovered that 100 Muslim school children had been given fried pig meat to eat at school, which is haram (forbidden) for Muslims (Republika, 24 December 1999). Magazines like Sabili and groups such as DDII also make these claims, but the commotion stirred up by these groups often serves further to inflame inter-religious relations. Although media reports appear every year claiming that some students have committed ‘apostasy’ by converting to Christianity, what may be more likely is that some Muslim students enrolled to attend a Christian school.
Another form of proselytization considered deceptive is the provision of material aid, particularly after natural disasters common to Indonesia such as a tsunami or earthquake. Suspicions may be raised by religious groups that provide charitable aid and material goods such as sembako to Muslims.29 In particular, at times there have been concerted campaigns to prevent Christian groups from providing aid or medical assistance to Muslim communities. An early example of Christian groups providing material aid with the underlying aim of Christianizing a Muslim community took place in 1968 after a flood in Surabaya, East Java (Hasyim, 1979: 280). In another example, in May 2006, after the earthquake in Bantul, Yogyakarta, a church went to deliver aid to victims, but was prevented from doing so by members of the Islamic-based Prosperous Justice Party (Partai Keadilan Sejahtera, PKS) because it was not a ‘Christian area.’ In 2008, efforts were also made to stop a Christian event in Bekasi because a church intended to hand out free food.30
In addition, article 2(b) might also protect against forced conversions. The use of force to convert someone to Islam is forbidden, according to the Qur’an (2:256): ‘Let there be no compulsion in the religion …’ There have, however, been some reports of forced conversions in Indonesia, for example, at the height of the conflict between Muslims and Christians in Maluku (1999–2001). Some accounts referred to specific incidents, such as the forced conversion (specifically, forced circumcisions) of 450 Catholics from Keshui, Maluku (Christian Solidarity Worldwide, 19 January 2001). More general reports have also emerged of claims of 4,000 coerced conversions of Christians to Islam during this conflict (Sydney Morning Herald, 27 January 2001). Such claims are not new, with reports of forced conversions to Islam as early as the 1600s, when some Portuguese were captured by Muslims in Aceh and Makassar and were allowed to live if they agreed to convert to Islam (Reid, 1993: 173–74). Nevertheless, these reports are generally rare in Indonesia and can perhaps be largely confined to the conflict in Maluku.
The third element in article 2(c) is the fear of spreading false teachings through the distribution of Christian publications. From the late 1960s, the New Order government tried to control religious publications by providing funding to the six religions to publish holy books, although the quantity of books published was relatively small. From 1969 to 1970, it sponsored the publication of 278,000 translations of the Qur’an; 8,000 copies of the Protestant Bible; 3,720 copies of the Catholic Bible; and 12,500 Hindu and Buddhist texts (Presidential Speech, 1970: 250). This support increased every year and in 1990–91, the number of publications had risen to over 440,000 copies of the Qur’an; 50,000 copies of the Protestant Bible; and 49,000 copies of the Catholic Bible (Presidential Speech, 1991: chapter 5, p.9). These religious publications were intended to be distributed to communities or people who did not yet have a (recognized) religion.
There are reports of Bibles (often in the local dialect) being distributed to Muslims in public places such as schools. For example, in 1999 there were reports of Bibles in the Minangkabau language being distributed in Padang, West Sumatra.31 This is particularly controversial because it is widely believed that all Minangkabau people are Muslim. In 2002, a Christian group reportedly gave away 100 Bibles in Bogor, West Java (Republika, 27 December 2002). In 2004, MUI expressed its disappointment at the lack of response from the Ministry of Religion concerning the publication and distribution of the ‘Holy Book of the Qur’an’ that allegedly contained elements of the Christian message, in West Sumatra, a majority-Muslim area (Republika, 15 July 2004). In 2006, Gideon’s International32 distributed Bibles to Muslim students in several pesantren (Islamic boarding school) and schools in Ponorogo, East Java, although they claimed to have the permission of the Ministry of Religion (Republika, 5 January 2006). Such claims are ongoing and further incite suspicions raised by the distribution of religious literature.
Finally, article 2(d) relates specifically to religious organizations that distribute religious pamphlets to people in their homes as a way of propagating their religion, such as Jehovah’s Witnesses. For example, Muhammad Natsir, the leader of Masyumi and founder of DDII, gives an account of three advent missionaries who were detained after entering the home of a Muslim with a brochure and trying to persuade him to convert to Christianity. The Muslim, who was 65 years old, allegedly died from ‘shock’ (Natsir, 1980: 77–78). In 1976, Jehovah’s Witnesses were banned by the Attorney-General because of the concern over the practice of ‘door knocking,’ although this ban was lifted in 2001 (Sairin, 1996a: 271–72). There is no evidence to suggest that Regulation 70/1978 has ever been enforced by the government or in court.
In addition to Regulation 70/1978 as discussed above, the Minister of Religion issued Decision 77/1978 on the guidelines for overseas donations to religious institutions in Indonesia. This was partly in response to allegations that foreign funding was sponsoring proselytization efforts. For example, on 21 July 1967 in a speech to the legislature, Dr Lukman Harun submitted copies of magazines from the United States that contained articles on the funds generated by the World Council of Churches as evidence of a Western agenda to fund Christian missionary activities in Indonesia (Hasyim, 1979: 300–8). Other publications by Muslims on Christianity in Indonesia highlight the large number of North American church organizations and overseas staff operating in Indonesia (Ka’bah, 1985: 82–84).
The intention of this regulation is to enable the government to maintain control over what kind of donations religious groups receive, the amount of the donation, where the donations come from, and the intended purpose of the donations to avoid any ‘negative’ effects of foreign aid. This decision prohibits overseas donations to religious organizations in Indonesia unless with the approval of the minister (art. 2). Restrictions are placed on foreign missionary workers, who can only engage in religious propagation if they have the consent of the minister, and can do so only for the limited period of two years, after which time they must be replaced by an indigenous worker (art. 3). Religious institutions that do not comply with this regulation may be the subject of legal action (art. 4).
The two 1978 regulations were the cause of great concern for Christians. On 24 August and again on 14 September 1978, the national Protestant and Catholic Councils (DGI and MAWI) sent a letter to Soeharto requesting the withdrawal of Regulations 70/1978 and 77/1978 in the interests of upholding the right to freedom of religion.33 As Mudzhar (1996: 232) points out, Christians protested because the regulations contradicted their position at the 1967 conference and could potentially cause Christian missionary activities to be severely restricted, because many churches receive international financial support. The regulations were considered to be unconstitutional and a betrayal of the Pancasila and religious freedom. They were branded by some foreign, Western media as ‘anti-mission laws’ and led to headlines such as ‘Religious freedom crisis hits Indonesian churches’ (Denffer, 1987: 36–41). As Steenbrink (2000: 93) argues, ‘the 1978 Decrees appeared to be a pro-Muslim move of [the] new Cabinet,’ which was of great concern to Christians.
The Ministry responded to this controversy with Decision 1/1979 on Guidelines for the Propagation of Religion and Overseas Donations to Religious Organizations. The 1979 decision restated that religious propagation could still occur but must be based on ‘mutual respect’ for other religions (art. 3) – that is, only aiming religious propagation at people who did not have a religion. It then reiterated almost word for word (but did not cancel) the key provisions of the 1978 regulations. The head of MUI at the time, Hamka, agreed with these laws (Mujiburrahman, 2008: 82), and MUI has continued to express its support for these laws in its publications over the years (MUI, 1995: 26).
Christian leaders again strongly objected to the 1979 regulation (Sairin, 1996a: 488–99). Nevertheless, in the 1980s, several further regulations dealing with foreign religious missionaries and controlling the content of religious sermons and proselytization were passed (Sudjangi, 1992: 121–24). Although never implemented, they remain valid today.
Conclusion
This brief historical analysis of Islamist campaigns to limit Christian proselytization through government policies prior to 1998 in Indonesia has shown that attitudes of resistance to Christian activities such as building churches, hospitals and schools are rooted in Indonesia’s colonial past, when Christianity was widely perceived to be favored by Dutch colonial authorities.
After independence in 1945, the 1960s crackdown on communism and the mass conversions to Christianity, Islamists petitioned the government to provide religious education, protect religions from denigration through a Blasphemy Law, regulate church permits and to limit religious proselytization. At the 1967 conference, this sparked national debates between Muslims and Christians about whether the ‘targets’ and methods of proselytization can be limited. In the late 1970s, pressure from the Indonesian Ulama Council led the Ministry of Religion to set boundaries on religious proselytization, which are still valid law.
This account of the origins of Islamist opposition to Christian proselytization and the policies that were subsequently introduced provides a background for the discussion that follows of the ideology, actors and institutions that influence contemporary legal disputes between Muslims and Christians. In the next chapter, I examine the role of religious ideology in the legal system and the influence religious communities have on policy through government-sponsored religious institutions. I link these developments to ongoing attempts to implement Islamic law and the way this has influenced the resolution of legal disputes between Muslims and Christians in West Java.

3  Religious ideology, representation and reform
When a state experiences regime change and embarks on a transition to democracy, this process is inevitably influenced by key actors within the community, such as religious leaders. Since 1998 and the fall of former President Soeharto and his authoritarian New Order regime (1966–98), religious communities have attempted to assert newly found democratic rights through legislative reforms concerning matters of religion, at both the local and national level. In particular, the state has responded to pressures from Islamists for change on matters of religion by revising and introducing numerous laws in an attempt to regulate religious activities in a way that favors the Islamist goal of implementing Islamic law at the national and regional level.
This chapter examines the representation of ideology in state institutions and law reform in relation to disputes between Muslims and Christians. It analyses how Muslims and Christians use government institutions and legal processes to reform laws that regulate religious affairs. Through these mechanisms, it demonstrates that Islamists have had a significant influence on law reform, with the result that majority rights are at times exercised at the expense of minorities.
One of the major issues of dispute, both within the Muslim community, and between Muslims and Christians, is the ideological foundation of the state, and whether Indonesia should become a state based on Islamic law. After the initial failure of this debate in 1945, there was an attempt to resurrect this proposal in 2000 through the efforts of Islamists to reinsert into the Constitution the Jakarta Charter, the obligation for Muslims to follow Islamic law. This chapter analyses the failure of this campaign and the implications for the status of the Pancasila state ideology and the right to religious freedom, particularly for the rights of religious minorities.
The key government department that regulates and oversees religious affairs is the Ministry of Religion. This chapter examines how Islamists are working through the Ministry of Religion, and the Indonesian Ulama Council (MUI) in particular, to campaign for national law reforms based on Islamic law. It also examines the corresponding national Catholic and Protestant councils and questions whether these organizations still represent the Christian community in post-1998 debates on public policy in Indonesia.
Both the status of the Jakarta Charter and the role of the Ministry of Religion have been the subject of vociferous national debate. Since 1998, the processes of decentralization and democracy have shifted power to the local level and opened up greater freedoms and authority for local governments to pass laws on religion. This has relocated debates on law and religion from the national to the local context. This book focuses on the province of West Java, the location for the three case studies that follow in Part II, because it is a majority-Muslim area where many incidents of violence between Muslims and Christians have taken place. It identifies contributing factors, such as the history of radical Islam, state ambivalence towards the violent actions of radical Islamists, and a competitive political environment. This has led to the politicization of religion, the introduction of local regulations based on Islam, and the criminal prosecution of religious minorities for offences against religion in court. This chapter will contribute to an understanding of the legal, institutional and social context of the court cases that follow in Part II.
Constitutional compromise and the limits of religious freedom
There have been two main issues of concern for Muslims and Christians regarding the relationship between religion and the Constitution since 1998: the constitutional changes to the right to freedom of religion, and the failed attempt to make Indonesia an Islamic state by constitutional amendment. There have been several major reforms since 1998, including the release of political prisoners, the separation of the police from the military, the separation of the judiciary from the executive, the ratification of several international conventions, and the holding of free and fair elections. There have also been significant amendments to the Constitution.1 An ideological compromise has taken place, with priority given to the Pancasila over an Islamic state, and the consolidation of the right to religious freedom, with some important qualifications.
During the drafting process of the original 1945 Constitution, there was considerable debate about the inclusion of the right to religious freedom and whether it should include the freedom to change religion. Many of the Christian participants in the debate, such as Renda Sarungallo Sihombing, of the Christian political party Parkindo, argued that the Constitution should include the freedom to change religion and that reluctance to do so indicated that the state was concerned that it would be accused by Islamists of promoting conversions between religions (Nasution, 1992: 202). Several members of Christian political parties, such as I.J. Kasimo, chairperson of the Catholic political party Partai Katolik, and Rumambi (Parkindo) supported the proposal to include the right to change religion (Nasution, 1992: 203). In 1953, this position was reaffirmed by a resolution adopted by the national Protestant council, which upheld the freedom to change religion and to propagate a religion (Nyhus, 1987: 517).
On the other side of the debate, Muslim leaders focused on the need for legitimate limitations on the right to freedom of religion for the sake of maintaining ‘religious harmony.’ Some participants in the drafting process of the 1945 Constitution, such as Oemar Salim Hubeis of the Islamic political party Masjumi, vehemently argued against the inclusion of such a provision. If such a provision had to be included, however, he argued that it should not apply to Muslims, presumably because of the teachings of Islam against apostasy.2
In the end, the original Indonesian Constitution 1945 included the right to freedom of religion under article 29, although it did not specify whether this includes a right to change religion. Article 29 reads as follows.3
1  The state shall be based upon the Belief in the One and Only God.4
2  The state guarantees all persons the freedom of religion and worship, each according to his or her religion and belief.
Since 1998 and the transition to democracy, the right to freedom of religion has been reaffirmed by the state. Article 22 of Law 39/1999 on Human Rights states that:
1  Each person is free to profess his/her religion and to worship according to his/her religion and belief.
2  The state guarantees all persons the freedom of religion, each according to his/her religion and belief.
This right to religious freedom is tempered by art. 70 of the Law 39/1999 on Human Rights, which allows for permissible limitations on the right to religious freedom:5
In the enjoyment of their rights and freedoms, each person is obliged to submit to the limits determined by law, with the sole purpose of guaranteeing recognition and respect for the rights of others and to fulfil the requirements of justice and taking into consideration morality, security, and public order in a democratic community.
In 2001, this right was further reinforced in the Second Amendment to the Constitution,6 and art. 28E was inserted as part of a new chapter on human rights, as follows:
1  Each person is free to profess his or her religion and to worship in accordance with his or her religion …
2  Each person has the freedom to possess convictions and beliefs, and to express his or her thoughts and attitudes;
3  Each person has the freedom to associate, gather and express his or her opinions.
This right is important, although this book does not analyze Muslim-Christian relations from the perspective of international law and the right to freedom of religion. Rather, it focuses on local understandings of religious freedom that demonstrate ‘freedom of religion’ has become a common discourse and catchcry employed by religious minorities and supporters of religious pluralism particularly since 1998.
In addition to the omission of the right to convert, the Constitution also includes the phrase derived from Arabic, keimanan dan ketakwaan, ‘belief and piety,’ in the revision to article 31 on education to the 2002 constitutional amendment. This is perceived by some Christians as an Islamic phrase that berbau Islam, literally meaning ‘smelling of Islam,’ or inspired by Islam.
Article 28I also includes the right to have a religion, and it confers on the government the responsibility of protecting and guaranteeing these basic human rights.7 In October 2005, the Indonesian government’s commitment to freedom of religion at international law was reinforced by the ratification of the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights and the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR).8 This is considered to be a significant step forward by human rights activists and nongovernmental organizations (NGOs), particularly because these two covenants became part of Indonesian law.9 The Indonesian government is therefore bound by the Constitution, international law and domestic laws on human rights to respect the right to freedom of religion of its citizens. The right to freedom of religion, as enshrined in the Constitution, is also significant because it is increasingly used as the basis to submit requests to the Constitutional Court for judicial review of national laws on religion that are considered to be unconstitutional, as I demonstrate later in the case studies.
It is equally important to note the constitutional limitation that has been placed on the right to freedom of religion in Indonesia. This limitation is referred to by the government to justify its policies that regulate religious affairs and set boundaries around activities and the rights of religious minorities in particular. This restriction is contained in article 28J(2) of the Constitution:
In the enjoyment of their rights and freedoms, each person is obliged to submit to the limits determined by law, with the sole purpose of guaranteeing recognition and respect for the rights of others and to fulfill the requirements of justice and taking into consideration morality, religious values, security, and public order in a democratic community.
This list of permissible limitations is similar to art. 18 of the ICCPR at international law, with the main exception being that the Indonesian Constitution lists ‘religious values’ whereas the ICCPR does not. Further, although the right to religious freedom in theory also applies to religious minorities in the Indonesian context it is often used to justify the protection of the rights of the religious majority. For example, in 2010, this provision formed part of the reasoning of the Indonesian Constitutional Court in its decision to uphold the constitutional validity of the Blasphemy Law.10
In this case, the court emphasized that this provision is different from article 18 of the ICCPR and is an explicit and legitimate limitation on the right to religious freedom in the Indonesian context (p.276). It stated that the ‘Pancasila is the basis of the state’ and stressed that the state is neither secular nor Islamic (p.271). It went on to explain how the state formed the Ministry of Religion shortly after independence in order to ensure religious harmony and the right to religious freedom, as well as to regulate necessary restrictions on this right (p.275). The decision of the Constitutional Court therefore reinforced the legitimacy of state limitations on the right to religious freedom. The limitation in article 28J(2) has also been relied on by government bodies such as the Ministry of Religion to justify its policies. At international law, however, the general comments to the ICCPR on freedom of religion assert that state restrictions on the freedom of religion must be narrow.11
Aside from the right to and limitations on freedom of religion, public discourse in Indonesia since 1998 has focused at length on whether Indonesia should become an Islamic state based on syariah law.12 To understand the campaign that took place in 2000, it is necessary to return to the circumstances surrounding the creation of the 1945 Constitution when the proposal was first made.
The drafting of the 1945 Constitution sparked the first of many debates about the relationship between law and religion in Indonesia. An initial draft of the Constitution, known as the Piagam Jakarta (Jakarta Charter),13 included the seven words ‘with the obligation to carry out syariah for adherents of Islam.’ If this draft had been passed, the inclusion of the seven words would effectively compel all Muslims to follow Islamic law, although it did not amount to the creation of an Islamic state (Negara Islam) (Hooker and Lindsey, 2002: 264–65). This phrase was later omitted from the final draft of the 1945 Constitution after concerns over the obligations syariah would impose on Muslims, and how this might affect non-Muslims in particular. This was a cause of concern for Christian leaders and Christian-majority provinces, which threatened to secede and therefore endanger the internal unity and stability of the newly independent country (Indrayana, 2008: 15). A large part of the opposition to the Jakarta Charter in 1945 also came from within the Muslim community (Dijk 1981: 52), which indicates the diversity of opinions within Islam on the relationship between Islam and the state. This is important and although this book focuses on the role of Islamists, it recognizes the inherent diversity and spectrum of views within the Muslim community in Indonesia.
Given the failure of the Jakarta Charter at independence, the Pancasila became the defining ideology of the Constitution and the state, and was perceived by many as a necessary compromise between the two most powerful religious communities in Indonesia: Islam and Christianity. It consists of five elements: belief in Almighty God, humanitarianism, nationalism, democracy and social justice. The first element of the Pancasila uses the word Tuhan, not Allah, referring to the Indonesian concept of God used by many religions, rather than the specific Arabic term Allah used by Muslims (Monnig-Atkinson, 1983: 688). The Pancasila was also a way of fending off unwanted accusations that Indonesia is a ‘secular’ state, as the Pancasila is symbolic of the willingness of the government to accommodate religion (Drijarkara, 1959: 40).
Throughout the New Order (1966–98), President Soeharto protected and ‘fostered’ the Pancasila (Hefner, 2000: 128), although more as a tool of social control than out of concern to protect religious pluralism. In the 1980s, the Pancasila was imposed by the government on all social and political organizations, displacing religion as the sole basis of such organizations.14 As the guiding reference point and ideology for the nation, the Pancasila was continually reinforced through government speeches. For example, in 1995, the former Minister of Religion Tarmizi Taher (1993–98) characterized Indonesians as people who adhere to the Pancasila and practice one of the five (now six) recognized religions; who refrain from directing missionary activities towards people who already have a religion; and who have the responsibility of promoting religious harmony (Taher, 1997: 56). Therefore, although religious freedom was included in the 1945 Constitution, this right was essentially subordinate or subservient to the Pancasila, the centralization of state power and the desire for social control over religious communities.
The fall of Soeharto’s authoritarian regime in 1998 led to a new era of openness and political debates on the nature and basis of the state. In 2000, the Jakarta Charter was again proposed for inclusion in the Constitution by a small Islamic political faction consisting of the United Development Party (Partai Persatuan Pembangunan, PPP) and the Crescent Star Party (Partai Bintang Bulan, PBB), which at the time occupied only 71 seats, or 12 per cent, of the legislature (Hosen, 2007: 4). This agenda was also supported with demonstrations from radical Islamist groups such as the Islamic Defenders Front (Front Pembela Islam, FPI) (Indrayana, 2008: 243, 383).
The majority of Indonesian Muslims, however, rejected this proposal, including many leaders and members of two largest Islamic groups in Indonesia, Nadhatul Ulama and Muhammadiyah, and the political parties to which they are linked, the National Awakening Party (Partai Kebangkitan Bangsa, PKB) and the National Mandate Party (Partai Amanat Nasional, PAN), respectively (Hosen, 2007: 198). These organizations argued that Islam does not need to be implemented formally, that is, in the Constitution. They also expressed fears that if the Jakarta Charter were introduced, it might re-ignite inter-religious tensions and threaten the rights of minorities (Tanthowi, 2008: 16–17).
There was also strong opposition to this proposal from religious minorities, especially Christians. In Christian-majority areas, such as Kupang (East Nusa Tenggara), demonstrations were held and threats were made to secede from Indonesia if the proposal were passed (Tempo, 5 November 2001). This debate again reflects the highly diverse nature of Indonesian Islam, because it not only concerned fears that Christian-majority areas may seek independence from Indonesia, but also reflected the lack of consensus among Muslims about what syariah law is or might look like in Indonesia (Lindsey, 2008: 36). It was also rejected by other minorities, such as the majority-Hindu population of Bali (Tempo, 14 August 2000). The proposal ultimately garnered little support and, in August 2002, the People’s Consultative Assembly (Majelis Permusyawaratan Rakyat, MPR) rejected the proposal for the inclusion of syariah in the Constitution.
The reaffirmation of the Pancasila ideology, to the detriment of an Islamic ideology, suggests there is greater support for religious diversity and pluralism or at least for maintaining the status quo of the six religions. Since 1998, many NGOs have sought to redefine and reinterpret the Pancasila as a more open, inclusive and tolerant ideology. There are many examples of this, but one of the most prominent occurred on 1 June 2009, when a demonstration was held at the National Monument (known as ‘Monas’) on the 63rd anniversary of the Pancasila. The peaceful rally organized by the National Alliance for Freedom of Religion and Faith15 was intended as a show of support for the Pancasila, religious freedom, and the rights of religious minorities such as Ahmadiyah, an Islamic sect that claims to be within Islam. The rally was attacked by around 400 members of radical Islamic groups, including the Islamic Defenders Front (Front Pembela Islam, FPI), Hizbut Tahrir Indonesia (HTI), and the Islamic Community Forum (Forum Ukhuwah Islamiyah, FUI), armed with clubs or sticks. They violently attacked the peaceful demonstrators, including women, leaving over 70 injured with some in need of hospitalization and surgery (see Crouch 2009a). This incident suggested, among other things, that the idea of an inclusive Pancasila is still opposed by those who support an Islamic state. It also indicates that law enforcement agencies lack the will to prevent radical Islamists from using violence to achieve their aims, which means that such groups remain an ongoing threat to those they target, such as religious minorities.
The debates over and changes to the Constitution have been part of the wider transition to a democratic and decentralized society, which has created more opportunities for religious resurgence and for both Muslims and Christians to proselytize as part of their democratic rights. The Pancasila, which includes the ‘belief in Almighty God,’ remains the central ideology for the state. The Ministry of Religion is the government department responsible for ensuring ‘inter-religious harmony’ (kerukunan umat beragama) by implementing policies that limit the right to religious freedom, which, as I explain below, has implications for the limits of proselytization.
The religious bureaucracy
Institutionalizing Islam: The Ministry of Religion
On 15 July 1945, in the lead-up to independence, an initial meeting was held with representatives from various political and religious organizations to discuss a proposal for a Department of Religion.16 A Christian representative from Maluku, Johannes Latuharhary, opposed the idea, arguing that the appointment of a Minister of Religion could cause conflict depending on whether he or she was Christian or Muslim (Dijk, 1981: 62). Many others agreed with him, including Abdul Abbas, a Muslim representative from Sumatra, and Raden Iwa Kusumasumantri, a nationalist. With only six of 27 members voting in favor, the initial proposal for a Ministry was rejected and responsibility for religious affairs was placed under the Department of Education (Dijk, 1981: 63).
Six months later, however, on 3 January 1946, the proposal was again raised, this time with wider support, and the Ministry of Religion17 was officially formed (Sairin, 2000: 92).18 The Ministry was largely a compromise with Muslim groups after the failure to include the seven words in the 1945 Constitution (Boland, 1982: 105–6). This is evident in the dominance of Islam in the Ministry from its inception. Initially, the proposed title was the ‘Department of Islamic Affairs’ (Fealy, 2003: 156). Up until 1954, a number of religious scholars at a national conference tried to declare the Ministry as part of a temporary Islamic state in Indonesia (Geertz, 1976: 212). Even the logo and motto of the Ministry is distinctly Islamic: the logo is a Qur’an resting on a rehal (Qur’anic bookstand), and its motto includes the Arabic-derived words ikhlas beramal (sincere commitment to service) (Fealy, 2003: 157).19 Over the years, the Minister of Religion has always been a Muslim.20
Nevertheless, the Ministry included a Directorate-General for each of the six recognized religions (see Lindsey 2012: 111). There are also two additional directors-general specifically for Islamic religious education and for the haj and umroh (pilgrimage to Mecca).21 The Ministry’s focus on Islam reinforced fears amongst non-Muslims of ‘Islamization,’ that is, a state based on Islamic law (Hefner, 1998: 111). This evident bias has been a point of contention for religious minorities since its inception (Boland, 1982: 106).
The establishment of the Ministry became an avenue to control public expressions of religion while supporting private religious observance. It promoted the idea that every citizen should have a single, fixed religious identity and adhere to one set of religious doctrines and beliefs (Steenbrink, 2003: 234). In particular, it sought to clarify the debate over the terms religion (agama) and local traditions (adat) or beliefs (kepercayaan), and the distinction between these two categories.22
The Ministry strengthened the distinction between government control over agama (religion) and aliran kepercayaan (mystical beliefs). The term agama comes from Sanskrit and refers to ‘a traditional precept, doctrine, body of precepts, or collection of such doctrines, anything handed down and fixed by tradition’ (Gonda, 1973: 499). Islam and Christianity are both considered ‘religions’ (agama) in Indonesia, as opposed to ‘mystical beliefs’ (aliran kepercayaan).23 According to Monnig-Atkinson (1983: 687), agama has strong associations in Indonesia with both Islam and Christianity, and to notions of power, wealth, political privilege, nationalism and literacy, due to the introduction of these religions through trade and colonialism. A common phrase used is ‘orang yang belum beragama’ – that is, a person who does not yet adhere to one of the official religions, which also implies that conversion to a ‘religion’ is inevitable (Monnig-Atkinson, 1983: 688–89). The Ministry therefore differentiates between religion (agama), which has been officially recognized and sanctioned, and belief (kepercayaan), which is discouraged and sidelined (Steenbrink, 1999: 282–83).
Further reinforcing perceptions of bias towards Islam, prior to 1998 one of the Ministry’s main roles was the supervision of the Islamic Religious Courts.24 In 1998, the Ministry was stripped of this power it shared with the Supreme Court to administer the Religious Courts (Pengadilan Agama).25 Despite this, the Ministry is still one of the primary vehicles for Muslims to advocate for Islam in public policy.
The role of the Ministry in overseeing the Religious Courts until 1998 was perceived by some Christians as an underhanded way of introducing Islamic law and as a government favor to Islamic groups. The formalization and unification of the Religious Courts in 1989 was a cause for great debate between Muslims and Christians.26 The draft law was taken as an indication that the government intended to bestow greater power and authority on the Islamic Courts (Hisyam, 2002: 307). The jurisdiction of the Religious Courts, however, is restricted to matters of marriage and divorce, wills, inheritance, wakaf (charitable trusts) and, since 2006, syariah economics.27 The majority of cases in the Religious Courts concern issues of marriage and divorce,28 and the Religious Courts have therefore become ‘divorce on-demand courts for women’ (Hooker and Lindsey, 2002: 282). The main reference point for the Religious Court judiciary is the Compilation of Islamic Laws, which covers issues of marriage, inheritance, religious trusts (wakaf) and divorce. Introduced through Presidential Instruction 1/1991 on the Compilation of Islamic Laws, the Compilation describes itself as a ‘guidebook’ and is not legally binding, not even on the Religious Courts (see generally Lindsey, 2012: 69–101).
Since 1998, Islamists have exerted power and influence through the Ministry of Religion, alongside MUI, in drafting ‘Islamic’ laws and defending a particular interpretation of Islam. Some national laws based on syariah did exist prior to 1998. In addition to the Religious Courts and the Compilation of Islamic Law, there were also changes in policy such as the founding of the Indonesian Association of Muslim Intellectuals (Ikatan Cendekiawan Muslim se-Indonesia, ICMI);29 the establishment of Islamic banking through Bank Muamalat Indonesia; the cancellation of the ban on wearing the jilbab (headscarf) in schools;30 and the abolition of the state lottery (Azra, 2004: 139). These changes were introduced in the 1990s as part of Soeharto’s limited openness to cultural Islam (Boland, 1982: 164). Hefner (2000: 109) highlights a less well-known policy change in the 1990s:
One of the least noted of the president’s many concessions [to Islam] at this time was his instruction that the DDII [Dewan Dakwah Islamiyah Indonesia] be allowed to operate in villages in Central and East Java where, a generation earlier, nominal Muslims had converted to Hinduism and Christianity.
These changes increased fears of ‘Islamization’ among Christians, that is, that Indonesia will become an Islamic state under Islamic law.
(Hefner, 2000: 18–19)
In the reformasi era, Islamists have been active in lobbying for the implementation of syariah law through the Ministry. For example, the system of zakat (tithe) has been upgraded to a law and further revised in 2011.31 Another change has been in the area of wakaf, that is, the ‘pious endowment given by Muslims for the support of Islamic religious activities’ (Federspiel, 1995: 283), for example property, such as a grant of land to a mosque or pesantren (Islamic boarding school). Under the New Order, this system was administered by government regulation and guidelines were set out further in the Compilation.32 Since 2004, the management of wakaf has been established formally as a national law.33 Islamic banking is another example. In 1998, state permission for the establishment of syariah financial institutions in Indonesia was granted.34 By late 2005 debate began on a Draft Law on Syariah Banking (Perbankan Syariah)35 and it was later passed in 2008.36
Further legislative reforms have addressed the administration of the haj, the pilgrimage to Mecca. Before 1968, the haj was run by private companies in Indonesia. Under Soeharto, the government asserted control over the organization of the haj by creating a Directorate-General for the haj under the Ministry and regulating the process through several Presidential Decisions.37 In 1991, Soeharto also personally made the pilgrimage to Mecca which was widely interpreted as a show of support for the Muslim community. Then, Law 17/1999 on the Haj introduced reforms to the government organization of the haj. Despite this, there have been ongoing complaints of corruption, poor management and inadequate service in regards to the organization of the haj. In 2005, these complaints culminated in the conviction of former Minister of Religion Said Agil Hussein Al Munawwar (2001–04), and his former subordinate Taufiq Kamil, on charges of embezzling more than Rp 700 billion (A$77 million) of haj funds (Jakarta Post, 7 October 2005). Partly in response to this controversy over the mismanagement of funds, Law 13/2008 on the Haj38 was introduced to reaffirm the Ministry’s role as the sole organizer, with the additional supervision of an independent committee consisting of government officials and members of MUI.39
Not all proposals for laws to regulate religious affairs or introduce Islamic law have been successful. In fact, one of the Ministry’s unsuccessful campaigns in the area of inter-religious relations is its attempts since the 1980s to pass a law on inter-religious harmony, which includes limitations on proselytization. As early as 1982, the Ministry of Religion proposed a Draft Law on a System of Religious Harmony (Aritonang, 2004: 419). A similar draft law was proposed in 1989 and again in 1997 (Aqsha et al., 1995: 93–97; Aritonang, 2004: 419). In 2002, the Ministry recommenced discussions about the possibility of a Law on Religious Harmony. In December 2003, the Research Office of the Ministry held a press conference to introduce a Draft Law on Inter-religious Harmony (Tempo Interaktif, 2 December 2003).
The 2003 Draft Law sought to address issues such as missionary activities; religious ceremonies and celebrations; places of worship; family issues; and blasphemy. It effectively aimed to collate and upgrade many of the Ministerial Regulations on religion to a law, as well as reinforce many MUI fatawa. The proposal for such a law has received little support because of sharp divisions on its merits.40 Expressions of support for the draft came from prominent figures such as a board member of MUI, Dr Amidhan Shaberah. He argued that there is a need for a code of ethics and guidelines among religions, that such a law is needed to help maintain inter-religious harmony, and that it would benefit religious minorities in particular by protecting their rights (Republika, 9 December 2008).
Groups against the Draft Law included the National Human Rights Commission (Komnas HAM), the Indonesian Communion of Churches (PGI), the Bishop’s Conference of Indonesia (KWI), the late former President Abdurrahman Wahid, and inter-religious harmony groups (Jakarta Post, 17 March 2000; Sinar Harapan, 13 August 2003). Representatives of these organizations argued that attempts to legislate harmony alone would not work and feared it could be used by the state to restrict proselytization unnecessarily (Bela, 2004). The 2003 Draft Law was also perceived to be against the principles of democracy, the Pancasila and the Constitution, and to discriminate against minority religions. Prominent scholar Djohan Effendi stated that the Draft Law should not be endorsed because it constitutes unwarranted state intervention in religious affairs (Jakarta Post, 24 June 2004). These groups created significant opposition to the Draft Law. In 2011, a new Draft Law was proposed by Commission VIII of the legislature, yet this draft also generated further public controversy and again was never formally proposed in the legislature (see Crouch, 2013). Although the Draft Law on Inter-religious Harmony remained on the 2011–14 legislative agenda at time of writing, it was unlikely to be passed in its current form.
In addition to failed proposals for reform, there have also been criticisms and calls for reform of the Ministry of Religion itself. In 1999, not long after the downfall of Soeharto, Abdurrahman Wahid, the then president, proposed to abolish the Ministry on the premise that it constituted government interference in religious affairs and breached the separation of state and religion. At the time, this proposal drew strong criticism from many circles, including leaders from Nahdlatul Ulama (NU) (Jakarta Post, 28 June 2002). In June 2002, there were suggestions that the Ministry should be replaced by a joint office to promote religious harmony across the country. Prominent scholars such as Komaruddin Hidayat of the State Islamic University Syarif Hidyatullah, and Ulil Abshar Abdalla of the Liberal Islamic Network (JIL), supported the proposed abolition of the Ministry (Jakarta Post, 28 June 2002). This was based on the need for greater accountability of public funds and the proposal for the Ministry’s responsibilities to be privatized, such as the administration of the haj. Further, in order to promote efficiency and avoid an overlap of administration, it was suggested that some of the Ministry’s roles could be divided up and more competently dealt with by existing government departments, such as the Department of Education also overseeing the provision of religious education. Despite the doubts cast on the credibility and integrity of the Ministry, the proposal to abolish the Ministry failed to gain momentum.
These criticisms of the Ministry suggest that some have taken an overly optimistic view of the potential role of the Ministry. For example, Boland (1982: 107) claims that the Ministry:
offered the possibility for religion, in particular for Islam, to function as effectively as possible in state and society; and, that in a Muslim country, this Ministry formed a useful middle way between a secular state and an Islamic state.
Although he suggested that it is useful for Islam, he failed to mention the negative influence it may have on other religions or beliefs. In fact, the Ministry’s explicit acceptance and favorable treatment of six recognized religions is to the neglect of other religions as well as beliefs. It is only these six religions that have national councils and are regularly consulted or included in decision-making processes of the Ministry. Moreover, the key body that represents the entire Muslim community within the Ministry of Religion is the Indonesian Ulama Council, which is strongly supportive of efforts to implement laws based on Islam, which, as will be shown, are often exercised in a way that compromises minority rights.
Establishing state-sanctioned ulama: The Indonesian Ulama Council
The Indonesian Ulama Council claims to be the inheritor of conferences of Islamic religious leaders held in 1947 and 1952. A provincial Indonesian Ulama Council was first established in West Java as early as 1957, led by KH Muhammad Sujai (Mufrodi, 1994: 88). In 1975, a national Indonesian Ulama Council was created as a quasi-government institution under the Ministry. It was established on 26 July 1976 with ulama representing the then 26 provinces (MUI, 2009). Chaired by Haji Abdul Malik Karim Amrullah, a prominent Islamic religious leader, writer, politician and activist within Muhammadiyah who came to be known as ‘Buya Hamka,’ MUI gradually established branches at the regional level across Indonesia. This was originally part of Soeharto’s plan to control the ulama and the public expression of Islam (Hooker, 2003: 60). According to Mudzhar (1996: 236), the reason MUI was formed so late in comparison to the other national religious councils (discussed below) is because Islamic leaders feared that it might be used or manipulated by the government further to subordinate Islam. One view, therefore, is that the New Order used MUI as a means to disseminate government policies to the Muslim community, primarily through fatawa (Mudzhar, 2002: 317).
A fatwa is a non-binding pronouncement by a qualified Islamic religious legal scholar on an issue, belief or practice made in the context of past interpretations (Federspiel, 1995: 59). The dynamics between MUI fatawa and state policy are complex, although a fatwa is not recognized as an official source of law by the state or the courts in Indonesia. A study by Mudzhar (1996: 236) found that most fatawa issued from 1975 to 1988 were supportive of government policy at the time, such as the 1984 fatwa on family planning. In the 1970s, the government’s approach to family planning failed because it had not convinced religious leaders of its policy and many ulama still taught that it was haram to use the pill or other family planning methods (Taher, 1997: 27–28, 57). In contrast, when MUI issued a fatwa in the 1980s, it lent greater credibility to the government’s family planning program. In this way, fatawa were a convenient means for the government to legitimate its policies in religious terms in the eyes of the Muslim community.
An alternative view is that the fatwa of the Indonesian Ulama Council informed and inspired government policy. Adams argues that fatawa based on Islamic law were influential in the drafting and passage of legislation based on the aspirations of the Muslim community and Islamic law (Adams, 2004: 1–2). He cites over ten laws that were influenced by the fatawa of MUI, the advice of MUI to the government, the submission of recommendations by MUI to the legislature and by the participation of MUI as a member of legal drafting teams.41 Adams also argues that MUI was instrumental in opposing policies that contravened Islamic law. For example, the jilbab or kerudung (headscarf) was initially banned in schools in Indonesia.42 On 12 October 1990, MUI sent a letter to the Department of Education and Culture requesting the cancellation of the decision. Then on 16 February 1991, the Director-General of Education and Culture issued a decision that permitted the kerudung to be worn as part of school uniform in schools (Adams, 2004: 165). Therefore, MUI had some influence in overturning policies that it saw as opposed to Islamic law.
Since 1998, MUI has become more independent of the government. For example, in 2000, the ideological basis of the organization was changed from the Pancasila to Islam (Hasyim, 2012). In terms of its members, MUI claims to represent the entire Muslim community in Indonesia. At times, however, its position has not reflected the diversity and divergence of opinion within the Muslim community, but instead appears to represent the interest of Islamists.
The two largest Islamic groups are Muhammadiyah and Nadhatul Ulama. Muhammadiyah is a modernist movement founded in Central Java by Ahmad Dahlan in 1912.43 It claims 35 million followers.44 Modernists reject taqlid, the practice of following the decisions of religious authorities, and believe that ‘the gate of ijtihad’ (the interpretation of legal sources) remains open (Hosen, 2005), and may be less tolerant of adat (local customs) which they see as leading to religious syncretism or relativism (Barton and Fealy, 1996: vii). Modernist Muslims seek to purify the faith by returning to the Qur’an and hadith (Sayings of the Prophet) to discern God’s will for society (Hooker, 2003: 230). One reason for the establishment of Muhammadiyah was to oppose and compete with the activities of Christian missionaries at the time.45
The other major organization represented by MUI is NU, which has grown since 1926 to become the largest Islamic organization in the world, boasting 50 million members with a particularly strong base in rural Java (Mujani and Liddle, 2004: 111). It was essentially a reaction to Muhammadiyah and aimed to preserve tradition. Traditionalists follow taqlid, the opinion of the ulama (religious scholars), rather than ijtihad, independent legal reasoning (Hosen, 2003). Members generally organize around pesantren (private Islamic boarding schools) and are often more tolerant of adat (local customs and traditions) (Fealy and Hooker, 2006: 40–41). As Mujani and Liddle (2004: 121) found, NU and Muhammadiyah are generally tolerant of other religions and do not usually object to Christians teaching in public schools, or to Christians building churches in Muslim residential areas.
MUI also represents the interests of ‘Islamists,’46 that is, individuals or groups who strive to replace the existing system of governance with an Islamic legal system.47 This aim is not surprising for a country with a majority-Muslim population, although the corresponding attitude of Islamists to non-Muslims raises broader concerns. For example, one study has shown that Islamists are less tolerant of non-Muslims, and are more likely to object to non-Muslim neighbors, a Christian teacher in a public school, or the building of a church in the neighborhood (Mujani and Liddle, 2004: 188). Islamists are also more likely to disagree with democratic principles such as the recognition of minority rights and equality before the law (ibid.: 119). Although Muhammadiyah is largely considered to be a modernist organization, it is sometimes associated with Islamism because, historically, more Indonesian Islamists have come from a modernist rather than traditionalist background (ibid.: 121).
Islamists, or ‘hardliners’ (garis keras) as they are known, are found across a broad spectrum of organizations in Indonesia (Lindsey 2011: 2). One important distinction is between Islamists who are willing to use violence, and those who are not. This book will use the term ‘hardliners’ or ‘radical Islamists’ to refer to the minority of Islamists who advocate and sanction the use of violence to implement Islamic teachings in the political and legal order of society. These ‘radical Islamists’ include groups such as the Islamic Defenders Front (Front Pembela Islam, FPI),48 Hizbut Tahrir and the Indonesian Mujahidin Council (Majelis Mujahidin Indonesia, MMI).49
One section of the Islamic community of which MUI is perhaps least representative is those who call themselves ‘progressive Muslims,’ the convergence of traditionalism and modernism characterized by a respect for classical learning with openness to modern influences and the West.50 Progressive Muslims are individuals or organizations who believe that the message of the Qur’an and hadith need to be reinterpreted in light of current social conditions and that Muslims must be more tolerant towards one another and towards non-Muslims. This includes many liberal Islamic groups or inter-religious harmony organizations initiated by Muslims that have flourished since 1998, such as the Liberal Islamic Network (Jaringan Islam Liberal) and the Wahid Institute. In 2005, the Indonesian Ulama Council issued a fatwa that banned pluralism, liberalism and secularism (MUI, 2005: 58–65). In the explanation to the fatwa, MUI identified secularism and liberalism as Western concepts that lead to relativism, the idea that all religions are the same. It singled out the Liberal Islamic Network as a proponent of these ideas, and denounced pluralism as wrong. The issuance of this fatwa suggested that MUI represents the voice of Islamists.
National Christian councils: Representing Christianity?
Christians are also represented in the Ministry of Religion. There is an historical distinction between Protestants and Catholics in Indonesia due to the colonial legacy. Christians may be referred to, or refer to themselves, as Nazarani or Nasrani – that is, followers of Jesus of Nazareth. There are various explanations for the use of this term. Stevens and Schmidgall-Tellings (2004: 658) claim that the term Nasrani was formerly used in Indonesia only to refer to Roman Catholics, while Protestants were known as Masehi, that is, followers of the Messiah, but that it is now used to refer to both Catholics and Protestants. My informants generally suggested that it was usually well-educated people who use the term Nasrani.51 This is considered more polite (as opposed to Kristen) because it is the translation of the Arabic term for Christian used in the Bahasa Indonesia version of the Qur’an (for example, 2:113).
The state and its institutions, such as the Ministry of Religion, generally use the term Kristen for Protestants and Katolik for Catholics. This clear distinction between Protestants and Catholics can be traced back to the initial policies of the Dutch government, which distinguished between Protestant and Catholic churches and only permitted one church to work in any given area (as discussed in Chapter 2). As Steenbrink (2003: 231) notes, ‘the choice between the two branches of Christianity became nearly as important as the one between Islam and Christianity.’ As new streams and expressions of Christianity, such as Pentecostalism and Evangelicalism, emerged in Indonesia, they were generally categorized as Protestant (Haire, 1981: 85). Over the years, however, the Christian community has grown beyond this simple Protestant–Catholic divide, and it must now be questioned whether the national councils still represent Christianity in Indonesia.52
There are two Christian councils within the Ministry. Catholics are represented by the national Bishops Council of Indonesia (Kantor Waligereja Indonesia, KWI).53 This institution was established in 1955 as the High Council of Indonesian Bishops (Majelis Agung Wali Gereja Indonesia, MAWI), and later changed its name to KWI. Protestants are represented by the national council for Protestants, known as the Indonesian Communion of Churches (Persekutuan Gereja-Gereja Indonesia, PGI), which primarily includes Presbyterian, Lutheran and Calvin churches, among many others. When it was formed in May 1950 with 27 member churches it was known as the Indonesian Council of Churches (Dewan Gereja-gereja Indonesia, DGI).54 Its largest member, both then and now, is the Batak Church (Nyhus, 1987: 84). In 2010, the membership of PGI consisted of over 83 church denominations (PGI, 2010b). The emergence of, and distinction between, these two national councils, one for Protestants and the other for Catholics, is also indicative of the ‘bitter rivalry’ between Protestants and Catholics in Indonesia (Steenbrink, 2003: 193).
Under the Soeharto regime, members of these two national councils enjoyed a ‘privileged position’ (Sidel, 2006: 48). Protestants and Catholics occupied a large number of positions of power and influence in government, disproportionate to the minority Christian population (Sidel, 2006: 63). Catholics and Protestants benefited from close relations with the government from independence and therefore considered themselves to be in a superior position compared to the newer Pentecostal and Evangelical churches.
Newer Christian denominations exist outside of the membership of KWI and PGI.55 From the 1920s until the 1980s, a third group, a mix of Evangelical, Charismatic and Pentecostal theology began to establish independent churches across Indonesia. Since the 1980s, as Evangelicals and Pentecostals have become more clearly defined institutionally, it is more accurate to say that there are now four broad types of Christian churches in Indonesia: Catholic, Protestant, Evangelical and Pentecostal. Some Evangelical churches are members of the Indonesian Evangelical Communion of Churches and Institutions (Persekutuan Gereja dan Lembaga Injili Indonesia, PGLII), which began in 1971 (then known as Persekutuan Injili Indonesia).56 Many Evangelical churches are independent, such as the Indonesian Reformed Church (Gereja Reform Injili Indonesia), the largest Evangelical Chinese church in Indonesia.
Pentecostal churches, which emphasized the Holy Spirit and spiritual gifts such as healing and prophecy,57 may be members of the Communion of Pentecostal Churches in Indonesia (Persekutuan Gereja-gereja Pantekosta di Indonesia, PGPI). Pentecostalism came to Indonesia through American missionaries in the 1920s. Between 1923 and 1970, at least 25 Pentecostal denominations were established (PGPI News, 2009c). PGPI began a few years later in 1979.58 Of its 78 member churches, some of the largest Pentecostal denominations include the Indonesian Pentecostal Church (Gereja Pantekosta di Indonesia), the Indonesian Bethel Church (Gereja Bethel Indonesia) and the Assemblies of God (Gereja Sidang Jemaat Allah).59
Protestant and Catholic churches have viewed Evangelical and Pentecostal churches with some concern, as they have lost some of their members to these newer movements. These movements are not always exclusive, however, as there have been ‘charismatic’ movements within Protestant and Catholic churches. Although Christians have, and often still are, traditionally classified along the organizational lines of PGI and KWI, this is no longer an accurate indication or representation of Christianity in Indonesia. It is also problematic because only KWI and PGI receive government funding through the Ministry of Religion, and are consulted by the government on matters of policy.
Since mid-2000, the Ministry has been aware that PGI no longer represents the Protestant community. It has discussed with PGI the possibility of creating a new umbrella organization for all Protestant, Pentecostal and Evangelical churches, which would replace PGI. It would aim to improve communications between the government and the churches, and to unify the growing number of new Christian denominations. According to officials from the Inter-religious Relations Division of the Ministry of Religion, PGI has been resistant to this idea to date, presumably because it does not want to lose its power, financial support and status within the Ministry. The long-held distinction between the Catholic and Protestant churches in Indonesia will also remain unchanged as there is no suggestion that Protestant and Catholic churches be gathered into one body.
One of the roles the national Christian councils have taken on is to issue public statements on behalf of the church concerning the introduction of laws regulating religious affairs. For example, in 2009, PGI issued a statement against the Draft Law on Zakat Administration and the Draft Law on Halal Products. It argued that Indonesia is not a ‘religious state’ (negara agama), that the state should not make laws for just one religious group, and that there needs to be a clear separation between religion and the state (Kristiani Post, 22 May 2009; Compass Direct, 19 August 2009). Another example is the draft law on population administration in 2006, which included changes to the national identity card. In this debate, PGI argued that it was not necessary to include ‘religion’ as a category listed on a person’s registration card (Yewangoe and Sairin, 2009: 145). Both PGI and KWI have generally opposed attempts by the state to regulate religion, such as the Draft Law on Inter-religious Harmony. Such laws are perceived as attempts to implement syariah law and raise concerns about the effect on religious minorities. Any government response to regulating religion must therefore recognize the diversity within the Christian community, and address concerns about the impact of such laws on minorities. Laws regulating religion, particularly syariah law, have been opposed by Christian-based political parties also, although as I will show, there is little popular support for religious ideology in Indonesian politics.
Losing faith in politics
Indonesia’s political landscape has historically divided between nationalist parties based on the Pancasila and religious-based parties. The vast majority of political parties in Indonesia are nationalist parties based on the Pancasila. The longest-standing nationalist party is Golkar, which won all six (rigged) elections under the New Order while backed by Soeharto and the army.60 The Indonesian Democratic Party Struggle (Partai Demokrasi Indonesia – Perjuangan, PDI-P) is a nationalist political party formed in 1998, an offshoot of the former PDI, which was created by the forced amalgamation of political parties under Soeharto in 1973. Partai Demokrat, established in 2001, is the party of President Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono (2004–09; 2009–14). Other main national political parties are PKB, created in 1998 by members of NU, and PAN, created in the same year by members of Muhammadiyah. Although PAN and PKB do not profess an Islamic ideology, their electoral support comes primarily from Muslims affiliated with the Islamic social organizations that established them (Fealy, 2001: 122).
Christians have a history of activism in both national and Christian-based political parties. There are several Christian political parties that are also based on the Pancasila, because it is seen to be opposed to an Islamic ideology, and these parties have clear links to pre-New Order Christian political parties.61 In 1945, two national Christian political parties were formed. The Indonesian Christian Party (Partai Kristen Indonesia, known as Parkindo) was intended to channel the political aspirations of Protestants. Many of those involved were from the Batak Church (Nyhus, 1987: 520). The other was the Catholic Party of the Republic of Indonesia (Partai Katolik Republik Indonesia, PKRI) formed by Ignatius Joseph Kasimo Hendrowahyono as a political party for Catholics (McTurnan, 1952: 158). Neither of these two parties received many votes at elections. By 1973, these parties were forced to become part of PDI, which in 1984 was required to embrace the Pancasila as its sole ideology. This effectively prevented any party from claiming a religion as its basis or ideology. Therefore, from 1973 to 1998, there were no political parties explicitly based on Christianity. Nevertheless, PDI-P is still supported by many Christians.
It has only been since 1998 that Christian-based political parties have been re-established in Indonesia. On 1 October 2001, the Peace and Prosperous Party (Partai Damai Sejahtera, PDS) was established by Denny Tewu and Ruyandi Hutasoit. It is the only political party with a logo that includes the cross, a central image associated with the Christian faith. The current leader of PDS is Ruyandi Hutasoit, the son of Manixius Hutasoit, one of the former leaders of Parkindo.62 Ruyandi is also a pastor and chairperson of the Doulos, a Christian mission agency that was burnt down in 1999 by radical Islamists who opposed its proselytization activities (Ananta et al., 2005: 25). PDS constituents primarily come from the Christian-majority provinces of Papua, Maluku, Southeast Sulawesi, and Central and West Kalimantan (Jakarta Post, 25 March 2009). The vast majority of its candidates are Christian and over 60 per cent of the party’s supporters are Protestant Christians (Shihab and Nugroho, 2008: 252).
In May 2006, after a falling-out amongst leaders of PDS, some members left PDS to form the Devoted Democracy Party of Indonesia (Partai Kasih Demokrasi Indonesia, PKDI). The party symbol is a pine (Christmas) tree in a circle of a white pentagon with the rosary, which is very similar to the symbol used by the former party Parkindo. In its background statement, PKDI describe its initiative as ‘Partisipasi Kristen Indonesia (Indonesian Christian Participation, PARKINDO),’ using the same acronym as the former Christian political party. Led by lawyer Stefanus Roy Rening, PKDI is a coalition of several Christian-based political parties.
Since independence, a number of political parties expressly based on Islam have formed, although with little success. In the first election in 1955, there were two Islamic-based political parties that gained a significant percentage, though not a majority, of the vote: NU and Masyumi.63 In August 1960, Masyumi was banned due to its involvement in the 1958 rebellions aimed at overthrowing the government (Ricklefs, 2008a: 306). A few years later, in 1973, NU was forced to merge into the United Development Party (Partai Persatuan Pembangunan, PPP), which today is the only Islamic-based political party to survive the New Order. The two parties with the largest support base that were formed post-1998 are PBB and PKS. The Crescent Moon and Star Party (Partai Bulan Bintang, PBB) was formed by the older generation of DDII and Masyumi activists in 1998 (Haris, 2003: 62).
To some extent, post-Soeharto Indonesia has witnessed the re-emergence of political Islam through the establishment of political parties that adopt an Islamic ideology. In 1998, Partai Keadilan (PK) was formed, claiming to embody the tradition of Masyumi. Despite its Islamist beginnings, there are indications that, since 2009, ‘PKS has shifted from a hardline party to a more moderate stance to expand its constituency’ (Shihab and Nugroho, 2008: 233). According to Fealy, attempts to ‘rebrand’ itself as a mainstream, rather than Islamist, party have not been entirely convincing, as its rhetoric of ‘openness’ and ‘PKS for all’ have been clouded by the secrecy with which its internal sessions were held at the 2010 National Congress (Fealy, 2010).
The political landscape opened up and diversified since the fall of Soeharto and the transition to democracy in 1998. Just one week after becoming president, Habibie gave permission for the formation of more political parties. Consequently, over 90 parties emerged during the second half of 1998, although only 48 of these parties qualified to contest the 1999 election (Riddell, 2002: 66). Some 18 political parties were based on an Islamic ideology. The three Islamic parties managed to obtain 14 per cent of the vote.64 For PK, this meant that it had to re-brand under the new name the Prosperous Justice Party (Partai Keadilan Sejahtera, PKS) to contest the 2004 elections.
In 2004, out of 24 parties that contested the election, 16 were based on the Pancasila.65 The five parties based on Islam received a total of 20.83 per cent of the vote, which represents a slight increase for Islamic parties from the 1999 electoral results (Suryadinata et al., 2003: 16–17). In particular, PKS won 7.3 per cent of the votes, or 45 seats (Ananta et al., 2005: 113–14). In terms of Christian parties, in the 2004 election, PDS secured 2.13 per cent of the national votes and 5 per cent of the votes in Jakarta (Aritonang and Steenbrink, 2008: 894). Many of its votes were won in predominantly Christian areas such as Manado (North Sumatra) as well as from the Chinese Indonesian community (TAPOL, 2008: 13).
Other significant changes took place in 2004, such as the first-ever direct presidential election. There were two coalitions: the National Coalition, including Golkar, PDI-P, PPP and PDS, which supported Megawati Soekarnoputri (the daughter of first President Soekarno) for president; and the People’s Coalition, including PD, PBB and PKS, which supported Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono. Significantly, these coalitions blurred any distinction between parties based on the Pancasila and those based on Islam (Suryadinata, 2005: 21). As Mujani and Liddle (2010a: 39) highlight, ‘the lack of significance of religious factors was particularly glaring in the presidential contest.’
Religious-based political parties fared no better in the 2009 elections. PKS only achieved 7.9 per cent of votes, while PPP received 5.3 per cent. PDS also did not fare well. It failed to reach the 2.5 per cent threshold and consequently no longer holds any seats in the legislature (DPRD). PKDI were also unable to reach the 2.5 per cent threshold. As Mujani and Liddle (2010a: 37) noted, in the 2009 elections there was ‘little evidence that voters were influenced by their religious, ethnic, regional or social-class identities.’
The success of religious-based political parties in Indonesia since the introduction of democracy has been limited, and Islamic parties in particular have failed to gain a significant percentage of votes in the national elections. Political parties were increasingly concerned with advancing and securing the Islamic-majority voter base, particularly in the lead-up to the 2009 elections. In turn, some parties have shown a willingness to support controversial national laws that seek to uphold aspects of Islamic law.
One key example is the Draft Law on Pornography. At the national level, the issue of prostitution and the regulation of sexuality were first debated in 1999 through the introduction of the Draft Law on Anti-Pornography and Pornographic Acts. It sought to regulate public expressions of sexuality in order to protect the community and promote good religious morals. The Draft Law was shelved and lay dormant until it arose on the legislative agenda again in 2005 (Pausacker, 2008).
The Draft Law caused considerable controversy for its attempt to regulate sexuality and the behavior of women. The Islamic-based political party PKS was one of the major advocates for the Draft Law on Anti-Pornography (Shihab and Nugroho, 2008: 248). Other supporters of the Draft Law included Muhammadiyah, Nadhatul Ulama and the MUI (Sherlock, 2008: 160). MUI had previously issued a fatwa that declared pornography forbidden (haram).66 The only political parties that opposed the Draft Law were the nationalist party PDI-P and Christian party PDS (Setara Institute, 2008: 13). The Draft Law was also of concern to liberal and feminist groups, and religious and ethnic minorities. For example, Christians in Papua and Hindus in Bali opposed the draft because they felt that it would restrict their traditional dress or customs (Pausacker, 2009: 121). After several amendments, it was finally passed in 2008.67 In April 2010, concerned groups, including women’s rights groups, legal aid organizations and PGI, lodged a request for judicial review of the Pornography Law in the Constitutional Court. The applicants argued that the law would unnecessarily restrict religious rituals of religions other than Islam.
Aside from the Pornography Law, the Christian political party PDS has been outspoken on issues related to religious minorities and pluralism, such as places of worship and local religious regulations based on Islam. For example, some of the 56 members of the legislature who petitioned the president in 2006 in opposition to perda syariah were from PDS. In another significant court case, PDS campaigned for the release of three Christian men accused of masterminding the violence in three villages in Poso in 1999, despite inconsistent and insufficient evidence to support these allegations. The campaign was unsuccessful, and the men were executed in September 2006.68 Chapter 4 will show PDS has been active in supporting other Christians accused of criminal offences against Islam. Further, in 2008, PDS was part of a fraction that rejected the Draft Law on Shariah Banking, and as a result has been criticized for being syariah-phobic. This may be in part because it does not argue that Islamic law must respect the rights of non-Muslims, but rather that Islamic law should not be enforced by the state.
One of the most significant aspects of the post-1998 transition has been the free and competitive political environment. A striking feature of this new political era in Indonesia is the relative lack of support in national elections for religious or faith-based political parties as opposed to nationalist parties – that is, those based on the Pancasila ideology. Although Muslim political parties such as PKS and Christian parties such as PDS may have a religious agenda, they have won little support at the national elections. As a result of the historical lack of support for faith-based political parties in Indonesia, issues of religious sensitivity cut across political lines. All political parties, excluding Christian ones, demonstrate a willingness to support the passage of laws based on Islam in the scramble for votes. There is also the further complicating factor of corruption and illegality that overshadows political parties and the state in Indonesia (Aspinall and van Klinken, 2011). This is of concern given that, as discussed in Part II (Chapters 4–6), laws may be manipulated by Islamists in a way that compromises the rights of religious minorities.
Democracy, decentralization and religion
An intensely competitive electoral environment has formed at both the national and regional level due to the rapid process of decentralization in Indonesia since 1998. Between 1999 and 2004 alone, the process of pemekaran (lit. blossoming), that is, the creation of new regions, saw a 50 per cent increase in the total number of regencies (Carnegie, 2008: 522).
