In addition to the opening of new and contested local political arenas, the decentralization of power has transferred greater power and freedoms to local authorities. This means that the politicization of religion is particularly concentrated at the local level.
Since the fall of President Soeharto and the New Order, Indonesia has undergone a transition from centralized control to democracy and decentralization. In 1999, the Habibie government implemented landmark Decentralization Laws.69 As of January 2001, the Decentralization Laws handed government control to the 400 regencies/cities rather than the 30 provinces that existed at the time. By 2009, Indonesia had about 500 regencies and cities. This redistribution of powers fundamentally changed the relationship and position between central and local governments. This process has been ‘one of the most substantial re-allocations of powers inside a nation state in modern times’ (Smith, 2003: 97), and implementation has therefore presented a significant administrative challenge. This has fuelled corruption at the local level, as decentralization may ‘provide a slice of patronage resources for the bureaucrats, political bosses or networks that dominate these areas’ (Aspinall, 2010: 27).
Major shortcomings were soon identified in the Decentralization Laws. Practical implementation was unclear and the relationship between the central government and the regions remained uncertain. For these reasons, the Decentralization Laws have been labeled ‘flawed’ (Bell, 2001: 43), and ‘contradictory’ (Smith, 2003: 105). Despite revisions to the Decentralization Laws in 2004,70 ambiguity over the powers of local authorities remains. Little has been done to keep the law-making activities of regional governments in check, particularly in relation to matters of religion, despite the fact that this power has been retained by the central government.
The Decentralization Laws regulate the levels of government administration in Indonesia. The provincial and regency/city governments are authorized to pass regional regulations (peraturan daerah, perda)71 on any area except those specifically retained by the national government, namely: foreign affairs, national defense, national security, judicial affairs (yustisi),72 economic policy, and religion.73 This power to regulate matters of ‘religion’ includes national religious public holidays, official recognition of a religion, policies on the implementation of religious life, and religious matters of national significance.74 The central government can delegate the power to oversee some aspects of religion to regional government authorities if it will help to develop the religious life of the community.
Nevertheless, local laws remain subordinate to a number of other higher laws, according to the hierarchy of laws in the Indonesian legal system. This hierarchy ranks laws as follows: the Constitution, Laws, Government Regulations, Presidential Regulations, Provincial Regulations75 and Regency/City Regulations.76 These laws are listed in order of rank and a lower law, such as a Provincial Regulation, may be invalid to the extent that it contradicts national law. Islamic law is not part of the government-recognized legal hierarchy in Indonesia. Since the failure of the Jakarta Charter at the national level, however, Islamists advocate for the implementation of Islamic law at the regional level. This demonstrates a shift in the focus of efforts for the implementation of syariah in Indonesia. Radical Islamic groups have taken a grassroots approach given the failure of the top-down implementation of syariah through the Constitution in 2002. In the grab for political power, regional governments have responded by claiming the power to regulate on matters of ‘religion,’ despite the questionable legal basis to do so.
As a result, there has been a trend across Indonesia for regional governments to introduce aspects of Islamic law through regulations. This has led to the promulgation of a large body of regional regulations known as perda syariah (peraturan daerah syariah), based on Islam, which seek to enforce aspects of Islamic law that may conflict with national laws. These regulations seek to revitalize and enshrine Muslim beliefs and practices, such as Friday prayers and the management of zakat (giving of alms). Some form part of a wider crackdown on social problems such as prostitution, gambling, alcohol and drugs. Others ban men and women from swimming together, expel students from school who cannot recite the Qur’an and forbid children from playing PlayStation at an entertainment complex in their school uniform. The wide range of regulations can be classified into nine categories: clothing, prostitution, social problems (maksiat), alcohol and drugs, religious rituals, zakat management, Qur’anic education, local governance, and non-Islamic regulations (Crouch, 2009b). Despite the questionable legal basis (dasar hukum) of these religious regulations, the growth of religious regulations has continued unchecked by the national government.
Controversy over the introduction and enforcement of religious regulations has arisen in relation to jilbab (women’s headscarf) inspections, and the caning of people convicted for syariah offences (ICG, 2006). Although non-Muslims are generally exempt from complying with perda syariah, the introduction of religious regulations has been an issue of concern for Christians. Many non-Muslims have expressed concerns about the proliferation of local regulations based on Islam, and about Islamic law in Aceh in particular.
Most religious regulations that have emerged are based on Islam. There has also been a struggle, however, in some Christian-majority regions with proposals for religious regulations based on Christianity. The prime example is a draft regulation to make Manokwari (Papua) a Bible-based city, which was first proposed in March 2007.77 Among other things, this draft would make it an offence to use symbols of religions other than Christianity (art. 26). It would make the building of a non-Christian place of worship a crime if a church already exists in the area (art. 30). It would also forbid wearing clothes that have religious significance, for example, the jilbab. If implemented, this regulation would make it very difficult for non-Christians to practice their religion, particularly because Protestantism and Catholicism are well-established in Papua. The draft regulation was rejected in 2007 and although a revised draft was issued in 2008 with minor revisions, it was not passed as law.78
This expression of conservative Christian religious values had little support from the Christian community outside of Papua, and was rejected by KWI and PGI (UCAN, 30 March 2007). The draft was also criticized by Muslim leaders and organizations, such as PKS and MUI, as problematic as the state should not endorse one religion, although this was ironic given that such complaints have been put forward by non-Muslim critics of perda syariah proposed in majority-Muslim areas.
There have been few instances of opposition to religious regulations at the national level. In June 2006, the first significant opposition to religious regulations from within the legislature arose when 56 legislators submitted a petition to the president. This petition was signed by members of PDI-P, Partai Demokrat and PDS (Jakarta Post, 14 June 2006). In response, 134 politicians from the Islamic-based PPP and PKS submitted a counter-petition in favor of perda syariah (ICG, 2006). Several ministers have spoken out on the issue since these petitions were lodged. In 2007, the Department of Home Affairs promised to review 37 perda syariah that were in conflict with higher laws (Jakarta Post, 30 August 2007). In the same year, the People’s Consultative Assembly Speaker, Hidayat Nurwahid (who is the former chairperson of PKS), asserted that demands to implement syariah did not need to be fulfilled by the formal implementation of Islamic law. In August 2008, Chief Justice of the Constitutional Court Mahfud Md declared that religious regulations should not be enacted by regional administrators because they were illegal and contrary to the Pancasila (Jakarta Post, 23 August 2008). Local governments continue to create new perda syariah, although most religious regulations remain of symbolic value only and are rarely implemented or enforced.
The decentralization of power has therefore led to greater politicization of religion – that is, the use of religion for political gain – at the regional level. This has created tensions between national and local laws, as well as between Muslims and Christians, and within Muslim and Christian communities. The influence of decentralization and the conditions that led to the emergence of religious regulations will be illustrated next by examples from local contexts.
Regional regulations and radicalism in West Java
The proliferation of regional regulations based on Islam post-1998 can be seen as an expression of a small disparate but vocal group of hardliners. Religious regulations exist in the majority of provinces and the largest numbers of religious regulations have been generated in the provinces of West Sumatra, South Sulawesi and West Java. In West Java, perda syariah exist in at least 14 cities and districts. In terms of impact, Tanthowi’s study of the influence of religious regulations on non-Muslims in Cianjur (West Java) found a very low level of awareness or understanding of the implementation of syariah (53 per cent; Tanthowi, 2008). Large numbers of non-Muslims declined to participate in the survey as they were afraid to disclose their opinions. Of the non-Muslims who were willing to participate, Tanthowi found that some non-Muslims had been forced to comply with the religious regulations, such as women being coerced to wear Muslim clothing in the workplace on Fridays. This indicates a reluctance or fear on the part of religious minorities such as Christians to challenge the emergence of regulations based on Islam.
Some of these religious regulations have been challenged in court. The most well-known case occurred in 2005 when a woman, Lilis Lindawati, was falsely arrested and accused of being a prostitute under the Perda of Tangerang 8/2005 on Prostitution. The ‘evidence’ was nothing more than lipstick and foundation in her handbag (which is common for Indonesian women). On 27 February 2006, Lilis was sentenced to 80 days in prison and fined Rp 300,000 (A$33). She was released four days later after her husband paid the fine. The injustice of this case and the validity of the regulation were challenged by a network of NGOs known as the Coalition Against Discriminatory Perda. In April 2006, the Coalition sought judicial review of the decision in the Supreme Court.79 Ultimately, the court declined to resolve the controversy surrounding her arrest and the legality of religious regulations in general. Instead, in April 2007, the Supreme Court announced that it had rejected the application and refused to decide the question of whether this religious regulation is outside of the powers of local governments because it believed this was a matter for the executive and legislature to decide (Pelita, 16 April 2007). No written decision has been published by the court in this case.
A less well-known example of regional regulations being challenged in court is a string of cases on regulations that limit the sale and consumption of alcohol. These regulations forbid the sale or consumption of alcohol in a public place, based on the presumption that the use and/or abuse of these substances are contrary to religious norms and social well-being. While some may argue that such regulations are more concerned with social order and morality than religion, the reaction from some hardline (garis keras) Islamic groups at the potential cancellation of these regulations suggests that such regulations are at least perceived by them to be indirectly upholding Islamic law. For example, in 2012, a report that nine regulations on alcohol had been repealed by the minister was met with protests from Islamic groups. This news also prompted the Islamic-based political party PPP to suggest that a national law should be passed to ban the sale and consumption of alcohol (Jakarta Globe, 16 February 2012). The mayor of Tangerang was also prepared to take a case for judicial review to the Supreme Court against the decision (City of Tangerang, 2012). The report that such laws would be cancelled, however, was denied by the Minister of Home Affairs (Jakarta Globe, 11 January 2012).
Court cases challenging the validity of regulations on the sale and consumption of alcohol have occurred in areas such as Subang and Kuningan (West Java) as well as Jambi, and in part are driven by business interests. The first case reached the Supreme Court on appeal in 2002. It was brought by 14 owners of small restaurants who were concerned because the 2001 regulation on alcohol in Kuningan did not allow the sale of ‘category A’ alcohol (that is, 1–5 per cent alcohol content), unless it was sold in hotels that had a permit. The applicants argued that the regulation was inconsistent with Presidential Decision 3 of 1997 on the Supervision and Sale of Alcohol, which does not require a permit for the sale of category A alcohol. The applicants were partly successful, with the court striking down the provisions in the Kuningan Regulation that were inconsistent with the Presidential Decision (Supreme Court, 2002). Similar cases have been brought on appeal to the Supreme Court concerning regulations on alcohol in Jambi (Supreme Court, 2008d) and Subang (Supreme Court, 2010a), with the same outcome.
Aside from religious-based regulations, NGOs have documented the high incidence of discrimination against religious minorities in West Java. According to Setara Institute (2010a: 3, 27–29), the highest number of violations of the right to freedom of religion between 2007 and 2009 occurred in the province of West Java. In a report by Nahdlatul Ulama, West Java was found to be the province with the highest level of religious intolerance (Jakarta Post, 21 December 2010).
A correlation has been identified between areas that have issued religious regulations and the history of radical Islamic group Darul Islam (Bush, 2008: 181). West Java is a center for hardline Islam and home to Darul Islam (Domain of Islam). In 1947, Darul Islam emerged in West Java promoting the ideology of an Indonesian Islamic state (Negara Islam Indonesia, NII) and as a rebellion against Dutch colonial attempts to retake Java. In 1948, it began an armed rebellion against the Republic and a year later declared Indonesia an Islamic state based on Islamic law.
Once the Dutch withdrew from Indonesia, Darul Islam began working towards the establishment of Islamic law in Indonesia. In February 1948, the first Darul Islam conference was held in the village of Pangwekusan, Tasikmalaya (Horikoshi, 1975: 70). On 7 August 1949, Darul Islam officially proclaimed the Islamic state of Indonesia based on syariah in the village of Cisampang, Tasikmalaya (Dijk, 1981: 92; Horikoshi, 1975: 72). The national leader of Darul Islam, Kartosuwirjo, who was also one of the founders of the Islamic political party Masyumi, formed several other Islamic organizations in Tasikmalaya (Dijk, 1981: 84).
The Darul Islam movement soon spread from West Java to other provinces, including Central Java, South Kalimantan, South Sulawesi and Aceh (Dijk, 1981: 1; ICG, 2005a). West Java was still the most important and strategic area, and there Darul Islam had an army of over 15,000 men (Fealy, 2007: 68). More lives were lost during the Darul Islam wars in West Java than in any other region (Fealy, 2005b: 17). Between 1953 and 1958, it is estimated that over 11,000 people were killed, 1,200 kidnapped, hundreds of thousands of houses burned, and over 1 million people internally displaced in West Java (Dengel, 1995: 147). In total, about 15,000 to 20,000 died. As an indication of the magnitude of the conflict, Fealy (2005b: 18) highlights that ‘the cost of the rebellion, both in lives and assets, far exceeds anything perpetrated by violent Islamic groups such as Laskar Jihad and Jema’ah Islamiyah during the post-Soeharto period.’
As late as 1956, the NII was still in control of half of the regencies of Tasikmalaya, and 75 of 201 villages were partly or wholly controlled by Darul Islam (Dijk, 1981: 102; ISAI, 1998: 107). The state was not able to contain this movement until 1962, 17 years later, preventing Darul Islam at that time from achieving its aim of establishing an Islamic state (Boland, 1982: 54–75). Although Kartosuwirjo was executed in 1962, the ideals and goals of Darul Islam have been taken up by other groups since.80
The struggle to establish an Islamic state has continued under the banner of Negara Islam Indonesia (NII). In 2002, the police made a number of arrests of suspected members of NII. None of the suspects arrested was charged (Jakarta Post, 11 April 2002). Several years later, in 2008, 17 members of NII were arrested and found guilty by a court for plotting to undermine the Indonesian state.81
Radical Islam is a minority within West Java, although 97 per cent of the population, or over 41 million people, are Muslim (BPS, 2010). It is the most densely populated province in Indonesia, and only 1.8 per cent of the population, fewer than 800,000 people, are Protestant, while less than 0.5 per cent, or 250,000 people, are Catholic (BPS, 2010). To cater for the Muslim population, West Java has the largest number of mosques of any province in Indonesia, with almost 38,000 (Ministry of Religion, 2010a).82 In contrast, there are only 1,075 Protestant churches and 117 Catholic churches.
The small number of Protestant and Catholic churches is one indication of the largely unsuccessful history of Christian missionary activities in West Java. This is partly because West Java was one of the majority-Muslim areas in Indonesia that remained officially closed to missionary activities until the twentieth century (Steenbrink, 2003: 209). The history of Christianity in West Java is also different to other parts of Java, due to the lack of Eurasian or Javanese members in the first congregations (Steenbrink, 2005: 286). In West Java, the first missionaries from the Netherlands Missionary Union (NMU) arrived in the 1850s. Although its workers obtained permits from the government, their presence in West Java generated unease among the colonial government because of concerns their activities may cause conflict with the Muslim Sundanese population (Coppel, 2002: 293). There were some missionaries from the Dutch Mission Society (Nederlandsche Zendings, NZV) working in areas such as Indramayu and Tasikmalaya, and baptisms into the church were recorded from the 1870s.83 Church congregations established by the NZV in the late 1800s and early 1900s were either Chinese Indonesian, as in Indramayu, or a mix of Chinese and other ethnic groups, as in Tasikmalaya (End, 2006: 46).
The number of Christians in West Java grew slowly from around 1,700 in 1900, to 3,400 in 1922 (Soejana et al., 2008: 664), although these were mostly Protestants. In 1927, 8,000 Catholics were recorded, although the majority were European with only 108 Indonesians (Steenbrink, 2007: 355). By 1942, the number of Indonesian Catholics had increased to 1,552, while the number of European Catholics had increased to 19,091 (Muskens, 1974: 820, 832). Many Catholics are concentrated in areas such as Bandung, and since 1998 the strength of radical Evangelical Christian movements is evident in areas such as Bekasi, West Java (ICG, 2010). I look further at the development of Christian communities in Indramayu, Tasikmalaya and Depok in Part II.
The growth of religious regulations and an increase in religious intolerance has coincided with the competitive local environment. Local elections have led to ‘fierce intra-elite competition’ among candidates and parties running for provincial and local government elections (Buehler, 2007: 140). In 2009, 69 of 109 representatives from West Java in the national legislature were from nationalist parties such as PD and PDI-P, while 29 were from Islamic-based parties. The reputations of former governors of West Java have been mired by cases of corruption. In 2009, R. Nuriana, the former governor of West Java (1993–2003), was investigated by the National Corruption Eradication Commission (Komisi Pemberantasan Korupsi, KPK). Known as the Bank Jabar-Banten case, he was implicated in major tax fraud by executives of PT Bank Jabar (Seputar Indonesia, 22 July 2009). In the same year, former governor H. Danny Setiawan (2003–08) was convicted for four years for receiving Rp 7 million (A$780) from the Bank of West Java (Media Indonesia, 11 May 2010). In 2008, Setiawan lost the provincial elections to H. Ahmad Haryawan, who is a member of PKS.
This brief discussion of the province of West Java indicates that regional debates on the relation between law and religion are just as important as national debates in an era of decentralization. Christianity has a relatively brief history in West Java and many of the first Christian communities established in places such as Indramayu and Tasikmalaya consisted primarily of Chinese Indonesians. The history of radical Islam and support for an Islamic ideology in the area is evident in the politicization of religion through support for regional regulations based on Islam.
Conclusion
There have been several challenges by Muslims to the constitutional foundations of the state and this has sparked tension and debates both within and between Muslims and Christians about the relationship between religion and the law since 1998. The outcome has been that the right to religious freedom has been further entrenched in the legal framework, and the effort by radical Islamic groups to insert the Jakarta Charter in the Constitution in 2000 failed to receive widespread support. The Pancasila remains the official ideology of the Indonesian state. This provides a solid basis for the constitutional recognition of religious diversity and the right to proselytize in Indonesia.
In the shift to democracy and decentralization, the national government has retained the power to regulate matters of ‘religion,’ although it can choose to delegate that power to local governments. Islamists have worked through two main institutions to introduce national laws based on Islam: the Ministry of Religion and the Indonesian Ulama Council. Although it no longer oversees the Religious Courts, the Ministry has retained its power to oversee the religious affairs of the six official religions in Indonesia. It has promoted ‘orthodox’ Islamic teachings and been instrumental in the creation of laws based on Islam. MUI is arguably more independent from the government than under the New Order. The two Christian organizations recognized and consulted by the Ministry of Religion, KWI and PGI, no longer represent the many faces of Christianity. This limits the extent to which new or independent churches outside Catholicism or Protestantism, often Pentecostal or Evangelical churches, can legitimately advocate change through the political process.
From a political perspective, more political parties based on Islam or Christianity have emerged post-1998. The results of the three national general elections since 1998, however, demonstrate that there is little support for religious-based political parties in Indonesia. Despite the lack of support for the introduction of syariah law as an obligation for Muslims via the Constitution, many political parties support the creation of national laws based on Islam in an effort to attract the majority-Muslim constituency in the competitive political environment.
This has led to a situation where both national and regional laws increasingly favor Islamic law, which may compromise the rights of religious minorities. This may be appropriate in a majority-Muslim country, except for the fact that what syariah is, and how, or even whether, it should be enforced via state law, is hotly contested in Indonesia. The national government’s inaction or reluctance to intervene means that regional governments are exercising their powers to implement laws on religion without any form of accountability.
This is demonstrated in the province of West Java, a majority-Muslim area with a history of radical Islam, where religious sentiment is being manipulated in regional politics in an era of decentralization. This has led to the introduction of regional regulations based on Islam. As my case studies in the following chapters will show, these legal developments have contributed to legal disputes between Muslims and Christians in West Java.

Part II
Legal case studies

4  Child protection and limits on proselytization
The case study in this chapter, the first of three in this book, focuses on one particular court case that arose in the Indramayu District Court, West Java. It analyses the Indramayu Religion Education Case, in which two Christian teachers and their pastor were found guilty on charges of proselytization under Law 23/2002 on Child Protection. This case is significant because it is the first case in Indonesia in which Christians have been charged and found guilty of the criminal act of deceiving Muslim children to change their religion.1 I use this case to examine how fears of Christian proselytization through institutions such as Christian schools and hospitals are being perpetuated through laws and their interpretation in the courts. I analyze the debate on religious education, and demonstrate how the policy of segregated religious education was extended from public to private schools. Compliance with the new Education Law has varied, however, as a range of new approaches to religious education has emerged.
I then turn to the second aspect of the case, child protection, and explore the implications of the new criminal offence of deceiving a child to change his or her religion. Finally, I look at some of the particular issues of fact, evidence and interpretation that were debated in the Indramayu Religious Education Case, in which three Christian women were convicted for the criminal offence of deceiving Muslim children to change their religion. This case is an example of the influence of radical Islamic leaders at the local level, who lodged criminal allegations with the police concerning Christian proselytization. Combined with the intimidation tactics of radical Islamic groups, these allegations contributed lead to convictions in the local court.
Indramayu Religious Education Case
This case arose in response to Law 20/2003 on the National Education System (the Education Law), which requires both public and private schools to provide religious education for students according to their religion. This requirement was inserted in response to long-held complaints that Christian schools do not provide Islamic religious education for Muslim students who attend the school.
Since 2000, a Protestant church in Indramayu, affiliated with the National Protestant Council (Persekutuan Gereja-gereja Indonesia, PGI), ran a ‘Happy Sunday’ Christian religious education program for Christian children. The program consisted of Christian songs, games and Bible study, as well as practical tutoring in reading, writing and mathematics, and outings to parks and swimming pools. This program was run by two Christian women, Ratna Bangun and Ety Pangesti, under the supervision of their pastor, Dr Rebekka Zakariah. In 2003, in order to comply with the law, a primary school in Indramayu approached this church and asked it to report the grades of the Christian students who attended the program to the school. It is common in rural areas in Indonesia for Christian religious education classes to be held outside school hours, given the shortage of religious education teachers and the lack of funding for religious instruction.
Up to 40 children attended the program, mostly Christians. The program was held on a Sunday and from the home of one of the teachers in a village area. Some Muslim children, as well as some children from inter-religious marriages (that is, children with one Muslim parent and one Christian parent), occasionally visited the program. The teachers claimed that all the children had the (verbal) consent of their parents to attend the program. Further, some of the Muslim parents had participated in an outing to the zoo as part of the program, confirmed by photographs taken at the event (District Court, 2005d: 8–10).
Dr Rebekka, who oversaw the program, also worked as a medical doctor. She provided free medical service to disadvantaged people in the area and reports had emerged that some of her patients had converted to Christianity. According to Kiai Syakur Yasin, leader of Candang Pinang pesantren (Islamic boarding school) in Indramayu, over 30 families had become Christian because of the free medical service and assistance they had received from Dr Rebekka.2 This angered some Muslim leaders in the community. The chairperson of Nahdlatul Ulama (NU), Syairozi Bilal, recalled that this situation caused tension when the new converts asked for identity cards listing their religion as ‘Christian.’ The regent sent a team to the area to investigate whether this was an incident of ‘Christianization.’3 In the opinion of Muslim officials from the Ministry of Religion, this was a clear case of apostasy and Dr Rebekka had bribed these people into changing their religion.4 Local religious leaders and government officials therefore had another reason, in addition to the Education Law, to complain about Dr Rebekka.
In late 2004, some local Islamic leaders complained to the local Indonesian Ulama Council (Majelis Ulama Indonesia, MUI) when some of the Muslim children and children of mixed marriages began singing Christian songs at home. The head of MUI of Haurgeulis, KH Mundzir Machmud, consequently reported the case to the police (Republika, 12 May 2005). The program was forced to close in April 2005. In May 2005, the women were arrested, and in the following month the trial began in the Indramayu District Court. According to Pastor Ruyadi Hutasoit, a colleague of Pastor Rebekka and the leader of the Peace and Prosperous Party (Partai Damai Sejahtera, PDS), the largest national Christian political party in Indonesia, his church had agreed to pay for 30 children to attend a pesantren (Islamic boarding school) as compensation for any harm or offence caused. Ruyadi claimed that this agreement was made on the condition that the case against the three women would be dropped.5 Despite this, the case against the three women was pursued in court.
The trial began on 30 June 2005 in the local Indramayu District Court.6 On 5 September 2005, the women were convicted for breaching article 86 of the Child Protection Law. This provision states that:
Any person who deliberately uses deceit, a series of lies or entices a child to choose another religion7 not of his or her own will, despite it being known or suspected that the child is not mature and is not yet responsible according to the religion he/she follows, will be liable to a maximum jail sentence of five years and/or fined at least one hundred million rupiah.8
The women were also initially charged with blasphemy under article 156a of the Criminal Code, although this charge was later dropped. In September 2005, the three women were found guilty under the Child Protection Law of the offence of deceiving a child to change religion. They received a three-year jail sentence, which they served in the Indramayu Correctional Institution. The teachers’ appeals to the Bandung High Court, and then the Supreme Court, were both rejected (High District Court, 2005; Supreme Court, 2006b). After these events, Rebekka’s church came under pressure to close. A number of international advocacy campaigns were initiated on behalf of the three women. On 12 June 2007, after spending over two years in prison, the women were granted an early release on parole. According to lawyer Aries Wibowo, some members of the Christian political party PDS, who had been supporting the women throughout the case, were present when the women were released.9
Debating religious instruction
The Indramayu Religious Education Case raised the ongoing concern that Muslim students who attend private Christian schools should not have to learn about Christianity but rather be provided with Islamic religious instruction. The Education Bill proposed in 2002 was the cause of major debate both in society and in the legislature (see DPR, 2003). The provision on religious education, which aimed to extend the policy of compulsory religious education to private schools, was of particular concern to Muslims and Christians. The arguments raised in this debate reflect many of the deep-seated prejudices and suspicions between Muslims and Christians from Indonesia’s colonial past. I examine the extent to which the Education Law has influenced schools at the local level and how some religious organizations have designed innovative approaches to religious education, irrespective of national government policy.
Advocating for religious education in Christian schools
Those in favor of the Education Bill and its provisions on religious education included Islamic-oriented political parties such as the then Justice Party (Partai Keadilan, PK), now known as Partai Keadilan Sejahtera (PKS), the United Development Party (Partai Persatuan Pembangunan, PPP) and the Crescent Star Party (Partai Bulan Bintang, PBB) (Jakarta Post, 10 June 2003; 7 May 2003). It also included nationalist political parties, such as the Democratic Party of Struggle (Partai Demokrasi Indonesia Perjuangan, PDI-P) and the Party of the Functional Groups (Golongan Karya, Golkar). Board members of the General Leadership Council of the Indonesian Ulama Council, such as Professor KH Umar Shihab (2000–05), were outspoken in their support of the right to religious education in private schools (Republika, 14 March 2003). According to KH Mar’uf Amin, the Indonesian Ulama Council was one of the key supporters of this proposal.10 At the time the Education Bill was proposed, leaders of Muhammadiyah also expressed their support, as did many Islamic educational institutions and organizations (Jakarta Post, 28 March 2003). The debate was even joined by groups of school children, such as those who protested in front of the Makassar (South Sulawesi) legislature as a show of support for the Bill and as a protest against apostasy efforts through education (Republika, 1 May 2003).
Supporters of the Education Bill contended that it was necessary for the state to enforce a sectarian policy on religious education in both public and private schools. It was seen as an important way to integrate religious education into all education institutions. This would ensure many Muslim students who attend Christian schools receive Islamic rather than Christian religious education, alleviating the perceived ‘risk’ of conversion. Many also believed religious education was a necessary response to the nation’s perceived moral decay, such as rampant corruption, by instilling ethics and morality via religious education.
Second, these parties appealed to and acknowledged international norms on two levels. They argued that the Education Bill was in line with the right to freedom of religion and equal opportunity because it allowed a student to receive religious education according to their religion even if they were attending a school run by another religion. Restricting religious education to the six religions, and only allowing children to attend religious education of his or her religion, was seen as a legitimate restriction on the right to religious freedom.
Third, and perhaps most important, its supporters believed the law was necessary to dispel the perceived and real risk of attempts at Christianization. They were concerned to ensure that a Muslim child who is enrolled at a Christian school is not required to learn about Christianity, that the risk of proselytization is contained and the possibility of apostasy prevented. At a rally held outside the legislature in June 2003 in support of the Bill, demonstrators, including Secretary-General of MUI Din Syamsuddin, claimed that those who opposed the Bill were Christians trying to convert Muslims through education in Christian schools (Jakarta Post, 11 June 2003). According to Yunahar Ilyas, board member of Muhammadiyah, the Education Bill was essential to ensure religious-based educational institutions do not use the provision of education as a means of religious proselytization, which in his opinion can lead to apostasy.11
Not all support for the Education Bill, however, should be considered genuine, particularly the support of nationalist political parties. There was speculation that Golkar only endorsed the Education Bill in order to obtain the support of Muslim voters in the lead up to the 2004 elections. At the time, Golkar legislator Baharuddin Aritonang admitted that the debate on the Education Bill was related to the vested interests of political parties in the looming 2004 elections (Jakarta Post, 12 June 2003). Some Muslim-based political parties even publicly declared that the controversial Education Bill was a trade-off with radical Islamists, as a substitute for the failed attempt to insert the seven words from the Jakarta Charter in the 2002 amendments to the Constitution (BBC, 12 June 2003). PDI-P, the political party of the then President Megawati Soekarnoputri, initially supported the Education Bill. On the day the Education Bill was endorsed, PDI-P boycotted the legislature to delay the passage of the Bill. This tactic had political motivations, as the Bill had provoked fierce opposition and PDI-P wished to delay its passage so it would not affect the party’s popularity in the upcoming 2004 elections (Jakarta Post, 12 June 2003).
Opposing state interference in faith-based schools
On the other side of the debate, many groups opposed the religious education provisions of the Education Bill. This included the largest traditionalist Muslim organization in Indonesia, Nahdlatul Ulama (NU), the Indonesian Communion of Churches (Persekutuan Gereja-gereja Injil, PGI) and the Bishops Conference of Indonesia (Kantor Wali Gereja, KWI) (Jakarta Post, 14 April 2003; 5 April 2003; 11 June 2003). In several predominantly Christian provinces, the Regional People’s Representative Council (DPRD) voiced its dissent, including in East Nusa Tenggara, Papua and North Sulawesi (Jakarta Post, 20 May 2003; 28 June 2003). The Education division of the National Commission on Human Rights (Komnas HAM) opposed the Education Bill, as did the former president of Indonesia, the late Abdurrahman Wahid.12 Many Christian and Catholic private educational institutions and organizations also spoke out against the Education Bill (Asia Times, 10 May 2003).
First, these groups argued that the Education Bill constituted unwarranted state interference into religious education and potentially into the affairs of private schools (Jakarta Post, 20 March 2003).13 It was argued that compelling private schools to provide religious education according to a student’s religion discriminated against Christian schools because, as mentioned earlier, many Muslim parents send their children to Christian schools due to the reputation for high-quality education in Indonesia. In comparison, according to Protestant scholar Jan Aritonang, Christian children rarely attend Islamic schools, so this requirement is unlikely to affect Islamic schools.14 Christian schools further argued that it is the right of private schools to require parents to sign a statement that they have no objections that it is a Christian-based school and does not provide Islamic religious instruction. Failing or refusing to give written consent means that parents cannot send their children to a Christian school. Opponents also cited the practical and financial burden these requirements would place on Christian schools, such as hiring a Muslim teacher, creating an Islamic religious education program, setting up a mushollah (prayer room) and so on. This was seen as impractical, as well as contrary to the purposes of Christian schools (Jakarta Post, 15 March 2003). It was argued that the Education Bill allowed the state to override the rights of private religious schools in this regard (Jakarta Post, 29 April 2003).
In the drafting process, Pastor Weinata Sairin, along with Catholic representative Father Benny Suseno, opposed the Education Law because they felt that it amounted to government interference in the private affairs of Christian schools.15 The Protestant Education Council and the National Catholic Education Council both rejected the draft law, which provided a penalty of ten years or a fine of Rp 1,000,000 (A$110) if a person or group did not provide religious education according to a child’s religion (Yewangoe and Sairin, 2009: 128; art. 57(3)).
In addition, these groups argued that this would place an unnecessary obligation on Christian schools, interfering in the affairs of private schools. Private Christian schools would be forced to provide more than just Christian religious education, so parents feared they could not be assured that their child would receive a Christian education even if they sent them to a Christian school. Opponents of the Education Bill argued that the state could not override the claims of two other major stakeholders in religious education: parents16 and educational institutions (Jakarta Post, 29 April 2003; 2 May 2003).
Finally, there were suspicions that the Education Bill was part of an effort to Islamize Indonesia, undermine Christianity and turn Indonesia into an Islamic state. The legislature of Papua, a majority-Christian province, argued that the enforcement of laws-based Islam would be detrimental to social harmony and a threat to national unity, and declared that it would not implement the law (Jakarta Post, 28 June 2003). This is a common argument raised by religious minorities in Indonesia each time a law based on Islam, or a law perceived to favor Muslims, is proposed. The National Human Rights Commission also appealed to international law, arguing that the Education Bill would promote a narrow view of religious education at the expense of the right to religious freedom for many religious minorities (Jakarta Post, 14 May 2003).
Despite these objections, the debate culminated in the passage of the Education Bill into Law 20/2003. The Islamic-based political party PKS openly claimed it to be one of its success stories (Shihab and Nugroho, 2008: 248). An analysis of the provisions of the Education Law on religious education as passed demonstrate the underlying concerns to minimize the ‘risk’ of conversion.
Reforming religious education: managing the risk of conversion
The Education Law reflects a shift in the religious education policy of the government. In 2002, there were indications that changes to government policy on education were underway when the role of religion in education was expanded further in the amendments to the Constitution. Article 31(3) states that the education system aims to increase the faith and ‘God-consciousness’ of its students.
Not long after this, in July 2003, Law 20/2003 on Education (‘Education Law’) was enacted. This was part of the democratic reform process in Indonesia and emphasized the implementation of decentralization and public accountability,17 and firmly established religion as part of the general education program.18 The two key provisions of the Education Law in relation to religious education are article 12(1)(a) and article 30. Article 12(1)(a) requires all schools to provide religious education for a student according to his or her religion. It states that:
Every student in every educational institution has the right to:
a)
Obtain religious education according to the religion they follow and to be taught by a teacher who is of the same religion …
This is one of the most controversial aspects of the law, because it establishes a segregated model of religious education that is now mandatory for both public and private schools. According to the Constitutional Court (2010a: 273), this provision is constitutionally valid because it upholds the right to religious freedom by ensuring that every student has the right to religious education in Indonesia.
In 2007, the implementing regulation was passed,19 which delegated the responsibility to oversee religious education to the Ministry of Religion. The Regulation requires all educational institutions to set aside a room for religious education classes (art. 4(4)), although it clarifies that an educational institution that is based on one religion is not obliged to build a place of worship for another religion (art. 4(7)). For example, this means that it is sufficient for a Christian school to set aside a classroom for Islamic religious education classes, it does not have to build a mosque or mushollah (prayer room). According to Atho Mudzhar, Head of the Research Division of the Ministry of Religion, this policy is intended to ensure that the provision of religious education is not an unnecessary financial burden on schools.20 The Regulation states that if an educational institution conducts religious education that is not in line with the requirements in this Regulation, it will receive a warning from the government or may be forced to close (art. 7(1)). The forced closure of a school is a serious consequence, given the impact it would have on the students, their families, and the loss of jobs for teachers.
The Regulation distinguishes between formal, non-formal and informal education. All non-formal religious education programs that have more than 15 students are required to register with the Ministry of Religion, so that it can monitor religious education programs that take place in the community. The Regulation sets out the kind of religious education programs schools should provide for each of the six recognized religions, including Islam, Protestantism, Catholicism, Hinduism, Buddhism and Confucianism. The Education Law contains no explicit offence if a teacher allows a student to attend a religious education class of a different religion.
The Regulation further requires that religious education should be a key element of school curriculum for all primary, secondary and higher education institutions. According to the 2004 Competence-based Curriculum (Kurikulum Berbasis Kompetensi), primary schools must provide three hours of religious education per week while secondary schools must provide two hours. A study of the actual hours allocated to religious education in public schools conducted prior to the introduction of this curriculum found that a student in Indonesia attended an average of 298 hours of religious education in the first six years of formal education, or just one hour per week (Rivard and Amardio, 2003: 214). This three hours per week guide therefore appears to be a significant increase in comparison to current practice at the time the regulation was introduced.
In addition to these national requirements, in the province of Aceh there is also Qanun 5/2008 on the Implementation of Education (replacing Qanun 23/2002) due to special autonomy. The Qanun shares many similarities with the national Education Law, but also has some important differences. The emphasis is on every child having the right to receive high-quality, Islamic education. The same right as contained in article 12(1)(a) of the Education Law is replicated in article 9(1)(a), but it also adds the right to be protected from behavior that is opposed to religious, legal or customary norms. This could be read as a prohibition on proselytization of people who already have a religion. The Qanun places social and moral obligations on students not to smoke, drink or take drugs, or to engage in pornography, porno-action, gambling or adultery. The Qanun also places a responsibility on parents to ensure a child receives basic religious education (art. 10(2)). There are specific requirements to recite the Qu’ran and students can only graduate from primary school if they can meet these requirements (art. 26(3)), while graduation from junior high school requires further competency in Arabic (art. 27(4)). Finally, it specifically addresses foreign, non-Muslim organizations and requires such institutions to obtain permission to establish an educational institution in Aceh (art. 59), placing a high level of government control over foreign educational organizations. This Qanun gives clear priority and emphasis to Islamic religious education. It also places greater responsibilities on students, parents, teachers and foreign, non-Muslim schools in particular. Apart from the standard right to religious education, the Qanun is silent on the provision of religious education for non-Muslim students in Aceh.
Overall, the national laws on education in Indonesia relating to religious education have undergone significant changes. As a result of Islamist campaigns to restrict the influence of Christian schools, the government requires religious education to be provided in both public and private schools according to the religion of the student. This policy has not yet been implemented widely, and I show that some schools subvert the policy, and by implication stereotypes of Christianization, by developing innovative, alternative approaches to religious education.
Innovation in religious education
Responses of Muslim and Christian organizations and schools to the introduction of the new policy on religious education, and to concerns over proselytization through religious educational institutions have varied. Many new approaches to religious education essentially subvert government policy on segregated religious education by providing students with the opportunity to learn about their own religion and that of others, as well as about related issues of human rights, ethics and morality.
The discourse and rhetoric of Islamist religious leaders since 1998 demonstrates concerns about the perceived efforts of Christian schools to proselytize Muslim students, more so than the implementation of religious education according to the Education Law. This is primarily evident through the issuing of a fatwa (opinion of Islamic legal scholars) strongly warning against, or even forbidding, Muslim parents from sending their children to Christian schools. For example, in Yogyakarta in October 1998, the Indonesian Ulama Council issued a fatwa that declared it haram (forbidden) for a Muslim student to attend a non-Muslim religious education class (MUI, 2008a: 25–26). This was followed by a concerted campaign against private Christian schools in the area, with many Islamic religious leaders strongly encouraging Muslim parents to send their child to an Islamic school (MUI, 2008a: 20–37). There were many banners displayed around the city of Yogyakarta at that time bearing slogans such as ‘Muslims who attend Christian schools are kafir [unbelievers],’ and ‘It is better to send your children to Muslim schools otherwise your children might convert to another religion.’21
Similar fatawa were issued at the local level in other areas, such as in Bogor, West Java. For example, the West Java Pesantren Cooperation Body (Badan Kerjasama Pondok Pesantren) based in Bogor, West Java, issued a fatwa that states that it is haram for Muslim parents to send their children to a Christian school because of the need to protect Muslim children from Christian proselytization.22
Although fatawa have been issued by MUI at the local level, the national MUI has not issued a fatwa on religious education. Chairperson of the national MUI (2005–09) and Special Advisor to the President on Religious Affairs (2007–09) KH Ma’ruf Amin, admitted that some of the branches of the Indonesian Ulama Council in the provinces or cities/regencies had issued ‘recommendations’ that Muslim parents send their child to an Islamic school to uphold ‘correct beliefs’ (aqidah).23
Leaders within MUI have been quick to point out that many Christian schools have refused to comply with the Education Law. For example, MUI of Yogyakarta reported that 97 Protestant and Catholic schools in the province of Yogyakarta did not provide Islamic religious instruction for their Muslim students (MUI, 2008a: 29–32). There is, however, no legal sanction for a school principal who fails to implement a religious education program. There are currently no data available on the total number of Christian schools that have not complied. According to Catholic scholar Frans Magnis Suseno, some Protestant and Catholic churches oppose the Education Law because they do not believe they should have to provide religious education for other religions in a private Christian school,24 although the school connected to his theological seminary did not take this approach. Many Christian private schools have rejected the law, particularly in areas with a Christian majority population, because of concerns that Muslim teachers may be ‘anti-Christian’ in their approach to religious education.25 This, however, only fuels stereotypes that the reason Christian schools do not provide Islamic education is because they are managed by a church that has a hidden missionary agenda.26
Some Christian schools have ignored the requirement to provide Islamic religious education for their Muslim pupils, while other Christian schools have modified its religious education curriculum.27 For example, some Catholic schools have introduced a more inclusive religious instruction class for students of all religions, known as pendidikan religiositas, or ‘religiosity education’ (Leirvik, 2004: 232).28 The curriculum was designed by the Catholic church in Semarang. Although Catholic religious instruction is still taught, it is now a separate class and non-Catholic students are not required to attend. This is not the approach of all Catholic schools, however. A study of Catholic religious education in 11 cities across Indonesia found that approaches vary, with five different types of curriculum currently in use.29 Some Catholic schools do not have a problem with the new religious education policy and have arranged for an Islamic teacher for their Muslim students.
A diversity of approaches has also been taken by Protestant schools.30 Some Protestant schools have changed the name and content of what used to be the Protestant religious education class. For example, many Protestant schools no longer refer to these classes as ‘religious education’ or ‘Christian education,’ but rather as pendidikan moral/ethika, ‘ethical/moral education,’ or pendidikan budi pekerti, ‘character education.’31 At the least, this indicates that Christian schools are aware that religious instruction is a highly contentious issue. The curriculum of some Protestant schools has been adapted to avoid controversy and to educate students about all religions, as well as about moral and ethical issues.
Aside from Christian schools, there have been two major changes in the approach taken by the schools run by Muhammadiyah. First, the Ma’arif Institute, founded by the former leader of Muhammadiyah (1998–2005), Ahmad Syafi’i Ma’arif, published a new religious education curriculum for Muhammadiyah schools. This course is designed for junior high schools (sekolah menegah umum, SMU) by Abdul Mu’ti (director of the Center of Dialogue and Cooperation among Civilizations) and Fajar Riza Ul Haq, the program director of the Ma’arif Institute. The course had two main emphases: education about Muhammadiyah, and education about Islam from a human rights perspective. The first subject, and corresponding textbook, was called Kemuhammadiyahan, ‘Muhammadiyah-ness’ or ‘the essence of Muhammadiyah’ (Ma’arif Institute, 2008a). According to Abdul Mu’ti, this material provided the opportunity for both Muslim and non-Muslim students to learn about the foundations, history and teachings of Muhammadiyah.32 It aimed to promote understanding about the core values of Muhammadiyah and to help Christian students understand the diversity within Islam, such as why Muslims who affiliate with Muhammadiyah would not feel comfortable praying in a mosque run by Nadhatul Ulama.33 The textbook even mentioned the highly controversial issue of aliran sesat, or groups considered to be ‘deviant.’ It approached this issue by stressing the need for harmony and tolerance, balanced with the importance of maintaining the true teachings of Islam (Ma’arif Institute, 2008a: 51–56). This is remarkable, given the ongoing controversy against Ahmadiyah (see generally Crouch, 2012a). Importantly, the teachers’ instruction manual also included information on ‘deviant’ sects to promote greater tolerance among Islamic religious education teachers (Ma’arif Institute, 2008b: 203).
The second subject concentrated on the teachings on Islam, and the textbook is simply entitled Al-Islam (Ma’arif Institute, 2008b). This class was designed for Muslim students so that they can learn about their religion from a human rights perspective.34 For example, one of the lessons deals with the right to freedom of religion and places of worship (Ma’arif Institute, 2008b: 14), which is particularly important due to the difficulties faced by religious minorities to build places of worship (see Chapter 5).
The textbooks were distributed to over 100 junior high schools and many more schools had requested copies of the textbooks.35 The books, however, have not been received well by all Muhammadiyah schools. For example, in Palu, Central Sulawesi (an area of intense conflict between Muslims and Christians from 1999–2001), one school initially refused the books because the school thought the textbook was promoting pluralism, which was declared haram by a national MUI fatwa of 2005 (as mentioned in Chapter 3).
Aside from a new curriculum within some Muhammadiyah schools, the Ma’arif Institute conducted a study into religious education provided by Muhammadiyah schools in areas with a Christian majority (Mu’ti and Ul-Haq, 2009). The research was conducted in Muhammadiyah schools in three majority Christian areas (Ende, East Nusa Tenggara; Putussibau, West Kalimantan; and Yapen Waropen, Papua). The results of the research were published in a book referred to by the acronym ‘KrisMuha,’ or ‘Kristen Muhammadiyah: Konvergensi Muslim dan Kristen dalam Pendidikan,’ ‘Muhammadiyah Christians: The Convergence of Muslims and Christians through Education.’ The report found that Muhammadiyah schools did not provide Christian teachers for Christian students who attended those schools, but the schools did allow Christian students to attend religious education classes at a nearby Christian school or church, and so fulfilled the requirements of the Education Law (Mu’ti and Ul-Haq, 2009: 226). In sum, these new approaches and efforts demonstrate that religious organizations and schools are reworking the curriculum of religious education quite independently of developments in religious education policy at the national level.
One of the real barriers for schools that are willing to comply with the religious education policy is a shortage of qualified teachers.36 The Ministry of Religion has acknowledged that this is one of the biggest issues that need to be addressed. The Ministry of Religion, together with the Department of Education, aims to address this problem by assisting schools to find qualified and available religious education teachers to teach religious education classes. One way of addressing this issue, which is common practice in Indonesia, is to allow religious education to take place outside of school hours. This allows flexibility so that, for example, attending Sunday School on a Sunday counts as ‘religious education’ for Christian children, as long as the church reports the grades of the children to the school, as happened in the Indramayu Religious Education Case.
A related issue dealt with by Muhammadiyah at the national level is whether it is permissible for a Muhammadiyah school to employ a non-Muslim teacher. A fatwa issued by Muhammadiyah states that if there is no Muslim qualified and available to teach, then it is permissible to employ a non-Muslim teacher (Muhammadiyah, 1990: 214). If there is a qualified (Muhammadiyah) Muslim teacher available, then they must be given preference (Muhammadiyah, 1990: 214). Even if a teacher is available, another difficulty is funding to provide wages, especially if there is only a small number of children of a particular religion at a school.
The shortage of teachers and funding is compounded by the absence of a formal accreditation program. Since 2010, the Ministry of Religion has implemented a training and qualification process for all religious education teachers.37 Each national religious council has the responsibility to oversee the certification process for teachers. For example, Pastor Jan S. Aritonang, as the vice-chairperson of the national PGI, was given the responsibility to oversee the certification process for all Protestant religious education teachers in Indonesia.38
There have been concerted efforts by hardline Islamic religious leaders to oppose perceived ‘proselytization’ efforts of Christian schools by encouraging Muslim parents to send their child to an Islamic school. Acutely aware of this issue, and of broader concepts of tolerance and human rights, some Muslim and Christian schools have begun to change their approach to religious education, placing a greater emphasis on human rights and religious freedom, as well as ethics and morality, independent of the more restrictive developments in government policy. In addition to the Education Law, however, there have also been expressions of concern about the need to protect the religion of a child in the amendments to the Child Protection Law.
Criminalizing deceptive proselytization of children
In Indonesia, government guidelines on proselytization have existed since the 1970s, as explained in Chapter 2. The offence of proselytizing a child to change his or her religion, however, is a relatively new development. Under Soeharto, Law 4/1979 on Children’s Welfare addressed the need for child protection in Indonesia, although it did not contain a prohibition on religious proselytization. In 1990, President Soeharto ratified the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child (1989).39 Article 14 of this Convention obliges states to respect the rights of children to freedom of religion, although it does not explicitly specify whether this includes the right to change religion.40 This interpretation is narrower than the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR), which has been construed widely to include the right to change religion.41
National laws in Indonesia relating to child protection and proselytization have undergone significant reform since 1998. In November 1999, the Minister of Human Rights announced that a draft bill on the protection of children would be drafted by the National Commission for Child Protection.42 In February 2000, a Child Protection Bill was suggested and in November 2001, after further debate, an amended version of the Child Protection Bill was proposed in the legislature.43 During this process, there was an absence of debate concerning article 86 of the Child Protection Bill.44 This may be explained by reports that the Child Protection Bill was passed despite poor performance from legislators in attending to their legislative duties (Jakarta Post, 24 September 2002). In October 2002, the Child Protection Law came into force and set out national standards for the protection of children, with the primary aim of addressing issues such as physical abuse, trafficking, child labor, prostitution, exploitation, adoption, guardianship and custody, among other issues.
The national Child Protection Law contains numerous provisions on the religion of a child that are designed to ensure a child has the right to education in the religion of his or her parents. It protects the right of a child to worship according to his or her religion, under the guidance of their parents (art. 6). It clarifies that before a child has the ability to choose, he or she must follow the religion of their parents (art. 42(2)). It places a corresponding obligation on children to practice their religion according to the teachings of that religion. The state is also forbidden from discriminating against a child on the basis of religion (art. 21).
Foster parents, guardians and adoptive parents are required to have the same religion as the child under their care.45 Furthermore, any religious organization that cares for children, such as an orphanage, is only allowed to care for children of the same religion (art. 37(3)). Before the Bill was passed, there was some debate concerning this issue and whether parents must be of the same religion as the child they adopt (Jakarta Post, 24 September 2002). Groups such as MUI were successful in lobbying the government to ensure that the Child Protection Law only allowed foster parents to adopt a child of the same religion, to prevent the risk of conversion through adoption (Jakarta Post, 19 July 2002). This change in policy appears to have been partly to appease members of President Megawati Soekarnoputri’s fragile coalition at the time (The Age, 22 January 2005). The Government Regulation that followed reinforced that foster parents must have the same religion as the foster child and if the religion of the child’s biological parents is not known, the child should follow the religion of the majority of the population in that area.46 This places a significant, yet necessary constraint on the activities of adoption agencies and overseas placements to ensure adoption processes are not manipulated on the grounds of religion.
The national Child Protection Law and corresponding Government Regulation on Child Protection reflect the position of MUI on the issue of adoption as expressed in a fatwa issued in 1984 (Ministry of Religion, 2003: 178–81). In particular, this fatwa decided that ‘Adopting a child from a birth father and mother is opposed to Islamic syariah,’ which suggests a couple can only adopt orphans. Further, when a child is adopted, it specifies that adoption should not change the religion of the child. Aside from the national law and the Indonesian Ulama Council, Aceh is the only province that is allowed to enforce Islamic law. After the tsunami in 2004, there were many issues concerning children from Aceh and this raised fears that Muslim children were being Christianized.47
There were allegations that the post-tsunami aid efforts of some organizations, particularly foreign non-Muslim organizations, were exploiting the vulnerability of Muslim orphans. For example, in 2005, Worldhelp, an American-based Christian aid group, attempted to transport 300 children from Aceh to Jakarta with the intention of raising them in an orphanage there. It was prevented from doing so by local officials and Islamic religious leaders because of fears that it would attempt to convert the children to Christianity (Human Rights Without Frontiers International, 17 January 2005). In the wake of such alleged incidents, the provincial government of Aceh passed Qanun 11/2008 on Child Protection, which in part addressed concerns over Christian proselytization by foreign aid organizations. This Qanun protects the right of a child to practice the religion of their parents (art. 4(1)). A child whose parents are missing must follow the religion of the majority of the population in that area (art. 5(1)), namely Islam. It requires Muslim parents and guardians to educate their child about their religion, particularly the recitation of the Qur’an (art. 8(2)). Foster parents must have the same religion or mission (misi) of the child for whom they care (art. 9(3)), as do guardians (art. 19(1)). There is also a prohibition in article 59 against foster parents or guardians changing the religion of the child that carries a penalty of imprisonment, which is similar to article 86 of the national Child Protection Law. There are clear parallels then between the national Child Protection Law and the Qanun on Child Protection in Aceh, although the latter arguably goes further in protecting Islam and in addressing concerns over Christian proselytization by foreign aid organizations.
The Qanun in Aceh and the national Child Protection Law were the first of their kind in Indonesia. The national government’s commitment to implement the Child Protection Law was reinforced by Presidential Decision 77/2003, which established the Commission for the Protection of Children (Komisi Perlindungan Anak Indonesia, KPAI) to oversee the Child Protection Law (art. 74–76). The Commission has three basic duties: to educate the public, to accept complaints and to submit recommendations to the government. In 2005, the Commission signed a three-year Memorandum of Understanding with MUI so that together they could promote, implement and supervise the Child Protection Law. The issue of child protection is of concern to MUI and this may explain why MUI played such a key role in bringing the case against the three women in the Indramayu Religious Education Case.
In the Indramayu Religious Education Case, it was alleged that the three women breached article 86 of the Child Protection Law, which makes it an offence to deceive or trick children to change their religion. I consider the interpretation and application of article 86 by the court in relation to the role of religious authority and the broader socio-political context of the case.
Court dynamics and religious authority
The Indramayu Religious Education Case adds a new chapter in the enforcement of limits on proselytization in Indonesia. It is the only case in which Christian religious education teachers have been charged and found guilty of the criminal act of ‘Christianizing’ Muslim children. The case took place within the context of a history of Christian missionary activity and radical Islam. It will be situated within the history of religious missionary efforts in the area and the debate over the extent to which Muslims can participate in the ceremonies of other religions in particular. The role of parents and whether a Muslim child is allowed to convert will be explored. Finally, I also analyze the implications of the Constitutional Court’s decision that article 86 of the Child Protection Law is constitutional.
Local history of Christianity and radical Islam
The regency of Indramayu, established in 1950, is a majority Muslim area, with over 1.7 million Muslims, 4,368 Protestants, 2,835 Catholics, 160 Hindus, 297 Buddhists and 13 Confucianists (Regency of Indramayu, 2010: 207). The minority Christian community in the area can be traced back to the arrival of Christian missionaries in the 1880s.
Among the first missionary applications to be approved for permission were for the regions of Indramayu and Cirebon (Coppel, 2002: 294). According to Soejana et al. (2008: 651), the foreign Christian missionaries first entered Indramayu in 1863. Missionaries such as D.J. van der Linden (1837–85) went there to strengthen the small Chinese Christian community that had been established there as a result of the first convert to Christianity in Indramayu, Ang Boeng Swi (1810–64) (End, 2006: 118). In 1858, about 14 of Ang Boeng Swi’s relatives were baptized with him (Coppel, 2002: 296). The conversion of Indonesian Chinese to Christianity was of ‘primary importance’ to the formation of Chinese congregations in areas such as Indramayu (Giok, 1952: 121). This led to the establishment of the first Chinese Indonesian church north of Cirebon (Schumann, 2010: 67). Between 1858 and 1899, 199 people were baptized (Soejana, 1997: 291). Most of these people, however, were Chinese Indonesians, and there were reports of conflict between Chinese Indonesians and non-Chinese members that impeded the formation of a united congregation. A school was also opened in the area, but most of the students who attended were Chinese Indonesians.
According to Coppel, it was common for missionaries to have greater success among the Chinese in Indonesia, partly because they made up a large percentage of the children who attended Christian schools. He notes:
In many of the mission posts, the missionaries found that it was the Chinese who were attracted particularly to the schools. In some places, for example Indramayu … the members of the parish were almost all Chinese.
(Coppel, 2002: 295)
The greater acceptance Christianity found in Indonesian Chinese circles is also highlighted by Pow Boen Giok, a Chinese convert to Christianity who was born in Indramayu. Giok notes that it was:
practically impossible for missionaries … to preach the gospel directly to the autochthonous population ie Sundanese and Javanese, without first coming into contact with the Chinese population.
(Giok, 1952: 121)
Indramayu was considered to be a difficult posting for missionaries because of the resistance of the Sundanese, the majority Muslim ethnic group in the area, to missionary efforts (End, 2006: 511). There were reports of challenges winning new converts because people were afraid they would lose their job if they became Christian, due to the way new converts were often ostracized from the community. As a result, some converts later returned to Islam due to the negative reactions their conversions generated (Soejana, 1997: 451). In light of this history, the majority of Christians in Indramayu today are Chinese (or at least non-Sundanese) and this has led to the perception among Islamists that Christians in the area are ‘foreigners.’ Pastor Supriyatno, of the Pasundan Christian Church and former general chairperson of PGI West Java, suspected that part of the issue in the Indramayu Religious Education Case was racial discrimination because Dr Rebekka, the pastor of the church, is a Chinese Indonesian.48
Converts to Christianity were a cause of concern for Muslims in the community. The first convert was Aung Boe Swie, and his conversion was followed by others such as Gouw Kho (Jakarta, 1868), Khouw Tek San (Purbolinggo, 1866) and Oei Soei Tiong (1898) (Hartono, 1974: 87). Another example of a conversion to Christianity in Indramayu is Kartawidjaja, an elite bureaucrat.49 His baptism in 1899 came as a shock and was considered by some to be apostasy (Ricklefs, 2007: 120). Ricklefs succinctly highlights that ‘Kartawidjaja’s story thus reflected the profound social and personal conflicts that sometimes attended conversions’ (Ricklefs, 2007: 120), and continues to surround high-profile cases of conversion today.
One of the practices that developed among Christian mission organizations in the early 1900s was to purchase land and establish Christian settlements, particularly because many of the converts to Christianity were poor. One such settlement was established in Haurgeulis-Rehoboth in 1912 (End, 2006: 445–48). In 1908, the Dutch Mission Society (Nederlandsche Zendings Vereeniging, NZV) established a hospital in Indramayu (End, 2006: 18). The NZV also provided practical assistance to the community in times of crisis, such as during the famine that hit Indramayu in 1903 (End, 2006: 21, 321).
Aside from being targeted by Christian missionaries, in the early 1950s Indramayu was a target for Islamization by Darul Islam, a radical Islamic movement led by Kartosoewirjo (Jubilee Campaign, 8 July 2005). By the 1960s, Darul Islam and its attempts to make Indonesia an Islamic State had largely been suppressed by the central government (as explained in Chapter 3), although not entirely. After Kartosoewirjo was killed in 1962, covert veterans of Darul Islam persisted and in 1970 the group broke into two branches (Wasis, 2003: 185). The leader of one of the branches was jailed in 1980, and he delegated his authority to Abu Toto (also known as Panji Gumilang). He led Darul Islam’s 9th Regional Command (known as KW9), which included West Java and Jakarta, and was believed to have about 50,000 followers (Fealy, 2007: 69). Under his leadership, the Al-Zaytun boarding school was constructed in Haurgeulis, Indramayu (Wasis, 2003: 187). It received financial support from former President Habibie and from Soeharto (Pringle, 2010: 128–29), and one of the buildings is named in honor of Soeharto.
The Al-Zaytun Islamic boarding school is the largest Islamic madrasah in Southeast Asia (Washington Times, 29 August 2005). It accommodates 7,000 students, both male and female, and has a building campaign to accommodate 18,000 more. It is also building a six-storey mosque. The point of concern, however, is that Al-Zaytun has suspected links to radical movements such as Darul Islam and Negara Islam Indonesia (NII) (Fealy, 2004: 112). In early 2000, Al-Zaytun was accused of deviating from the teachings of Islam, although investigations by the Ministry of Religion found no evidence of this (Hefner, 2009: 104). In 2002, the police made a number of arrests of suspected members of NII, from South Bandung, North Lampung and West Java. Those from West Java had alleged connections to Al-Zaytun boarding school (Jakarta Post, 9 April 2002; 11 April 2002).
While there is no direct link between Al-Zaytun and Darul Islam in the Indramayu Religious Education Case, radical Islamists, many from outside the area, demonstrated during the court proceedings. They called for jihad (holy war) and mati syahid (religious martyrdom) if the women were not convicted. The court hearings were accompanied by chants of Allahu Akbar (God is great) and demands that the women be hanged. Anonymous death threats were also made towards other pastors and Christian workers in the area at this time (District Court, 2005d: 2). The Defense argued that the evidence given at trial may have been influenced by the intense intimidation, threats and bribery from radical Islamic groups. It is possible that the presence and pressure exerted by radical Islamists at the trial negatively influenced the evidence given by the witnesses, as well as the decision of the court.
Such opposition and tension between Muslims and Christians in Indramayu has occurred previously on several occasions. In 1967, a church in Indramayu was burnt down because of accusations a Christian had insulted Islam.50 There have also been tensions caused by Christians using houses as churches.51 Aside from the pressure of radical Islamic groups at court trials and incidents over churches, there have also been concerted campaigns to introduce aspects of Islamic law in local regulations. These do not have the status of regional regulations – that is, they have not been passed by the local legislature, but are rather instructions or decisions issued by the regent. Nevertheless, it is still a symbol of support for hardline Islam in the area. There is also a connection between religious-inspired regulations and concerns over issues of immorality, partly because of the history of prostitution in the area. Religious regulations are perceived to be necessary to address the moral crisis in Indramayu – that is, the prostitution industry and the export of prostitutes.52 Indramayu is notorious as a center for prostitution in Java, servicing brothel complexes in Jakarta and elsewhere, and trafficking women overseas (Soedirham and Puspita, 2003; Jakarta Post, 26 October 2005).
