This perceived moral crisis has provided some of the motivation for the creation of a number of regulations on aspects of Islamic law. Between 2001 and 2009, there were over 50 regulations issued in various forms, including in the form of a Letter, a Circular, an Appeal, an Instruction or a Decision of the Regent. These regulations address issues such as prohibiting alcohol and pornography, requiring Muslim clothing in schools, Qur’anic tests in schools, zakat (giving alms) and compulsory reading of the Qur’an for civil servants. The emphasis on religious education is particularly strong, with over 20 regulations addressing the obligation for all Muslim children to receive Islamic education. There is also a decision concerning spiritual counselors for Muslims in hospitals. These regulations – on education and spiritual care in hospitals – are evidence of a concern for the ‘vulnerable’ such as children or the sick. It may also be a reaction to the controversy caused by the Indramayu Religious Education Case, which raised doubts about the intention of those who provided religious education and medical services.
Teachers and inter-religious relations
There are numerous versions of what actually happened in this case, depending on the person asked. One of the key issues was the intent of the teachers and the broader question of the extent to which the children of Muslim parents are allowed to learn about the teachings of another religion or participate in an event of another religion.
In terms of the nature and purpose of the program, it was generally agreed by both parties that the program included learning Christian songs; coloring in pictures of people such as Abraham, Noah, Lazarus and Zachaeus (described by the Prosecution as ‘Christians,’ although they are figures common to the Abrahamic faiths); reading stories from the Bible about Jesus; and learning Christian prayers. The Prosecution and the MUI investigator Muhammad Nursadat called this program ‘Minggu Ceria Kristenisasi,’ the ‘Happy Sunday Christianization program.’ The insertion of the term ‘Christianization’ into the name of the program was an attempt to label the program with negative connotations and fears that are inherent in the term ‘Christianization’ in Indonesia (District Court, 2005f: 5).
The Defense claimed that this was not the actual name used by the church and that ‘Christianization’ was not the intention or agenda of the teachers or the program (District Court, 2005f: 1). The Defense pointed out that this program was initiated at the request of Christian parents and the local primary school so that Christian students could aim to achieve a good score for religious studies at school (District Court, 2005b: 7, 8). The Defense contended that although the program was aimed at Christian students, there was no legal prohibition in Indonesia on Muslim students attending a Christian program.
The court agreed with the Prosecution that the program should only be for Christian children, and non-Christian children should not be allowed to participate in the program (p.5). This goes further than the Education Law, and interprets the segregation policy as a prohibition on a child learning about another religion. The court accepted the evidence of the MUI investigator, although it did not go as far as to call the program a ‘Christianization’ program (p.12).
This decision is similar to the fatwa of MUI that declared it haram for Muslims to attend Christmas celebrations. It is common in Indonesia for Muslims to invite Christians to celebrations such as buka puasa, the meal to break the fast during Ramadan. Similarly, Christians often invite Muslims to Christmas celebrations at the workplace, at school or in the neighborhood (Mudzhar, 1996: 233). The controversy over Christmas celebrations first arose in the 1980s. In March 1981, MUI issued a fatwa that opened a broader debate on attendance at religious celebrations by those who profess another religion (Ministry of Religion, 2003: 235–42).53 The fatwa stated that it is haram for a Muslim to attend a Christmas celebration because these celebrations are Christian religious rituals. It justified this position by arguing that while Muslims may cooperate with non-Muslims for community or humanitarian causes (Q 49:13, 31:15, 60:8), attendance at such celebrations could jeopardize the faith of a Muslim. This was followed by several fatawa from Muhammadiyah that reinforced the MUI fatwa by declaring that Muslims should not say ‘Merry Christmas’ to Christians (Muhammadiyah, 1991: 209–10; Muhammadiyah, 2003: 276).
The fatwa of MUI did not go down well with the government because of its potential to destabilize inter-religious relations, and the government specifically instructed MUI to retract the fatwa (Mudzhar, 1996: 237). The Minister of Religion supported MUI, arguing that it was necessary for the sake of national unity (Ministry of Religion, 2008a: 202, 243–46). He issued a further Letter of Circulation in September 1981 to clarify that it was permissible for a government official, as part of his or her duties, to be present at an event of another religion (Ministry of Religion, 2008a: 235). This led the government Inter-religious Harmony Committee to hold a meeting to discuss the issue of religious ceremonies and holy days (Sairin, 1996a: 518–21). The controversy was so intense that the chairperson of MUI, Hamka, resigned at the end of the year not because of ill-health, as reported, but because of the disagreement between the government and the Ministry of Religion (Mudzhar, 1996: 237–38). Opinions on the Christmas fatwa today differ, although fatawa are not legally binding on Muslims in Indonesia.
Rights of parents
The issue of the rights of parents in relation to the religious education of their children was central in the case. The Prosecution claimed that the children did not have the consent of their parents to attend the program. They relied on the evidence of the grandmother of two students who claimed that she had not given her permission (p.5). The credibility of this evidence was questioned by the Defense, who pointed out that this same grandmother had attended the Taman Mini Indah Indonesia (Beautiful Indonesia Miniature Park) outing, evidenced by photographs presented at the trial, and was clearly aware that her grandchildren had been attending the program. The Defense also argued that Pangesti had visited the grandmothers’ house to ask her permission that the children attend (District Court, 2005f: 7). The Prosecution responded by claiming that several other children did not have the permission of their parents, although this argument was made in the absence of any evidence or testimony from these parents. In the court decision, there was no discussion of what would have amounted to consent, whether oral or written consent was required, or whether consent needed to be obtained from one or both parents/guardians. Despite these contradictions and the absence of evidence, the court accepted the Prosecution’s argument that consent had not been obtained from the parents and that consent should have been obtained (District Court, 2005b: 5).
This case was complicated by the fact that, according to Rebekka, some of the ‘Muslim’ children who attended the program were actually children of mixed marriages – that is, one Muslim parent and one Christian parent.54 The controversial issue of mixed marriages, and the religion of children from those marriages, has long been a subject of intense debate in Indonesia.55 An initial Draft Law on Marriage put forward in 1973 proposed to allow inter-religious marriages. The removal of this provision before the passage of the Marriage Law in 1974 was widely perceived as a concession to Muslims who strongly disagreed with this provision. As it currently stands, Law 1/1974 on Marriage neither specifically prohibits nor permits inter-religious marriages.56 The status or permissibility of inter-religious marriages in Indonesia therefore remains unclear. This is despite the fact that the traditional Islamic position is that a Muslim man may take a Muslim, Christian or Jew as a wife, although a Muslim woman may only marry a Muslim man (Q 5:5).
A number of fatawa issued by Islamic religious leaders since then have attempted to impose a different interpretation of inter-religious marriages in Indonesia. In June 1980, a fatwa issued by MUI expressly forbade Muslims from marrying Christians.57 It made two points: that marriage between a Muslim woman and a non-Muslim man is haram; and that a Muslim man is forbidden (diharamkan) from marrying a non-Muslim woman. MUI reissued this fatwa in 2005 (MUI, 2005: 35–44). Muhammadiyah (2003: 205–6) has also issued a fatwa that forbids a Muslim from marrying a person who has no religion. It also forbade a Muslim woman from marrying a Christian man, although it allows a Muslim man to marry a Christian woman. This issue was not discussed in court.
Evidence of child conversion
Another disputed issue was whether a child has the right to convert. It also raised the issue of what amounts to conversion to Christianity, whether the Muslim children had actually converted, and whether it was necessary for the children to have converted for the elements of article 86 to be satisfied. The head of the local MUI, Mundzir, initially claimed that one Muslim student had converted to Catholicism. The validity of his evidence was quickly countered by the Defense, who pointed out that this was not possible through this program because the teachers’ church was Protestant, not Catholic (District Court, 2005f: 1). An 11-year-old student gave evidence that she was ordered to convert, but she had forgotten how or when this occurred (District Court, 2005b: 23). In the opinion of expert Muslim witness Abdul Wachid Syakuri, however, actual conversion was not necessary, contrary to the wording of article 86. The mere involvement of Muslim children in the Happy Sunday program meant they had implicitly converted. He was quoted as stating that ‘Muslim children who are invited to participate in a Happy Sunday program and sing Christian songs and pray Christian prayers are apostates [murtad]’ (p.16).
On the other hand, the leader of the Protestant Division of the Ministry of Religion of West Java, John Nainggolan, opined that ‘Christian children’ are children who have been baptized and that mere participation in a Happy Sunday program did not make a child Christian. Building on this, the Defense argued that the teachers had not baptized any of the children. This was not disputed by the prosecution (p.8). The court accepted the evidence of John Nainggolan that baptism was a sign that a child was a Christian and that the Muslim children had not been baptized. The court found, however, that article 86 did not require a child to have converted, despite the wording of the article, but merely that there had been attempts to convert a child (p.21). This interpretation is significant, as it sets a low threshold for breach of this provision.
Another issue in dispute was the influence that the program had on the behavior of the Muslim children. The Prosecution argued that some of the children had skipped boy scouts (Pramuka) on Sunday mornings to go to the program, while other children had become more disobedient at home after they attended the program (p.10). The Defense, however, reasoned that the students were very diligent and obedient children at the Happy Sunday Program (District Court, 2005b: 5). They also argued that the teachers had never forbidden the children to go to the mosque, wear the jilbab (headscarf) or read the Qur’an (District Court, 2005f: 7). The court accepted that the children were good students, but focused more on the behavior of the teachers who had allowed Muslim children to attend the Christian program (District Court, 2005b: 5). Overall, the court was inclined to accept the Prosecution’s version of the facts without question, which raises the issue of whether radical Islamic groups influenced the verdict, as discussed above. The court responded to issues of evidence and the credibility of witnesses in a similar fashion. This highlights the lack of capacity of the District Courts to make decisions independent of the influence of Islamist religious leaders and radical Islamic groups.
The issue of whether children can convert was compounded by the fact that even if a child gave evidence that they had converted, the evidence was not given under oath and so raised issues of admissibility. The rules of evidence in a criminal trial are contained in Chapter 16 of the Criminal Code.58 To determine whether a crime took place, and whether the accused committed the crime, the court’s decision must be based on at least two pieces of ‘legal evidence’ (art. 183). ‘Legal evidence’ can include the testimony of a witness, expert evidence, a letter, a statement of the defendant, or circumstantial evidence (art. 184–88). In the Indramayu Religious Education Case, 15 witnesses gave evidence. The witnesses included the Head of the local MUI, the principal of the primary school, a teacher from the primary school, some of the Muslim children who attended the Happy Sunday program, and some of the parents of the Muslim children. No Christian students or parents gave evidence. The only Christian witness called was from the Ministry of Religion.
The issue of the admissibility of evidence was raised by the Defense, who pointed to article 171 of the Criminal Code, which states that:
People who may be examined to give information without swearing an oath are:
a)
Children who are under 15 years old and are not yet married;
b)
People who suffer from amnesia or a mental illness, but who occasionally have a good memory.
On this basis, the Defense argued that the evidence given by the children, who were all under 15 years old, was a mere ‘guide’ and needed to be verified by other witnesses (District Court, 2005d: 12). The second, related issue was the weight that should be given to the evidence of adult witnesses. The Defense argued that the majority of the evidence given by the witnesses was secondhand information, or hearsay (p.11). They cited article 1(27) of the Criminal Code, which states that:
Witness evidence is evidence in a criminal case comprising information from the witness concerning a criminal event that he or she heard him or herself, saw him or herself and experienced him or herself together with the basis for acquiring knowledge of that information.
This provision is understood as a prohibition on hearsay. The Defense relied on this article to argue that most of the evidence given by the witnesses (excluding the children) was second-hand because they did not see, hear or experience the Happy Sunday program for themselves. Further, the Defense suggested that the evidence given by three of the witnesses was inadmissible because of their failure to attend the trial to give evidence (District Court, 2005d: 12).
The court ruled that the evidence of six of the witnesses, namely the MUI leader, the school principal, one of the parents, the head of the neighborhood, a teacher and the MUI investigator, was testimonium de auditu, or hearsay. The court further decided that the evidence given by four children ‘must only be considered as informative’ (District Court, 2005b: 23). This left the evidence of two experts and the written testimonies of three children who had not given evidence at the trial. According to article 185(7) of the Criminal Code, evidence not given under oath cannot be used on its own but must be supported by other evidence given under oath. Therefore, the majority of the evidence led by the Prosecution was inadmissible or weak at best.
Despite the inconsistencies in evidence and the need to weigh carefully the admissibility of evidence against the established rules of admissibility, the court nonetheless found that article 86 had been breached. This calls into question the ability of the District Court to decide cases of religious sensitivity in a fair and impartial manner.
Interpretation of Islamic hadith
Another issue in the case was the way in which the court referred to hadith, sayings of the Prophet, despite the fact that it is not a source of law in Indonesia. The Prosecution argued that there were three elements that had to be satisfied to find a breach of article 86 (District Court, 2005e: 26–28). These were:
1  Any person;
2  who deliberately uses deceit, a series of lies or entices a child to choose another religion not of his or her own will;
3  despite it being known or suspected that the child is not mature and is not yet responsible according to the religion he/she follows.
The first element is straightforward. In relation to the second element, the Prosecution relied on the evidence of expert witness Abdul Wachid Syakuri, from the Ministry of Religion. Syakuri argued that it is forbidden to proselytize people who already have a religion, according to Decision of the Minister of Religion and Minister of Home Affairs 1/1979 on Guidelines for the Propagation of Religion and Overseas Donations to Religious Organizations (District Court, 2005b: 15). As quoted in Chapter 2, article 4 states that religious missionary activities cannot be aimed at a person or group of people who already have a religion, as stated on their identity card. Almost every Indonesian has a ‘religion’ on their identity card.
The Prosecution argued that the teachers had breached article 4(a) by giving the children small gifts and snacks as ‘bribes.’ The prosecution argued that because the two Muslim children who first attended the program were given such gifts, this attracted other Muslim children to the program (p.5). They also claimed that the teachers had breached article 4(b) by giving one of the children a Bible, although it was disputed whether the child had asked for it.
The Defense argued that the most important element was intention (District Court, 2005d: 12–14). They argued that the program was primarily for the spiritual benefit of Christian children and to help them achieve a good school report. They argued that the gifts given to the children were not evidence in themselves of enticement or deception, but rather a reward for good behavior (District Court, 2005d: 1). They emphasized that outings were just for fun and that there had been no attempts to baptize children as this was not the aim of the program.
Whether requiring the children to wear blue t-shirts with the church logo and a picture of a cross on the front and the name of the church printed underneath constituted part of the attempts to convert the children was debated. There were three outings run by the program: to Taman Mini, to the zoo and to a swimming pool. The Defense argued that the t-shirts were simply for identification purposes and that it was common practice for groups on outings.59 The Prosecution argued that requiring a Muslim child to wear such a uniform was part of the attempt at ‘Christianization’ (p.6). The court considered the wearing of Christian uniforms as part of the overall program of Christian activities, which was intended only for Christians (p.22).
The Defense also argued that the children still followed Islam by participating in sholat (daily prayers) and going to the mosque (District Court, 2005d: 14). They therefore asserted that the elements of article 86 had not been satisfied. The court decision does not explain the reasoning for the conclusion. It merely appears to have accepted the Prosecution’s interpretation of article 86 and found that the women had breached this section by allowing Muslim children to attend the Christian education program without the consent of their parents (District Court, 2005b: 19–22).
Crucial to the court’s finding of a breach of article 86 was the use and reliance on hadith (sayings of the Prophet) during the trial. In this case, the Prosecution quoted, interpreted and applied the following the well-known hadith:
All children are born into a religious belief (according to the Islamic faith), so it is the responsibility of their parents to bring them up (the child) Jew (Yahudi), Christian (Nasrani), Zoroastrian (Majusi) (worshipping fire) …
(Al-Bukhari; District Court, 2005e: 2, 4)
This was interpreted to mean that a child must be brought up in the religion of their parents and cannot change religion without the permission of their parents. In response, the Defense acknowledged the hadith but argued that the children had not converted, and had the permission of their parents to attend the program (District Court, 2005f: 19). There is no mention in any of the court documents that the Defense opposed the use of hadith or questioned the validity of its use in the secular District Court. In the decision of the court, this hadith was specifically relied on in its findings that a child needs the consent of his or her parents to convert. Hadith, however, are not a source of law recognized by the state in Indonesia. The court also found that the Muslim children had been invited to convert to Christianity by following the Happy Sunday program (District Court, 2005b: 6).
The court found the women guilty of deceiving Muslim children to change their religion and handed down the punishment suggested by the Prosecution: three years’ jail and a fine of Rp 1,500,000 (approximately A $189). Although the defendants appealed to the Bandung High Court and the Supreme Court, both courts rejected the applications, but failed to give reasons for their decisions (High District Court, 2005; Supreme Court, 2006b).
Judicial review of the Child Protection Law
While the women were still in prison, the constitutional validity of article 86 of the Child Protection Law was challenged in the Constitutional Court. The Constitutional Court is provided for in article 24C of the third amendment to the Indonesian Constitution.60 In 2003, it was established by Law 24/2003 on the Constitutional Court. The Constitutional Court is the only court in Indonesia that has the power to receive applications for judicial review of laws (undang-undang) enacted by the legislature that are considered to be unconstitutional (arts 3A and 10(1)(A)). The establishment of the Constitutional Court is significant because in the past Soeharto ensured that the courts did not have the power to exercise judicial review, a crucial check on the power of the executive.61 The one limitation on the power of the Constitutional Court is that its decisions are prospective, so they can only be applied after the judgment is handed down.62
In January 2006, a request for judicial review was filed with the Constitutional Court by Reverend Ruyandi Hutasoit, who is a colleague of the three women and the leader of the Prosperous Peace Party (Partai Damai Sejahtera, PDS), the largest Christian political party in Indonesia.63 In his request for review, the applicant argued that article 86 of the Child Protection Law violated Indonesia’s constitutional provisions protecting religious freedom.64 The applicant also cited the Indramayu Religious Education Case as an example of unlawful restrictions on religious freedom.
In the brief 12-page decision of the Constitutional Court, the judiciary first considered the authority of the court to hear the case (Constitutional Court, 2005: 6), and then the legal standing of the applicant (p.6–9). Ruyadi argued that as a religious preacher and teacher, his right to religious freedom under the Constitution was compromised by the Child Protection Law. The court held that Hutasoit lacked standing to challenge the Child Protection Law under article 56(1) of Law 24/2003 on the Constitutional Court because his rights had not been impaired in any way. As a result, the court declined to consider the case any further, avoiding the substantive issues in this case.
Conclusion
The Indramayu Religious Education Case is the first and only time in Indonesia that religious education teachers have been convicted for the criminal offence of deceiving a child to change religion. It illustrates both the influence of democracy, which opened up the possibility of law reform, and the impact of decentralization, which politicizes religion at the local level and sees local courts defer to religious leaders in cases involving religious matters.
Through the Education Law, the religious education policy has been widened since 1998 to mandate all schools, whether public or private, to provide religious education according to the religion of the student. The government has addressed the perceived need of the Muslim electorate to limit proselytization and mitigate the ‘risk’ of conversion to Christianity or apostasy. Therefore, the religious education provisions of the Education Law primarily reflect concerns that Christian schools are stretching the boundaries of ‘acceptable’ proselytization by requiring Muslim students to attend Christian religious education, or simply by failing to providing Islam religious education for these students. Nevertheless, a range of new approaches to religious education are emerging at the local level which challenge the government’s current position on segregated religious education by giving children the opportunity to learn not only about their own religion but also the religion of others, in the context of human rights and morality.
The state has also responded to Muslim concerns over proselytization through the inclusion in the Child Protection Law of an offence of deceiving a child to change their religion. This may be an important principle to guide adoption agencies and protect orphans because of the implications of religious identity in Indonesia. The Indramayu Religious Education Case, however, demonstrates that the Child Protection Law has been interpreted very broadly, such that there is no requirement that a person intended to convert a child, or that a child did convert. Aside from the courts, this case demonstrates the active role played by Islamic leaders from MUI, who took the initiative to investigate the incident and report it to the police. The case against the teachers was then vocally supported at trial by the intimidating presence of radical Islamists.
The District Court decided that if a child is allowed to attend a religious education class of another religion this constitutes the offence of deceiving a child to change his or her religion. This decision suggests that the District Court is inclined to defer to Islamic religious leaders and their interpretation of national law in light of hadith, particularly when under pressure from radical Islamists. The District Courts play a crucial role at first instance in determining such religious disputes and it is imperative that the courts have the capacity to make decisions in an impartial manner, independent of religious leaders.
The decision at first instance was supported by the Bandung High Court and the Supreme Court on appeal, although little reasoning was provided for doing so. The Constitutional Court has also since rejected an application for judicial review of article 86 of the Child Protection Law. Reforms to the law and subsequent court cases since 1998 on the issues of religious education and child protection have therefore validated and further entrenched stereotypes and suspicions of Christian activities in Indonesia. This is because Christian schools are perceived by Muslims as a deceptive means of proselytizing Muslim children, seeking converts and therefore threatening the future of the Muslim community. While this is an exceptional case, it demonstrates the potential for national laws to be manipulated at the local level.

5  Conflict over sacred sites
A crucial part of state-religion relations is whether, and to what extent, a state regulates the buildings and property of religious communities. Places of worship1 of religious minorities are a highly politicized issue in Indonesia and remain a matter of significant concern ten years into the reformasi (reform) era. One of the main points of contention between Muslims and Christians in Indonesia has been applications for permits for a place of worship. This chapter explores the Depok Church Permit Case to illustrate the concerns that the construction of a church amounts to or facilitates attempts to convert Muslims. The construction of a church may be perceived as a symbol of the growth of Christianity and an intention to ‘Christianize’ the area. A church may therefore be perceived as a threat to a majority-Muslim area. This has led to claims that building a place of worship is a form of aggressive Christian proselytization. Most Christians, on the other hand, argue that a church building is necessary as a central place to worship and practice their religion.
This chapter examines the violent responses of radical Islamists from sporadic, large-scale attacks to local but more persistent campaigns against churches, including petitioning local governments to cancel permits. Opposition from these groups influenced the agenda for law reform, which implemented a more bureaucratic process to obtain a permit. An analysis of the new regulation highlights some of the key problems that have emerged in practice, and identifies common underlying reasons for the sustained opposition to some churches. Finally, I also explore the reactions of Christian congregations and how attempts to assert democratic rights through legal processes have largely failed, or victories in court remained hollow. This demonstrates that court decisions may have little practical effect in light of threats of violence from radical Islamist groups and unwillingness on the part of law enforcement agencies and the local government to enforce the court decision. Permit disputes therefore highlight the acute limitations of judicial mechanisms of review, in the absence of local political will to respect the authority of the courts and the rights of religious minorities.
Depok Church Permit Case
In May 2009, the Protestant Christian Batak Congregation (Huria Kristen Batak Protestan, HKBP) of Depok became one of the first Christian congregations in Indonesia to lodge an application with the Administrative Court (Pengadilan Tata Usaha Negara, PTUN) in Bandung in relation to its permit to build a church. It sought to challenge a decision issued by the mayor of Depok to cancel the permit for the partly built church.
The history of this permit dispute stretches back ten years. In June 1998, the congregation was granted a permit by the mayor of Bogor, Edi Yoso. Four months later, in October 1998, the construction of the church commenced. Between 1998 and 2000, however, the Muslim Solidarity Forum2 led by Syamsi Nasution opposed the construction of the church.
On 9 May 1999, an Islamic prayer ceremony, known as istighotsah, was held to protest against the building of the church. Istighotsah is a ceremony where Muslims gather together to pray for a particular situation and may be part of a demonstration or protest. According to Nahdlatul Ulama (NU), istighotsah means pertolongan or help and is likened to tawassul, that is, intercession or drawing near to God (Agil and Fattah, 2008: 3, 8).3 This event marked the dispute as religious, as opponents rallied in the name of Islam. In addition to the istighotsah, the Muslim Solidarity Forum also lobbied the local government directly, demanding that construction cease (FSUM, 2009a: 4).
As a result, on 8 July 2000, the then mayor of Depok, Badrul Kamal, issued a recommendation that construction halt temporarily until a further decision of the government. This effectively put all construction works on hold. Several years later, in 2007, the congregation wrote to the mayor of Depok, Nur Mahmudi Isma’il, on three separate occasions, asking for clarification of the status of its permit and for permission to continue building. Despite repeated attempts to contact the mayor, he did not respond, so the congregation recommenced building. The Muslim Solidarity Forum began actively to oppose the building of the church once again, this time under the leadership of Budi Waluyo of the local Raya Cinere Mosque. The congregation only had time to complete the foundations and the first level of the building before opposition from Muslim groups began to intensify. On 26 October 2008, this culminated in a second istighotsah ceremony held in protest against the building of the church, attended by people from six local mosques and some residents from the local neighborhood (Administrative Court, 2009a: 36). After the Muslim Solidarity Forum trespassed on the land to erect a banner in protest against the construction of the church, the church lodged a complaint with the police. On 27 March 2009, with no prior warning to the congregation, Mayor Nur Mahmudi Isma’il (who is from the Islamic-based Prosperous Justice Party) issued a decision to cancel the original permit.
This case is highly symbolic because this church is the largest Protestant denomination under the national Indonesian Communion of Churches (Persekutuan Gereja-gereja Indonesia, PGI). The Batak are also the fifth largest ethnic group in Indonesia, with a population of over 6 million in 2000 (Suryadinata et al., 2003: 48–52). Of these, an estimated 3.5 million are members of the Batak Protestant Church (WCC, 2006; Nadeak et al., 1995: 5), while many others belong to other Protestant denominations. This case is also significant because it is one of the first court actions taken by a church against a government authority in relation to a dispute over a church permit in Indonesia.
The mayor’s decision was based on submissions from various government departments and community groups, such as the Muslim Solidarity Forum, the local branch of the Ministry of Religion (Ministry of Religion, 2008c) and the Inter-religious Harmony Forum (Forum Kerukunan Umat Beragama; see FKUB, 2008). The latter is particularly important because the Forum is one of the key reforms in the process for obtaining a permit to build a place of worship introduced by the Joint Regulation of the Minister of Religion and the Minister of Home Affairs 8 & 9/2006 on Places of Worship.4 This Forum, which exists at the provincial and city/regency level, is responsible for processing applications for permits to build a place of worship, facilitating dialogue between religions, and assisting in the prevention and resolution of disputes. There was disagreement within the Inter-religious Harmony Forum, as the Protestant representative believed that the Forum’s recommendation had been misinterpreted and used by the mayor to legitimize his decision.5
On 6 May 2009, represented by current national leader, Bishop Bonar Napitupulu,6 and the church pastor, Bishop Mori Sihombing, the church filed a case in the Administrative Court of Bandung challenging the decision of the mayor to cancel the building permit. During the court hearings, demonstrations demanding the closure of the church were held by people wearing clothes bearing the words ‘Front Pembela Islam’ (Islamic Defenders Front, FPI) took place outside the court. While the Muslim Solidarity Forum denied links to FPI, members of FPI had attended a meeting at a local mosque in 2007 and agreed to support the Forum as coordinators of the opposition movement against the church.7
On 29 October 2009, the Administrative Court held that the church had obtained its permit legally under Joint Decree 1/1969 on Places of Worship,8 and that the building also fulfilled the requirements of national and local building laws. The court found that the church was not misusing the permit, which is the only legal ground on which a mayor could legitimately cancel a permit. On this basis, the court ruled in favor of the church.
The mayor appealed to the Jakarta High Administrative Court and lost, and then to the Supreme Court, which held that it did not have the jurisdiction to hear the case.9 In October 2010, the building of the church recommenced, but cautiously, due to continued threats of attack. This is because the church still experienced opposition at this time, evident from banners that were erected outside the church property. For example, a banner outside the church property in November 2010 read:
Since 1982, the people of Cinere and the surrounding community have rejected HKBP. The Decision and Letter of the Bandung Administrative Court and the Jakarta High Administrative Court offends the Muslim Community of Cinere. Religious matters are not local matters but national and global. Legal authorities and the government must take firm legal action before the community takes action.
The banner carried with it the threat that if the government did not act to stop the construction of the church, ‘the community,’ presumably sympathizers of the Muslim Solidarity Forum, would take the law into their own hands.
One of the key issues at the heart of the Depok Church Permit Case is the issue of ‘Christianization,’ or Christian proselytization. The Muslim Solidarity Forum suspected that the construction of the church was a means of ‘Christianizing’ the area. The leader of the Muslim Solidarity Forum, Budi, stated in a letter that ‘this is obviously an effort at Christianization’ (FSUM, 2009a: app. 24). Further, in September 2008, at a meeting with the local government, the leader of the local Indonesian Ulama Council (MUI) was quoted as saying that ‘the Muslim community is afraid of this Christian [Nasrani] movement.’ Many informants, both Muslim and Christian, denied these claims, because the church is generally regarded as catering for the majority-Christian Batak community, and therefore not actively seeking converts from the non-Batak community. There is also an obvious language barrier if non-Batak people attend the church because services are primarily conducted in a Batak dialect (rather than one of the languages spoken by the majority of Muslims in the area – Indonesian, Javanese or Sundanese). Nevertheless, the motives of the church remain contested, and threats continue to be made against the church.
Such fears of ‘Christianization’ fuel motivations behind the increasing number of attacks by radical Islamic groups on churches since the late 1960s. A large proportion of these attacks have occurred in the province of West Java primarily against Protestant, Pentecostal and Evangelical (rather than Catholic) churches.10 There has also been opposition to the construction of mosques in majority-Christian areas, as well as attacks on mosques of groups considered to be ‘deviant,’ such as Ahmadiyah (see Crouch, 2009a). In addition to acts of violence and intimidation campaigns, some Islamists are responding to the perceived threat by demanding tighter regulation of the permit process. In 2006, this culminated in the introduction of national Joint Regulation 8 & 9/2006 on Places of Worship. Implementation at the regional level has been mixed, however, as the transition to decentralization has seen local authorities manipulate conflict over churches as opportunities for political gain in the highly competitive political atmosphere. In order to appear responsive to the demands of the Islamic constituency, some local government leaders have cancelled existing church permits, particularly in West Java, prompting churches to bring cases to court.
Opposition to churches
The destruction or forced closure of churches in Indonesia dates back to the establishment of churches by Christian missionaries in the 1600s (Aritonang, 2004). Some of the first churches built in Java by Indonesian Christians were later burnt down (Ricklefs, 2008b: 117). These churches were the result of the work of Kiai Sadrach, a convert to Christianity who combined Protestantism with Javanese culture in a manner often unacceptable to European missionaries.11 Sadrach’s followers met with such opposition that between 1800 and 1884, all the churches they had built were burnt down. Although such incidents are evidence of attacks on churches prior to the 1900s, the more common starting point for accounts of attacks on churches in Indonesia is the 1960s.12
Violent reactions from radical Islamists to perceived efforts of Christian proselytization via church building, as evident in the Melauboh Affair and the Makassar Affair (see Chapter 2), led to the introduction of the Joint Decree on permits in 1969. Administered by the Ministry of Religion, the Joint Decree gave the mayor or regent the responsibility to oversee and monitor religious activities to ensure religious communities did not disturb public peace and order (art. 2). To obtain a building permit, a religious community was required to obtain permission from the mayor or regent. In making his or her decision, the mayor/regent would consider the opinion of the Ministry, the plans of the proposal and ‘local conditions,’ which could include consultation with religious leaders (art. 4). Rather than providing clear guidelines for those who wished to apply for a permit to build a place of worship, the Joint Decree left the application process to the discretion of local authorities, who could consult with religious leaders if desired. It was also silent on the cost or fee to obtain a permit, which was determined by local authorities. The Joint Decree was deliberately vague, with only six brief provisions, and designed to ensure the government maintained unrestricted power to control applications for the construction of places of worship. This Joint Decree was a convenient tool of political control for the New Order government, while also pandering to the insistence of Islamists that churches must be controlled through a permit application process.
The Joint Decree applied to all religions, although it was not always followed. Mosques have been built without official permission or, if they do obtain a permit, the process may be easier in Muslim-majority provinces, while in Christian-majority areas the process is easier for churches. Muslim minorities also face challenges to build a mosque in the Hindu-majority province of Bali, the Christian-majority province of Papua, or the Christian-majority province of East Nusa Tenggara. The Joint Decree therefore affected all religious minorities, depending on the context. In particular, Christians faced increasing challenges in West Java as government authorities were reluctant to grant permits for fear of a negative reaction from the Muslim constituency, and often never responded to applications for permits.
Despite these difficulties, there was still a significant increase in the number of places of worship in Indonesia under the New Order, including churches. In 1974, there were 387,720 mosques, 16,690 Protestant churches and 4,085 Catholic churches (Ministry of Religion, 2007c). By the end of the New Order (1998), 24 years later, the number of mosques had almost doubled to 609,774, the number of Protestant churches had nearly doubled to 31,759 and the number of Catholic churches had increased more than three times to 14,860. Further, statistics on the ratio between followers and places of worship for 2005 indicate that the ratio of churches to Christians was 1:521, while the ratio for mosques to Muslims was only 1:930. This means that there is almost double the number of churches for the small Christian minority as there are mosques for the Muslim majority in Indonesia.
The disproportionate growth in the building of churches has also contributed to the increase in incidents of violence. Many existing Christian congregations, or those that chose to build without obtaining a permit, bear the brunt of violent attacks from radical Islamic groups. These hardliners justified attacks based on the Joint Decree and argued that action must be taken against churches that have not obtained a permit if the government remains ambivalent. As a result, during the New Order (1966–98), over 450 churches were burnt down or forced to close, either temporarily or permanently (Gultom, 2009a). During this period, attacks on churches were particularly concentrated on the island of Java. From the late 1990s to the early 2000s, there was a noticeable intensification of opposition to churches, and during this period violent attacks were carried out at a large number of churches in a single day (Gultom, 2009b).
The national Protestant Council maintains a running total of the number of churches facing difficulties, although it is important to note that the types of opposition or resistance varied widely, and only some churches were completely burnt down, while other churches may have their premises sealed off or permit suspended temporarily. During the 17 months of the Habibie government (1998–99), more than 156 churches were destroyed. In the following 21 months under President Abdurrahman Wahid (1999–2001), 232 churches were opposed or attacked (Gultom, 2009b). Although the number of attacks dropped under President Megawati Soekarnoputri (2001–04), 92 churches were still destroyed during this time. This corresponds with the containment of conflicts in places such as Maluku by 2001, although concerted campaigns of opposition to churches by radical Islamic groups continued. During the first term of President Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono (2004–09), over 100 Christian churches were attacked or closed (Gultom, 2009c, 2009d). Overall, this means that more churches were attacked or faced barriers to obtaining a permit in the first ten years of democracy than in the entire 30 years of the New Order (1966–98). The opposition was particularly acute in the province of West Java. Data from the national Protestant council (PGI) record over 200 attacks on churches in the province, or 35.17 per cent of all attacks that have occurred since 1998 (Gultom, 2009b, 2009c). This is more than double the number of churches that were attacked or destroyed in West Java under Soeharto.13
This strategy, however, has since been mirrored by some organizations such as MUI, which aim to highlight the similar difficulties that Muslims outside Java face to obtain a permit to build a mosque. For example, research conducted by the Inter-religious Harmony Division of MUI from February to April 2011 found that 28 communities had experienced difficulties obtaining permits for a mosque in Kupang (East Nusa Tenggara); Bali; North Sulawesi; West Papua and Papua (Yusuf, 2012: 22–24). The main point of difference, however, is that there is no suggestion that these minority Muslim communities face the same threats of violence.
The increase in intimidation and attacks against Christian churches, particularly in 2004–05, led Christians to call for legislative reform because radical Islamic groups were citing the regulation to justify their violent actions. Islamists also called for reform in the hopes of introducing tighter regulations that would control the construction of churches.
Debating the Joint Regulation 2006
Organizations such as MUI continued to urge the government to ensure compliance with the Joint Decree (MUI, 1995: 146). These demands later gave way to calls to revise and strengthen the 1969 Joint Decree at least as early as 1990, although no draft was produced at that time (Aqsha et al., 1995: 468–69). The primary aim since 1969 has been to revise the Joint Decree to tighten and clarify the process to obtain a permit. Calls for law reform from all sides gained renewed urgency in 2004 and 2005, when attacks on churches in West Java became particularly intense.
Finally, in late 2004, President Yudhoyono instructed the Ministry of Religion to prepare a new regulation (Republika, 4 December 2004). In September 2005, the drafting process for the new regulation began with a meeting between the police, the Ministry of Religion, the Attorney-General, the Department of Home Affairs and the Department of Justice and Human Rights (PGI, 2006a). This was a significant step in comparison to 1969, when no consultations were undertaken with religious organizations regarding the drafting of the regulation. Between January and March 2006, consultations were conducted with representatives from the six religions (FKUB, 2008: 156). The resulting Joint Regulation has been promoted by the government as a compromise between the six religions represented at these consultations. The six official religions have then disseminated information about the new Joint Regulation among its respective members. This sentiment was echoed by many other respondents, particularly government officials, who claim that the regulation is a result of the agreement between the six religions.
Despite the consultation process, the development of the new regulation did not take place without opposition. Even though the proposal was only for a new ministerial regulation, and therefore did not have to be approved by the legislature (Dewan Perwakilan Rakyat, DPR), 42 members of the legislature, led by a member of the Christian Partai Damai Sejahtera (Peace and Prosperous Party, PDS), filed a petition with the DPR rejecting the draft regulation (Kristiani Post, 24 March 2006). The proposed Joint Regulation went through several drafts before the final version was passed on 21 March 2006.
In particular, the Joint Regulation 2006 was opposed by the Christian political party PDS, as well as members from Partai Demokrasi Indonesia – Perjuangan (PDI-P) and Partai Demokrat (Soefyanto, 2009: 68–69). There was resistance to the Joint Regulation from religious minorities, who argued that the process to obtain a permit is against the Pancasila, human rights,14 and the right to freedom of religion as contained in the Constitution (art. 28E). Further, the Joint Regulation had justified the violent acts of radical Islamic groups, while the state failed to protect the places of worship of religious minorities or to prosecute the perpetrators of violence.
Many agree with the Joint Regulation, including the chairperson of Muhammadiyah, Din Syamsuddin, who believed that it was necessary to maintain religious harmony (Jakarta Post, 31 March 2006). Radical Islamist groups, such as the Anti-Apostasy Alliance Movement (Aliansi Gerakan Anti-Pemurtadan), argued that it should even have the higher legal status of a Government Regulation (Peraturan Pemerintah) or a Law (Undang-undang), rather than just a Ministerial Regulation (Republika, 13 September 2005).
The new legislative framework endorsed by the Joint Regulation established a process for obtaining permits that was widely supported by Islamists for two key reasons. First, the creation of the Inter-religious Harmony Forum effectively created a further bureaucratic obstacle for religious minorities to obtain a permit. Second, the new process is open to the influence of local religious leaders and the discretion of local authorities, which has led to further confusion on how to obtain a permit.
The Joint Regulation provides greater detail and certainty in the process to obtain a permit compared to the 1969 Joint Decree. It has, however, created new problems for religious minorities such as Christians, due to the reluctance of local authorities to grant a permit in the face of opposition from Islamist groups in West Java.
Role of the Inter-religious Harmony Forum
Under the Joint Regulation, the governor, as head of the province, and the regent or mayor, as head of the regency or city, are given responsibility to monitor inter-religious harmony. This is based on the common assumption that the Joint Regulation is imperative to prevent inter-religious conflict and preserve public order and religious harmony, continuing the rhetoric of the Soeharto period.
The primary way the Joint Regulation seeks to ‘implement’ religious harmony is through the establishment of an Inter-religious Harmony Forum (Joint Regulation 2006, art. 8). The Forum consists of representatives from each of the six recognized religions in proportion to the percentage of adherents of these religions in each province and city/regency (art. 1(6)). For example, in 2009, the Depok Forum had one Catholic, Protestant, Buddhist, Hindu and Confucian representative; the other 11 members of the committee were Muslims.
The Forum has several responsibilities, including to conduct inter-religious dialogue; formulate policy recommendations; and to educate the community on laws related to religious harmony at the regency/city and provincial level (art. 8(1); 9(1)). The city/regency Forum has the additional responsibility of providing written recommendations for requests to build a place of worship (art. 9(2)). The Forum consists of 21 members in the provincial Forum and 17 members in the city/regency Forum (art. 10(2)). The members of each Forum are chosen by the Inter-religious Harmony Advisory Council, which consists of representatives from relevant government departments (art. 11).
The Forums are established by regulations of the governor and regent/mayor (art. 12). These local regulations outline the tasks of the Forum and the reporting procedures, although these are generally along the same lines as the new national regulation. The regulations specify the criteria for representatives and the length of members’ terms, which may vary slightly from region to region. For example, members are elected for three years to the provincial Forum of Central Kalimantan, while members to the provincial Forum of Yogyakarta are elected for five-year terms.15 Other regulations establish the Forum in broad terms and do not provide details about the criteria for members or the terms of membership.16 This has led to discrepancies in implementation across Indonesia.
In order to provide greater direction for provincial governments, a guide for the organization and administration of the Forum was created at the Biannual Forum National Congress of 2009.17 One of the main problems identified at the Congress was that many Forums exist with little or no financial assistance from the local government, and therefore do not have offices or funding to produce reports. Forum members argue that they require funding for a suitable office, to run education campaigns, and to fund publications and reports arising from their investigations.
The other major complaint from Forum members was the lack of guidance on how to deal with situations that are not covered in the Joint Regulation. This is perhaps more a question of the limits of responsibility or authority of Forum members, as local authorities often expect them to do more than their stated role. A particular concern for minorities is the composition of the leadership positions on the Forum. For example, during the formation of the Forum in Yogyakarta, there was dispute over the election of the vice-chairperson. As the chairperson was a Muslim, the Christian representatives sought to elect a non-Muslim vice-chairperson, while the Muslim representatives were adamant that the position should be held by a Muslim.18 Another issue that emerged in areas where one religion has a clear religious majority is that the Forum is accused of favoring the majority. At times, however, it is the Forum that is the cause of complaint among Islamists. For example, in the Depok Church Permit Case, the Muslim Solidarity Forum opposing the church staged a protest outside the Forum office to express its frustration that the Forum did not recommend that the mayor cancel the church permit (FSUM, 2009a: app. 23).
Many other organizations have criticized the new Forum for being elitist and adding another layer of bureaucracy to an already lengthy process (Wahid Institute, 2007b). The criticism is not just from religious minorities, but also from within the Muslim community. In a detailed investigation of churches in Jakarta, Paramadina Institute (2012) found that the Forum has at times hindered, rather than facilitated, applications for church permits and has yet to play a significant role in mediating church disputes. Overall, reports remain mixed on whether the Forum will assist in the processing of applications in the long term,19 although there are early indications that it has at times been overshadowed and encumbered by religious bureaucracy and crippled by majoritarianism in its decision making.
Permit application process
The Joint Regulation placed new requirements on a religious community to obtain a permit to build or renovate an existing place of worship (art. 13–20). The general conditions stipulate that an applicant must demonstrate a ‘real need’ for a place of worship and have regard to the total number of religious followers at the village level (art. 13(1)).20 These terms are vague and open to interpretation, as is the following provision, which requires that the construction of the place of worship must ‘respect inter-religious harmony and not disturb the community’ (art. 13(2)). The applicant must also comply with the general administrative conditions for the construction of a building for ‘religious purposes’ (art. 14(1)). This refers to both national and local building laws. A building has a ‘religious function’ if its primary use is as a place of worship, such as a mosque or mushollah (small room set aside for prayer), a church or chapel, a Hindu temple (pura), Buddhist monastery (vihara), or Confucian temple (kelenteng).
If there are fewer than 60 members of a congregation in the village, then the total number of religious followers in the sub-regency, city/regency or province can be relied on (art. 13(3)). This provision is significant, because it essentially defeats the claims of those who argue that a church should not be built because the majority of its members do not live in the neighborhood (rukun tetangga, RT, or rukun warga, RW), or sub-regency.
The greatest controversy is over the requirement that a religious group must obtain the signatures and photocopies of the national identity cards of at least 90 members of the congregation and at least 60 ‘local residents’ of another religion (art. 14(2)). This is known as the ‘90/60 requirement.’ MUI was not satisfied with the requirement, suggesting a higher standard of 200 people (Soefyanto, 2009: 75). PGI also disputed the numbers required, claiming they are arbitrary, unreasonable and often unrealistic (Soefyanto, 2009: 76). On this basis, it suggested a lower threshold of 60/40 signatures (Jakarta Post, 20 February 2006). This requirement of course also presents difficulties for Muslim minorities in Christian-majority provinces, such as Papua and East Nusa Tenggara.
One of the reasons for the fixation on numbers is because of the position expressed in a collection of Islamic hadith that is well known in Indonesia. In Bulughul Maram, a hadith states that there must be a minimum of 40 people in attendance at the mosque for Friday prayers.21 Some Muslims in Indonesia may therefore feel that other religions, such as Christianity, should also have a certain number of members before they can build a church (Soefyanto, 2009: 71).22 While other alternative explanations have been offered for the 90/60 requirement, such as that it was simply the result of a consensus reached among religious leaders in the drafting process, this hadith may explain why some Islamic leaders express concern when a church is built without a significant number of members.
In practice, the 60-signature requirement is clear and the signatures must come from the local community, masyarakat setempat. Given that this document must be signed by the head of the village, it can be presumed that the signatures must be those of residents who live in the village (art. 14(2)(b)). The 90 signatures of religious followers must also come from residents from the area.23 This is actually a surprisingly low threshold given the Indonesian population and the high level of religious affiliation. There are indications, however, that some misinterpretations have emerged about the application of the new Joint Regulation.
The requirement of 90 signatures has been subject to various interpretations. When the Depok Forum conducts investigations to verify an application, it has found that many listed as ‘local’ members of the church do not actually live in the neighborhood of the proposed church building. In the opinion of Dr Loedwijk Gultom, a Protestant (and Batak) member of the Depok Forum, this means that the church does not meet the 90/60 requirement.24 According to the Joint Regulation, however, as long as members of the congregation live in the same province as the church, this is not a ground for rejecting an application. In the Depok Church Permit Case, the Plaintiffs obtained the signatures of over 100 local residents in the area as evidence of local support to the court, which is more than the requirements under the Joint Regulation.25 The Muslim Solidarity Forum disputed the authenticity of these signatures, claiming they were of people who did not live in the neighborhood or village. In response, the church alleged that Islamists who held demonstrations against the church in 1999 were not local residents but came from organizations such as FPI. In defense, the Muslim Solidarity Forum claimed that the majority of its support came from local residents.26 Whether the local community supports or opposes a church proposal is often hotly contested.
The final requirement in the application process is that an applicant must obtain a written recommendation from the city/regency Ministry of Religion and from the city/regency Forum (art. 14(2)). Although these documents were obtained by the church in the Depok Church Permit Case, the mayor used the recommendations of the Ministry and the Forum to support his decision to cancel the permit. The Ministry, along with the Forum, therefore still plays a very significant role in determining applications for a permit.
The Forum and the Advisory Council were to be formed in the provinces and cities/regencies within one year, that is, by 21 March 2007 (art. 27(1)), although this deadline was not met in many regions. The reason that some areas were able to establish a Forum quicker than others is because an inter-religious harmony committee already existed in these areas and so was conveniently converted into the Inter-religious Harmony Forum. It is common for the Forum to be referred to as the Inter-religious Communication Forum (Forum Komunikasi Umat Beragama), which is the previous name of these forums, rather than the Inter-religious Harmony Forum, which is the name of the new forum.
In summary, instead of clarifying the process, procedures have become more bureaucratic and conflict continues to arise because the underlying issues are not being addressed.
Disputes over religious buildings
Despite the introduction of the Joint Regulation 2006, attacks and threats of violence against churches in Indonesia continued. The building of a church not only symbolizes the presence of Christians in the area but also the possibility that Muslims might convert and join the church, which implies a potential shift in community identity and religious affiliation. In this section, I will analyze the common arguments put forward in opposition to the construction of the church, illustrated by the Depok Church Permit Case. The same arguments, however, are often used against other religious minorities, such as Ahmadiyah, or Muslim-minorities in Christian areas.
Claims of territoriality
One of the most common complaints voiced by Muslims is that Christians should not be allowed to build a church in a ‘Muslim area’ – that is, an area with a Muslim majority. This is evidence of a tendency to claim territory in the name of Islam for fear of the perceived growth of Christianity and of concerns that Christians intend to proselytize or convert people in the area. These claims may be justified by reliance on population statistics, the religious history of the area, and developments such as the introduction of local laws based on Islam.
As early as 1965, there were indications of a dispute over a church built in the ‘Muslim area’ of Dompu, East Nusa Tenggara (Hasyim, 1979: 282). In 1969, after the Slipi incident over a church in Jakarta (see Chapter 2), Muhammad Natsir (1980: 23), the leader of Masyumi and founder of the Indonesian Islamic Preaching Council (Dewan Dakwah Islamiyah Indonesia), declared that:
Many times the Islamic community has complained to the government about efforts to ‘Christianize’ [meng-Kristen-kan] Muslims, for example by building churches in areas with a Muslim-majority population …
In the Depok Church Permit Case, the Defense argued that the cancellation of the permit was justified because the church was being built in a ‘Muslim area’ (Administrative Court, 2009a: 12). In 2008, 90 per cent of the population of Depok identified as Muslim, yet there was also a sizable Christian population, 5.35 per cent Protestant and 3.58 per cent Catholic (City of Depok, 2009a).
Although Depok may have a Muslim-majority population, whether Depok was originally a ‘Muslim’ or ‘Christian’ area continues to be a matter of debate.27 Some Christians claim that the word ‘Depok’ derives its name from the first local Christian congregation established in the area. In May 1696, a former Dutch East India Company officer, Cornelis Chastelein, purchased what is now about 6 per cent of the land of Depok for farming. He brought 200 slaves with him, who originally came from Makassar and Bali. Chastelein was active in spreading the teachings of Christianity and founded the first local Christian congregation, known as ‘De Eerste Protestante Organisatie van Christenen.’28 Between 1699 and 1713, about 150 people in the area were baptized.29 In June 1714, when Chastelein passed away, he left some of his land to the families of 12 of his slaves who had converted to Christianity (Kompas, 2004: 324).
The establishment of the first Indonesian ecumenical Christian theological college in 1878 was also evidence of presence of the Christian community in Depok (Soejana et al., 2008: 756). Sponsored by several mission societies, the college recruited widely from across Indonesia, though a large number of its students were Batak.30 In light of the history of the area, the chairperson of the Cornelis Chastelein Foundation and descendant of one of the 12 families, Boy Loen, claimed that what is now known as the city of Depok was originally a Christian area.31 On the other hand, the MUI of Depok, which claimed to represent groups such as Hizbut Tahrir Indonesia (HTI) and FPI, hotly disputed any suggestion that Depok was a Christian area and instead emphasized the influence of Islam in the area.32 The chairperson of MUI of Depok, Ahmad Badruzzaman, did acknowledge the history of Cornelis Chastelein in the area, but he claimed that the word Depok is derived from the term padepokan, which means ‘residence’ or ‘hermitage.’33 The claim made by MUI that Islamic communities were established in Depok prior to the arrival of Christianity is not, however, supported by evidence.
One example or evidence of the presence and influence of Islamists in an area is perda syariah (local regulations based on Islam). Although campaigns to introduce perda syariah have so far failed in Depok, some elements of syariah (Islamic law) have been included in local regulations. The mayor has issued several appeals (himbauan) based on syariah that forbid the consumption of alcohol, encourage people to only use their right hand when they eat or drink, and require civil servants to be able to recite the Qur’an.34
Christian concerns regarding perda syariah are indications of fears of Islamization’ – that is, concerted attempts to make Indonesia an Islamic country under Islamic law. According to the Protestant representative of the Depok Forum, Loedwijk, rumors have circulated among Christians that ‘some people want to make Cinere a Muslim area.’35 When the church in the Depok Church Permit Case questioned mayor Nur Mahmudi Isma’il about his decision to cancel its permit, he emphasized the fact that he made the decision as a Muslim and as a representative (and the former chairperson) of the Islamic-based political party PKS.36 In 2009, he was criticized by Christians for introducing a ban on the consumption of alcohol in Depok, which some believed was based on Islamic law.37 He was re-elected in the 2010 elections and served as mayor of Depok for a second term.38 Aside from claims of territoriality based on majoritarianism, the history of Islam in the area, or campaigns to introduce local laws based on Islam, many churches are accused of being illegal.
‘Illegal’ buildings
Another argument commonly voiced is that a church is illegal because it does not have a permit, the permit is invalid, or because it is meeting in a private house. The presumption that flows from this argument is that radical Islamists can justify their actions to close down ‘illegal’ churches, using violence where necessary, if the government is inactive or ambivalent towards such churches.
Some churches that were established prior to 1969 do not have permits. Post-1969, many churches were unable to obtain permits because approval was at the discretion of the local government. Some churches temporarily navigated this problem by using private houses. This led to new issues, however, as neighbors complained about the noise of church services conducted in homes and the difficulties it created in terms of traffic and parking. In November 1975, to appease concerns in this regard, the Department of Home Affairs issued an Instruction to the governor of every province clarifying that private houses should not be used as churches, and urging local authorities to enforce this law.39 In areas where this was of particular concern, such as Jakarta, the governor issued an Instruction reiterating the national government’s stance that houses cannot be used as places of worship.40 This remained an ongoing issue of concern and, in 1995, the Minister of Religion issued an Instruction admitting that the 1969 regulation was not being followed and urging provincial officials from the Ministry to ‘solve problems created by places of worship’ by taking a greater role in the administration process alongside the local government.41
House churches remain a cause of complaint and some local governments, such as in Bandung, have responded by issuing further prohibitions on the use of homes as churches (Compass Direct, 20 January 2006). Some congregations have obtained permission to use a house as a place of worship, only later to have permission revoked. For example, Pastor Syaiful Hamzah, a convert from Islam, faced opposition to the small church that was based at his home in North Jakarta. In 2008 he was granted permission from the Ministry of Religion to run a service in his home. Despite this, in September 2008, nearby residents, together with village officials, police officers and representatives of the village mosque, demanded that he cease holding services at home. He relocated services to a church building that had a permit some distance away.42
A more common solution is for churches to hold services in hotels, high-rise buildings, apartments and shopping centers. A church may choose to conduct services in such locations because it is easier to get away without a permit in an existing building, the buildings are usually owned by Chinese (often Christian) business people who are sympathetic to Christians, and there is less chance of complaints in a business district because the building is not in close proximity to private housing.
A church may also be accused of being ‘illegal’ if a permit was not obtained from the current local government. In the Depok Church Permit Case, the mayor argued that the church did not have the permission of the current local government due to the restructuring of the area as a result of decentralization. The region of Depok was first formed in 1871, although it was not formalized by a national law until 1981 when the government created the administrative city of Depok.43 Further restructuring took place in April 1999 as part of the national process of decentralization, and what was then Depok was combined with some parts of Bogor to form the city of Depok.44 It was at this time that the sub-regency of Cinere was formed, which is where the church in the Depok Church Permit Case is located.
In support of the mayor’s decision to cancel the permit, the defendant argued that because the sub-regency of Cinere was only formed in 1999, and the church obtained the permit before this time, the sub-regent of Cinere had not had the opportunity to consider the application for the permit (Administrative Court, 2009a: 12). On this basis, the Defense argued that the permit was not valid and the church should be required to apply for a permit under the new system via the relevant local authorities post-1999. The court dismissed this argument, because the new Joint Regulation clearly states that permits obtained under the previous regulation are still valid. Nevertheless, the argument demonstrates the administrative difficulties that arise as a result of decentralization and the division of local government administration since 1998.
Churches may therefore be considered to be illegal because of a lack of a permit, because the church is meeting in a private dwelling, or because there have been changes to local government as a result of the process of decentralization. One approach is that different church denominations can share the one building, which often occurs on a temporary basis, although this assumes unity between Christian churches.
Assumptions of unity
In areas where church buildings already exist, Islamists often argue that there is already a church (or several churches) in the area and there is not a real need for another one. This is partly because of a lack of awareness or unwilling to acknowledge the fragmentation and disunity amongst Christian churches, and the independent nature of some church denominations, particularly Pentecostal and Evangelical churches.
In the Depok Church Permit Case, the mayor argued that there was already a large number of churches in the area and so there was no need for another church. In fact, Depok has a higher ratio of mosques to Muslims than churches to Christians. While only 89.74 per cent of the population is Muslim (City of Depok, 2009a), 94.55 per cent of all places of worship are mosques or mushollah (City of Depok, 2009b). On the other hand, 5.41 per cent of the population are Protestant and 5.08 per cent of places of worship are Protestant churches, but for Catholics, who make up 3.58 per cent of the population, only 0.3 per cent of places of worship are Catholic churches. The relatively high number of Protestant churches is perhaps an indication of the large number of denominations and new independent churches, such as Evangelical and Pentecostal churches, which are usually classified by the state as ‘Protestant’ rather than Catholic.
Whether there is a ‘real’ need for another church building is debatable and difficult to determine given the lack of guidelines in the Joint Regulation. For example, it is unclear whether Christians are expected to attend an existing church in the area, even if it is a denomination or tradition different to their own. It is already a common practice in Indonesia for churches without a permit to hold services in the building of another church denomination. For example, in the Depok Church Permit Case, church services were being conducted in another church in the area, although the building was too small to fit the entire congregation.
Muslims who oppose a church often claim there is no need for another church as many others already exist in the area. This argument, as pointed out by Syafii Anwar, director of the International Centre for Islam and Pluralism, highlights a general lack of understanding about the denominational fragmentation of Christian churches.45 Due to the divisions within the Christian community, each Protestant denomination usually has its own building. This has led to a situation where there are more churches in some areas than mosques.
Addressing why these concerns are specific to Protestant, Pentecostal or Evangelical churches, as opposed to Catholic churches, Franz Magnis-Suseno, a scholar and well-known Catholic priest in Indonesia, emphasized the centralized organization of the Catholic Church.46 Local Catholic congregations are required to obtain permission from the National Catholic Council (Kantor Waligereja Indonesia, KWI) in order to build a new church. Permission will only be granted if the congregation is a reasonable size, if another Catholic church does not already exist in the area and if the church has good relations with the non-Christian local community. This ensures accountability and due process for the construction of Catholic churches. By contrast, many independent Protestant, Pentecostal or Evangelical congregations are not accountable to a higher authority regarding decisions to build a church. This means that these congregations often build churches much faster regardless of the size of the congregation, with little regard for the existence of other churches in the area or of opposition from the local community. Part of the difficulty of establishing relationships with the local community is the growing reality of urbanization and the problems this creates in terms of local planning issues.
Local planning and urbanization
Another common concern is that a church contributes to traffic congestion, creates parking issues, or is located in a crowded urban space. The growth of urbanization, particularly in areas of West Java close to the capital city Jakarta, has increased competition for, and disputes over, access to land. One example is the conflict over the Christian Arastamar Theological College (also known as SETIA) in Jakarta. In 1991, when an application for the construction of the college was lodged, 500 local households signed a document petitioning the local government to prevent the construction. The local government responded by issuing an order to stop construction but, after negotiations, the construction was permitted to continue (Aqsha et al., 1995: 474–75). Since the college began, there have been ongoing tensions between college students, many of whom are from eastern Indonesia (that is, non-Javanese), and local Muslim neighbors (Javanese), as well as radical Islamic groups. The differences between students and local residents are therefore not only religious but also differences of ethnicity.
On 26 July 2008, long-term tensions broke into a violent conflict between local residents and students, after allegations that a student had stolen a water pump from a local resident (Jakarta Post, 1 August 2009). The college buildings were substantially damaged, 1,200 students had to be evacuated and the campus was closed indefinitely. Yusuf Lifire, administrator of SETIA, suspected that the perpetrators were from the radical Islamic group FPI, although the instigators were never identified or prosecuted.47 Leaders from the national religious councils were called in to assist in the process of dialogue, including Slamet Effendi Yusuf as a representative of MUI. He downplayed factors such as religion or ethnicity, and instead emphasized that local Muslim residents were primarily concerned about the campus because of the loud music and the consumption of alcohol.48
Regardless of the reasons for the conflict, the validity of the permit of the college came under investigation. The deputy governor of Jakarta suggested that the college move campus entirely, citing the rising cost of land as the reason.
