In 2011, it was reported that the Jakarta Administration Agency had acquired the property, and that the seminary had been relocated, without assistance from the government (Jakarta Post, 3 September 2011).
Similar concerns over planning and urbanization arose in the Depok Church Permit Case. The area of Depok has changed significantly over the years. According to Slamet Effendi Yusuf, board member of MUI, Cinere was formerly a very traditional, Muslim community.49 It has undergone changes over the past few years due to the process of urbanization, which gained momentum due to the first national settlement of housing for low-income earners (perumahan nasional). This is partly because of the establishment of the University of Indonesia in Depok in 1987. From a population of just 240,000 in 1982, Depok grew to a population of 1.2 million by 1999 (Kompas, 2004: 322). This created difficulties when the processing of an application for a permit extended over a number of years. For example, Betty Sitorus from the Batak Church explained that when the church purchased the land in Cinere it was a rural area with very few neighboring houses.50 Since then, the area had developed significantly, and had become crowded as more people moved into the area. Increasing urbanization has also led to greater diversity and pluralism within communities, although ethnic differences in particular have been another source of tension.
Ethnicity and foreignness
Another argument is that a church is too ‘exclusive’ and congregation members keep to themselves. This is underlined by the issue of ethnicity, which was one of the topics of discussion that were prohibited under the New Order, referred to by the acronym ‘SARA’: ethnicity (suku), religion (agama), race (ras), and intergroup relations (antar golongan). It is also linked to suspicions that proselytization may be a cover for a broader goal ‘to legitimize local domination or imperialist expansion by foreign powers’ (An-Na’im, 2009: 6), for example, by Western countries considered to be ‘Christian.’
The issue of ethnicity reared its head in the courtroom in the Depok Church Permit Case. The mayor argued that this incident raised the sensitive issue of SARA and that the construction of the church had ‘the potential to create conflict over issues of SARA’ (Administrative Court, 2009a: 13, 20–24, 44). This may have been a reference both to religious and ethnic differences. The court did not elaborate on this argument, although it did refer to the need to maintain the ‘values of the Pancasila’ (Administrative Court, 2009a: 50, 57). More generally, there is a persistent stereotype that Batak culture is kasar (rude, blunt) and aggressive, while other cultures such as Javanese are considered to be halus (refined, polite) by comparison. Vergouwen has captured the particular characteristics that have been attributed to Bataks in the following way:
It is a singular trait of a Batak’s character to think that he is always in the right and as a consequence he will make an issue over the merest trifle, fighting over swallows’ feathers, it is called. He is also particularly desirous of gaining the victory in a lawsuit as being the most sagacious party; he suffers a corresponding shame if he loses.
(Vergouwen, 1964: 377–78)
Other cultural stereotypes that portray Batak culture as opposed to the teachings of Islam include that the Batak like to gamble, eat dog and drink alcohol. Although the concept of SARA was a creation of the New Order, the fact that it was raised by the mayor in court as an argument against the building of a church suggests that ten years after the fall of Soeharto it remains an issue of concern.
Ethnicity is another reason why churches other than Catholic churches are more often attacked. While many Catholic churches are a mixture of ethnic groups, there is a greater tendency in some Protestant denominations to focus on one ethnicity (implicitly or explicitly). The Batak church in the Depok Church Permit Case is one example.51 Church members identify ethnically as Batak and most services are conducted in the Batak dialect. Given that this denomination has churches across Indonesia, including West Java, Batak Christians are perceived by many Javanese Muslims as ‘foreigners’ both in terms of ethnicity and religion.
In part, this is related to the implications of government policy of transmigration and the issues this has caused for putra daerah (literally ‘sons of the area’), that is, locals, in provinces such as Papua. There have been disputes in Papua over this issue because of the preferential treatment given to indigenous Papuans under special autonomy. For example, if an adat (traditional) Papuan community claims that the land on which a mosque is build is its traditional land, then it can be compulsorily acquired by them.52 This has been a cause of resentment from the Muslim community in Papua, who are largely non-Papuans and have settled in Papua as part of the government transmigration program. Transmigration is suspected by Islamists to be one way Christians enter a community to begin proselytization. Such allegations were made as early as 1967, when some Christian missionaries were accused of working through this program in Pasaman by pretending to be Muslims and then on arrival built a church (Ka’bah, 1985: 37; Rasjidi, 1976: 430–31).
Another group often considered ‘foreign’ by some Indonesians are ethnic Chinese Indonesians. Of the 5 million ethnic Chinese in Indonesia, at least one-third are Christians (Ananta et al., 2005: 30). After the fall of Soeharto in 1998, anti-Chinese riots broke out across Indonesia (see generally Lindsey, 2005: 41–76; Coppel, 2002). Nevertheless, since the introduction of democracy, the Chinese community has enjoyed greater freedom to express and incorporate Chinese culture within the Christian community, such as the use of Mandarin in church services, and the hosting of Chinese-speaking preachers from overseas (Soleiman and Steenbrink, 2008: 904, 917). Another cause of resentment towards Chinese Indonesia Christians is their perceived wealth and the prevailing stereotype that there is a socio-economic gap between Christians (who are considered ‘rich’) and Muslims (who are considered ‘poor’). Perceptions of Christians as ‘foreigners’ is reinforced by the belief that churches receive large amounts of foreign funding, creating an economic gap, as I explain next.
Economic disparity and corruption
Underlying disputes over church permits are often feelings of jealousy or envy towards wealthy church members, and the real or perceived economic disparity between Muslims and Christians. Churches are envied for the seeming ease with which they obtain funds to be able to purchase land and build a church (often, it is said, through reliance on foreign donations). There is also the stereotype that Christians, particularly Chinese Christians, drive expensive cars to church while Muslims walk or ride a motorbike to the nearest mosque although, in reality, many Muslims also own cars while many Christians can only afford motorbikes.
One reason that Chinese Indonesian churches are attacked or opposed is because of their perceived wealth. A common myth is that the Chinese control 70 per cent of the Indonesian economy although, according to Chai and Roy (2007: 64), this claim is difficult to substantiate and certainly not all Indonesian Chinese can be considered ‘wealthy.’ Nevertheless, there has clearly been a growth of rich, often charismatic or Evangelical, churches that endorse the prosperity doctrine, a belief that God will bless His followers with material possessions (Time magazine, 26 April 2010). This growth can in part be attributed to the attraction the message of material success has for wealthy or middle-class Chinese Indonesian Christians.
Although Christian congregations generally struggle to meet the permit requirements, it appears less difficult for wealthy, Evangelical (often Chinese) churches in major cities, such as Jakarta. For example, in September 2008, a new Christian mega-church opened in Jakarta, perhaps the largest Chinese Christian Evangelical facility in the world, and the largest state-of-the-art classical music center in Indonesia (Jakarta Post, 19 October 2009). The church is led by well-known Chinese Indonesian Pastor Stephen Tong.53 Tong founded the Indonesian Evangelical Reformed Church in 1989, a denomination he invented based on his own unique theology, a blend of Reformed theology or Calvinism, with an evangelical focus on the Bible.54 It took 16 years for the Evangelical Reformed Church to be granted a permit to build the $27.3 million, 4,500-seat Millennium Cathedral.55 The executive director of the Reformed Centre of Religion and Society, Benyamin Intan, claims that the church obtained the signatures and approval of 200 local residents.56
This church is indicative of a broader phenomenon of the establishment of large Pentecostal and Evangelical mega-churches. Given the large number of small churches that continue to be harassed or closed by radical Islamic groups, particularly in West Java, this provides fuel to accusations that wealthy Evangelical churches bribe local officials in order to obtain a permit. In contrast, churches that are forced to close or are unable to obtain a permit are those that refuse, or cannot afford, to pay the necessary bribes. Coupled with this is the presumption that churches receive funding from outside Indonesia and, by inference, are part of foreign interference in domestic affairs. It is also perceived as an ‘unfair’ advantage if a church receives foreign funds, and there are fears that this amounts to interference by Western, secular and/or Christian churches and governments in Indonesian domestic affairs.
Finally, allegations of bribery and corruption are made, casting doubts on whether the signatures in support of a church, or the approval of local authorities, were obtained by honest means. Allegations of corruption are often referred to by the term nyogok, bribe – that is, the payment of an extra sum of money to government officials to ensure an application for a permit is approved. Under the New Order, ‘Underpaid bureaucrats supplement(ed) their incomes with informal sources of income by selling licenses and levying personalized forms of taxation’ (Nordholt 2004: 32). This situation has changed little today.
In the Depok Church Permit Case, the Muslim Solidarity Forum, a coalition of Muslims who opposed the church in Depok, accused the church of using bribery to obtain local support. The leader of the Muslim Solidarity Forum, Budi Waluyo, cited rumors that the church gave out sembako57 to local residents in exchange for signatures of agreement to the construction of the church.58 When questioned about accusations that the church had bribed locals to agree with its proposal, church committee member Betty Sitorus denied the allegations. She admitted that the church usually hands out sembako as gifts to its employees, such as the security guards, during religious festivals such as Idul Fitri, the celebration at the end of Ramadan.59 She stressed that this is common practice in Indonesia, particularly for Muslims, and that it was not distributed in exchange for signatures.
Nevertheless, the Muslim Solidarity Forum also claimed that the church has been dishonest by providing false identity cards to meet the requirement of 90 church members. The Defense even presented a statement from one local resident, Abdulloh Jawi, who argued that he had never signed the document in agreement to build the church and claimed his signature must have been forged (Administrative Court, 2009a: 31). There was also a similar statement given by another local resident that she did not give consent for her identity card to be used in support of the church’s application for a permit. The church denied these allegations, and pointed to a statement of agreement to the proposed church that had been signed by numerous local residents (p.26–27). The Plaintiff also produced two local Muslim residents as witnesses, Abdul Basir and Nasir. Nasir, the Head of RW 4, testified that he agreed to the construction of the church, while Abdul gave evidence that the people who had demonstrated against the church were not locals from the area (p.32–33).
To counter this, the Defense submitted a letter from residents who disagreed with the construction of the church, as well as letters of opposition from several local mosques and Qur’an recitation groups, although the members of these groups overlapped (p.30–31). The defendant called five witnesses, including H Koen Soelistijo, the leader of RW 14 (1986–98), who was the leader of the only RT of the four in the area that opposed the construction of the church back in 1998 (p.34).
In response, the church alleged that the Muslim Solidarity Forum engaged in corrupt practices and manufactured support for its campaign by bribing people to attend the istighotsah ceremonies held in protest against the church. On the other hand, the Muslim Solidarity Forum suspected judicial corruption in this case and believed that the only reason the church won was because it bribed the judiciary. The Batak are well known in Indonesia as successful business people, high-ranking government officials, military officers and lawyers (Rae et al., 2008: 560). One of the reasons Batak lawyers are said to have a high success rate in court is because of their close connections with the police and other government officials.
Having identified the reasons why places of worship may be opposed, I will now discuss the significance of some key court cases regarding disputes over permits and examine how different mechanisms and institutions have been used to complain against the actions of government officials.
Asserting legal rights in court
According to the new regulation, if a dispute over a church arises, a meeting must first be held with the local community (art. 21–22). If the dispute remains unresolved, the regent or mayor, assisted by the Ministry of Religion, must facilitate a meeting between the religious groups concerned. If this fails, then the parties involved can take the case to court, as occurred in the Depok Church Permit Case.
The resolution of disputes was one of the biggest issues raised at the Inter-religious Harmony Forum National Congress in 2009. Members of the Forum from the regions sought advice on how to deal with situations not covered by the new Joint Regulation. Many expressed the need for guidance on the appropriate course of action when a Christian congregation has fulfilled all the requirements, but faced opposition from Muslim groups, particularly from groups outside the community, such as FPI (FKUB, 2009c). According to the chairperson of the Depok Forum, KH Farid Hadjiri, this is a major problem in Depok and he expressed frustration at the lack of guidance in the regulation.60 This indicates that formal dispute resolution procedures are largely ineffective in the face of concerted campaigns to close churches and inaction from law enforcement officials who are unwilling to prevent violence.
In addition to the use of violence and attempts to influence the law reform process, Islamists have responded by pressuring local authorities to cancel church permits. This elicited two main reactions from Christians: complaints to non-judicial institutions such as the Ombudsman and cases brought to the court. The Depok Church Permit Case is one of a handful of court cases in which Christians have asserted their rights to build a church by challenging decisions of local authorities to cancel a permit. This has been made possible by the new rights and freedoms granted under democracy, combined with the momentum gained by the court victories won by some churches, even if the outcome has at times proven hollow.
Channels for advocacy and complaint
When faced with opposition to the construction of a church, Christians have asserted rights by lodging complaints with existing independent institutions such as the National Human Rights Commission or the Ombudsman, and by establishing non-government organizations (NGOs) as vehicles for advocacy. Complaints about violent attacks on churches have been made to independent bodies at the provincial and national level, as well as to international organizations such as the United Nations. In taking this approach, both the national rhetoric of the ‘Pancasila,’ and international discourse on human rights and religious freedom, have been used by Christians to support their case. Further, recourse to non-judicial mechanisms of review after a court decision has already been made suggests that these institutions are being used to compensate for the weaknesses of the courts.
Complaints have been made to the national Ombudsman,61 to its regional branches, and to the local Ombudsman of Yogyakarta. By 2011, the national Ombudsman had received 11 cases since it began, six of these in West Java, including complaints from the church in the Depok Church Permit Case (Ombudsman, 2010).62 The willingness and capacity of the Ombudsman to respond to such complaints often depends on whether the complaint is against a local government or against the Inter-religious Harmony Forum. The Ombudsman only has the power to investigate the actions of government institutions and therefore cannot pursue claims against the Forum, which is considered by the Ombudsman to be an independent body.63
On this basis, the national Ombudsman has declined to respond to complaints in some cases. For example, in January 2007, a complaint was made to the National Ombudsman Representative Office for Central Java and the Special Province of Yogyakarta by the Indonesian Bethel Church about an application for a church permit in Purbalingga, Central Java. In this case, the regent had rejected an application for a permit to build a church based on the recommendation of the Forum. After receiving the complaint, the Ombudsman responded to the complainant by determining that the case was outside its authority because it considered the Forum to be an independent body, not a government institution (Ombudsman, 2009a; FKUB, 2007).
Other churches have had more success in cities where a local Ombudsman exists. For example, in October 2008, the local Ombudsman of the province of Yogyakarta received a complaint about a church permit. The Ombudsman investigated the matter and facilitated consultations with the Inter-religious Harmony Forum, the Ministry of Religion, the church and the local government. On 24 March 2009, the local Ombudsman issued a recommendation concluding that the leader of the village had the power to reject permits, but must provide adequate reasons for his decision (Ombudsman, 2009b). As no reasons had been provided in this case, the Ombudsman requested that reasons be given. At December 2009, however, no reasons had been forthcoming and the church had not been able to obtain a permit.
The Ombudsman has received a small number of complaints against local government officials in comparison to the National Commission for Human Rights (Komnas HAM),64 which has the power to receive complaints concerning government decisions that may breach human rights, and investigate and make recommendations to the government (see Herbert, 2009). Komnas HAM was established in the final years of Soeharto’s New Order,65 which raised concerns that it was largely an exercise in window-dressing in an attempt to legitimize the Soeharto government. Despite its initial critics, after the transition to democracy in 1998, the authority and independence of Komnas HAM was strengthened by legislation.66 As Lindsey (2007: 15) has pointed out, Komnas HAM was ‘one of the New Order’s few successful public law innovations.’
Komnas HAM has received numerous complaints based on the right to religious freedom regarding threats to or attacks on churches.67 For example, in March 2008, the Indonesian Christian Church (Gereja Kristen Indonesia, GKI) of Bogor lodged a complaint after the permit it received in 2006 was cancelled by local authorities. In response, on 7 April 2008, Komnas HAM sent a letter to the Minister of Religion, the Minister of Home Affairs, and the mayor of Bogor, requesting clarification of the decision and a quick response in this case (Administrative Court, 2008: 8). Although no response was received, the church used the letter from the Ombudsman as evidence in court (see the Bogor Church Permit Case below). In October 2008, the church lodged a complaint with the National Human Rights Commission detailing the disruption to its plans due to the campaign against the construction of the church (Komnas HAM, 2008). Although Komnas HAM can make recommendations to government authorities, the force of its decisions are limited because they are not legally binding and depend on the responsiveness of government agencies.68 As the Bogor Church Permit Case demonstrates, it may, however, contribute to evidence in a court case.
Despite the potential for non-judicial mechanisms of accountability to resolve complaints against government agencies before going to court, the extent to which the recommendations of these institutions are followed depends on the respect and independence accorded to it by the state. Given that there are no ramifications for government officials who refuse to recognize a binding court decision, there is also little incentive for government officials to comply with a non-binding recommendation.
Challenging government decisions pre-1998
Another forum for complaints has been the Administrative Court, which provides an avenue for judicial review of administrative decisions in Indonesia. The concept of an Administrative Court in Indonesia was proposed as early as 1964, but it was not until 1978 that Soeharto announced that the administrative courts would be established.69 It was another eight years before the draft law was adopted, and it did not come into effect until January 1991.70 This reform was significant because, in addition to the establishment of the Administrative Courts, it introduced the principle of judicial review of administrative action.71 It defines an administrative decision as a written decision by a body or administrative official, consisting of acts under administrative law based on laws that are ‘concrete, individual and final, and that give rise to legal consequences for a person or legal body’ (art. 1(9)). Persons or legal entities affected by the decision may seek judicial review and must either show that an administrative decision is opposed to the law or opposed to general principles of good governance (art. 53).72 The Administrative Court has the power to award several types of remedies, the main remedy being to nullify an administrative decision, or to order the defendant to issue a new decision.
During the New Order, the only case in which a church challenged the decision of the local government is the Nababan Case in 1993.73 Bishop Nababan had been the bishop (ephorus)74 of the Batak Church75 for six years. In 1993, he filed a case in the Administrative Court of Medan (North Sumatra) to challenge a decision of the head of the Coordinating Agency for National Stability, then Indonesia’s largest secret service agency (known as Badan Koordinasi Stabilitas Nasional, or Bakorstanas).76 Bakorstanas sought to remove Nababan from office and appoint an interim bishop to facilitate the congress for the election of a new bishop.
This litigation arose due to the challenge the Batak church posed to the power of state elites in the area. In 1987, Nababan had been elected as bishop of HKBP, winning over his rival Simanjuntak (Klinken, 1997). Nababan had fallen out of favor with government officials after the Batak Church, under his leadership, established an NGO that had been outspoken on environmental and human rights abuses committed by a giant paper-pulp factory near Lake Toba. As the elite benefited from this factory, these allegations threatened their political and economic interests (HRW, 1994b: 95). Aside from human rights issues, other accounts of this case emphasize that Nababan introduced some practices into the church, such as evangelical revival meetings, which were controversial and a source of division at the time (Aritonang and Steenbrink, 2008: 562).
Due to a combination of these factors, the government intervened in the internal elections for bishop of HKBP by preventing the re-election of Nababan and ordering the re-election of his rival Simanjuntak, who was sympathetic to the government. This split the Batak Church into factions: the first loyal to Nababan and the second loyal to the state and in favor of Simanjuntak (HRW, 1994b: 94). Although this case was portrayed by the government as a conflict internal to the Batak Church, it was in reality a case of state interference in the religious affairs of the church.77
In court, Bakorstanas was found to lack the authority to make a decision on the internal affairs of the church because it lacked executive power (Bedner, 2001: 174; Bedner, 1997: 187). Nababan was therefore successful in seeking the cancellation of its decision. In retaliation, Bakorstanas challenged the Chairperson of the Administrative Court Lintong Siahaan, arguing that his status as a member of the Batak Church influenced his decision in this case. The court rejected this claim, although Siahaan’s subsequent resignation raised questions about the validity of the court decision (Bedner, 2001: 174).
The Nababan Case was one of the only cases in the New Order in which a church challenged the decision of a government body. Since 2008, however, there have been several cases brought by churches to the Administrative Courts.
Judicial review in the Administrative Court
By the end of 2012, there had only been four court cases across Indonesia where a church sought judicial review of an administrative decision to cancel its permits. All four cases occurred in the province of West Java. These cases are evidence of Christians asserting their democratic rights through legal processes. The Administrative Court is therefore becoming an important forum for the resolution of such disputes, although the scope for enforcing judgments without the support of the religious majority and in the face of threats of violence from radical Islamic groups is limited.
Bogor Church Permit Case
In 2008, the Bogor Church Permit Case was initiated by the Indonesian Christian Church (Gereja Kristen Indonesia, GKI) of Curug Mekar, Bogor. It challenged the power of the Head of the City of Bogor Administration and Planning to cancel its permit (Administrative Court, 2008). The church had initially applied for a permit from local authorities in 2002. The 1,700-member church claimed to have made numerous efforts to disseminate information about its plans and even obtained video evidence of local residents agreeing to its proposal. On 13 July 2006, four years later, the church was finally granted a permit.78
The permit was granted in 2006 during the transition phase from the old to the new permit regulation system. Although the Joint Regulation was passed in March 2006, the new process had not yet been implemented in Bogor. The Inter-religious Harmony Forum was only formed in 2007, however, so the dispute had begun before the Forum was established.79
The construction of the church initially had the support of the local government and the mayor attended the opening ceremony of the construction site to lay the first brick. Not long after construction began, the church faced increasing opposition from members of Islamic groups such as FPI, PKS and the Bogor Ulama Forum (Administrative Court, 2008: 24–25). The church claimed to have amicable relationships with many long-standing Muslim residents in the area, but attributed the opposition to some Muslim residents who had moved into the area just two or three years ago and who were encouraged to oppose the church by members of radical Islamic groups.
On 14 February 2008, the local government suspended the church’s permit.80 The church then took its case to court. In court, the Defense argued that one of the reasons that the church should not be built was because the road on which the church site was located had been renamed after Islamic figure Kyai Haji Binu, the founder of Al Ghazali pesantren (Islamic boarding school) in Bogor (Administrative Court, 2008: 27). Like the Depok Church Permit Case, the defendant used such evidence to argue that a church should not be built in a ‘Muslim’ area. This argument had no legal standing in court, and the church succeeded at first instance. The City of Bogor Administration and Planning lodged a subsequent application to the High Administrative Court in Jakarta, but it was dismissed.
It is significant that this case was even taken to court, as a closer look at the situation demonstrates that Christians are still divided on the merits of taking legal action when such conflicts arise. According to lawyer Fatmawati, it was difficult for the church to agree on instituting court proceedings because:
the image of the courts in Indonesia is very corrupt and they [the church] were convinced we would lose … one of the judges in the case was not embarrassed to say that he would accept the largest bribe.81
This suggests that the history of corruption within the judicial system is a significant deterrent for potential litigants. This case is not only remarkable because the church went to court, but also because it was successful. By law, this means the church can now recommence construction. Some of the church congregation and leaders, however, remain fearful of provoking Islamists who oppose the church. Local politics may also have played a part, with the Mayor of Bogor, Diani Budiarto, winning the elections backed by the Democratic Party, PKS, and PDI-P. There were suspicions raised that the local administration had collaborated with the mayor to close the church due to the 2010 elections (Jakarta Post, 15 January 2011). In January 2011, the Bogor Administration appealed to the Supreme Court, but the court refused to hear the case because since 2004 it had no longer had jurisdiction to hear appeals regarding complaints of regional government officials.82 This means that the decision of the High Administrative Court stands and the church should now be able to continue construction.83
In June 2010, however, an Islamic coalition known as Forkami filed a complaint against the neighborhood leader, alleging that he had forged signatures of local residents that were submitted by the church in support of its application for a permit. In January 2011, the District Court of Bogor found the neighborhood leader guilty, although the church claimed that those signatures had not been included in the application for a permit (District Court, 2010a).
The dispute continued since the Supreme Court declined to hear the case, which effectively affirmed the decision of the Administrative Court in favor of the church, although the mayor refused to recognize the court decision in favor of the church. The mayor then issued a letter on 11 March 201184 in an attempt to cancel the permit for the second time. The church sent another letter to the Supreme Court requesting assistance. In June 2011, the Supreme Court issued a fatwa85 stating that its 2010 decision not to hear the case was final and that it could not hear the church’s concerns.
Having exhausted the legal system, the church leaders then complained to the national Ombudsman, despite the fact that the Ombudsman is designed to be used prior to going to court rather than to enforce a court order. On 8 July 2011, the Ombudsman issued a recommendation86 instructing the mayor to cancel the second letter it had issued, advised the Minister of Home Affairs to monitor the situation to ensure the church could begin building, and warned the mayor to comply with the 60-day deadline.87 The 60-day ultimatum lapsed and, given the failure of the mayor to respond adequately to the recommendations, the Ombudsman submitted its report to the legislature and the president,88 in the hope that it may lead to the impeachment of the mayor.
Since then, several attempts at the national level have been made by the legislature to mediate between the local government and the church. This dispute remains ongoing at the time this chapter was written and demonstrates the limited reach of the law without the political will to ensure law enforcement at the regional level of court decisions that are in favor of religious minorities.
Purwakarta Church Permit Case
Despite the inability to enforce court decisions, other churches have been encouraged by the legal victory in the Bogor Church Permit Case. For example, in 2009, the Santa Maria Catholic church of Bungursari took the regent of Purwakarta to the Administrative Court of Bandung (see Administrative Court, 2009b; High Administrative Court, 2010a). In the Purwakarta Church Permit Case, the church challenged the cancellation of its permit to build in the village of Cinangka, Purwakarta.89 On 2 February 2009, the church sent a letter to the Ministry of Religion and the Inter-religious Harmony Forum to request a recommendation. On 3 March, it also sent a letter to the regent to request a permit. On 10 March, the Forum gave its approval to the church proposal and on 8 April the regent issued a permit for the church. However, sometime between March and October, opposition to the church grew. In July, MUI sent a letter to the regent requesting cancellation of the permit. In October, the chairperson of the Forum, the Indonesia Ulama Forum and the Ministry of Religion did likewise. In response, the regent cancelled the permit based on the revised recommendations of the Forum and the Ministry. The Regent Dedi Mulyadi90 claimed his decision was based on the results of investigations and recommendations by the Inter-religious Harmony Forum and the Ministry of Religion in Purwakarta. Like the Depok Church Permit Case, this decision was made not long before local elections were held in 2010.
In February 2010, the church was successful at first instance because the court ruled that the decisions of the Forum and the Ministry are not a legitimate basis for cancellation of a permit. The regent appealed to the Administrative High Court of Jakarta and lost (High Administrative Court, 2010a). The church decided not to recommence building until it received a letter of execution from the court to ensure it had permission to continue due to concerns about acts of violence or retaliation from groups such as FPI.91 The lawyer for the church, Liona Supriatno, also suspected that those who opposed the church were affiliated with FPI.92 Similar to the Bogor Church Permit Case, the Purwakarta Church Permit Case is also an example of the way in which violence triumphs in the absence of a strong state and respect for the rule of law.
Bekasi Church Permit Case
In another case, in January 2010, the Protestant Christian Church of Bekasi93 took Mayor Sa’duddin to the Administrative Court of Bandung (Bekasi Church Permit Case). The church disputed the authority of the local government to seal off the construction site due to opposition from the local Muslim Communication Forum.94 In 2007, the church had purchased the 1,000-square meter block of land. Its application to the Bekasi Inter-religious Harmony Forum and the Bekasi regent remained unanswered for several years. Finally, on 13 January 2010, the church reported the case to Komnas HAM, and in April that year took its case to court, supported by a coalition of 31 Batak lawyers and one Muslim lawyer.
In 2000, the church began meeting in private homes to worship. As its numbers grew, it bought land in June 2007 to build a church. On 11 October 2007, the head of the village gave his recommendation. The Inter-religious Harmony Forum failed to respond to the church’s request for a permit. In 2008, several letters were sent to the mayor requesting the cancellation of the church’s permit, including from the village Muslim Communication Forum, the Betawi Brotherhood Forum (Forum Betawi Rempung, FBR),95 and leaders of the three local mosque congregations. On 18 August 2009, the Ministry declined to give consent due to opposition from the community based on the report of the head of the local subdistrict (Administrative Court, 2010a: 10).
On 25 December 2009, the church held a Christmas service on the land. Word that the church was meeting on the land ‘illegally’ was reported to the local government. On 3 January 2010, the mayor issued a decision informing the church that it must cease all activities on the vacant land. On 12 January, the land was sealed off to prevent church members from entering, according to the instructions of the mayor of Bekasi, who was supported by PKS. Some of the concerns cited by those who opposed the church were issues of crowding and traffic, rather than religious differences. The mayor of Bekasi had offered an alternative solution, to provide land for the three churches to use jointly, but the churches did not agree to this proposal.
The church decided to take its case to court. At the trial, witnesses for the church claimed that the local residents had agreed to the church in 2007.96 In 2010, however, local residents were then approached by three Muslim community leaders, Ustad Amil Anung, Ustad H Naimun and Maryadi, and forced to sign a letter requesting that the regent cancel the permit. These allegations of coercion were denied by Naimun and Maryadi, as witnesses for the regent, who claimed that they took this action because residents had been bribed into giving their consent to the church. Aside from these local residents, the vice-chairperson of the Inter-religious Harmony Forum also appeared in court. When questioned why the Forum did not respond to the permit application submitted by the church, he said it had delayed giving its recommendation as it was still waiting to receive the recommendation from the Ministry of Religion (Administrative Court, 2010a: 80). This was a bureaucratic response to mask its reluctance to make a decision on a politically sensitive issue.
All four court cases have occurred in the province of West Java. The choice of the courts as a forum for dispute resolution is placing increasing pressure on the judiciary to resolve these disputes. It appears that the Administrative Court has become an important forum to resolve disputes over the legality of decisions to cancel a permit.
Several trends are noticeable in these cases. First, all these churches had obtained permits to build and the permits were then cancelled. There are indications that in each case the permit was cancelled because of campaigns by radical Muslims, both locals as well as from groups such as FBR or FPI. Second, all decisions were made close to local elections, which indicates a responsiveness on the part of local politicians to the Muslim-majority electorate and a desire to maintain votes in a competitive political environment. Third, the inability of the newly established Inter-religious Harmony Forum to assist in resolving disputes has left some churches no option but to argue their case in court. The choice of the courts as a forum for dispute resolution has placed greater pressure on the judiciary to resolve these disputes. Even if a case is successful in court, these cases demonstrate that court decisions may have little practical effect in the face of continued threats of violence from radical Islamists, without the willingness of the state to protect religious minorities.
Judicial review in the Supreme Court
Aside from the Administrative Court, which hears cases of review of administrative decisions, the process of judicial review in the Supreme Court has been used to challenge the validity and scope of the Joint Regulation. There have been two attempts to challenge the Joint Decree 1/1969, and two attempts to challenge the Joint Regulation of 2006 in the courts. These cases were brought by religious leaders, but were indirectly supported by human rights groups and NGOs, including Indonesian Legal Aid. All these have failed because of the courts’ reluctance to depart from the official government position on permits and presumably because they do not want to be seen to favor religious minorities.
In 2005, the first request for judicial review of the Joint Decree 1/1969 was lodged with the Central Jakarta District Court by a team of 29 lawyers. This request for judicial review was a class action against the President of Indonesia, the Minister of Religion and the Minister of Home Affairs. The applicants relied on article 22 of Law 39/1999 on Human Rights and article 28E(1) of the Constitution on the right to religious freedom (Soefyanto, 2009: 105–6). They appealed to the ‘universal’ notion of the human right to freedom of worship. In response, the Minister of Religion emphasized the need for social order and criticized the vagueness and imprecision of the application and the lack of clarity as to ‘who’ was injured.
There was some debate about the different standards that exist due to the difference between national and regional regulations, which the applicant argued led to unequal standards across the archipelago. Several examples were given, such as the Regulation of the Governor of Riau No. 450.2/KS/9601 passed in 1981, a Decision of the Governor of Bengkulu No. 283/1993 on the Procedure and Conditions to Build a Place of Worship and to Spread the Teachings of a Religion, and a Decision of the Governor of Bali No. 33/2003 on the Procedure and Conditions to Build a Place of Worship. According to the Defense, these were permissible ways for provinces to implement and modify the 1969 Joint Decree according to local conditions (Soefyanto, 2009: 114). The Minister also quoted statistics on places of worship to show that the numbers have increased over the years in support of the argument that the Joint Decree has not impeded but rather promoted the construction of places of worship (Soefyanto, 2009: 116). The court never issued a decision in this case, however, because the new Joint Regulation passed in 2006 cancelled the old Joint Decree.
Independent of this case, in late 2005, a similar application for a request for judicial review of the Joint Decree 1/1969 was lodged with the Supreme Court by a team of three lawyers. The defendants in this action were the Minister of Religion and Minister of Home Affairs. The applicants also argued that the Joint Decree inhibited their constitutional right to religious freedom. Unlike the previous application, they claimed that the 1969 decree contradicted Law 28/2002 on the Construction of Buildings because that law does not specify that any further conditions are required to obtain a permit for religious buildings (Soefyanto, 2009: 121–22). On 22 November 2005, the Minister of Religion responded to the allegations, rejecting the application because the Joint Decree upholds the right to religious freedom, acts as a permissible restriction on this right, and is not opposed to the Constitution. The Supreme Court appears to have agreed, as the application was rejected in October 2006 (Supreme Court, 2006g).
Due to the introduction of the new Joint Regulation in 2006, a fresh request was lodged for review of the Joint Regulation with the Supreme Court by a team of 40 lawyers under the name the ‘Religious Freedom Defense Team.’97 The review was sought on the grounds that the Joint Regulation contravenes and unnecessarily restricts the constitutional right to freedom of religion. The application was lodged with the court in April 2006, less than three weeks after the new Joint Regulation was issued in March 2006.
The Religious Freedom Defense Team based its argument on the Constitution and the right to religious freedom, as well as on the rule of law and Law 39/1999 on Human Rights (Soefyanto, 2009: 141). They emphasized the injustice of discrimination based on the lines of religious majority and minority (Soefyanto, 2009: 144). They highlighted the illegal destruction of churches and expressed frustration at the lack of protection of rights (Soefyanto, 2009: 145–46). They claimed that conflicts over churches disturb the economic stability of the nation and foreign investment. They also highlighted several incidents of attacks on churches since the introduction of the Joint Regulation in March (Soefyanto, 2009: 147).
The Minister of Religion referred to jurisprudence of the Supreme Court, arguing that the court should not accept the application because it was unclear (Soefyanto, 2009: 149). It emphasized that the Joint Regulation was the result of ‘community participation,’ that is, it had been agreed by religious leaders of the six national religious councils. It referred to the 90/60 requirement and claimed that the process is fairer now because local regulations – such as the need for 40 families in Riau, 50 families in Central Sulawesi or 100 families in Bali – must now comply with the national regulation (Soefyanto, 2009: 159). On 28 February 2008, the court handed down a decision in which it agreed with the minister and rejected the application for review (Supreme Court, 2008b).
A few months later, on 13 October 2006, a similar request for judicial review of the Joint Regulation was lodged with the Supreme Court by a team of 25 lawyers from the Christian political party PDS of East Java (Supreme Court, 2006f). In addition to the argument that it was against the right to religious freedom, the applicants asserted that the Joint Regulation was inconsistent with Law 10/2004 on Law-making, because a ministerial regulation is not listed as a recognized form of law according to the Indonesian hierarchy of laws. The applicants also argued that the requirement to provide proof of 90 members of a church by submitting copies of congregation members’ identity card discriminated against children who do not have an identity card and so are treated as if they do not have a right to a place of worship. In regards to the 90/60 requirement, they claimed this requirement is too difficult for Christians to meet, and that it is easier to build a nightclub in Indonesia than a place of worship (Soefyanto, 2009: 172). They also made submissions regarding the difficulty of obtaining a permit from local government, and instances of opposition to the construction of churches without good reason (Soefyanto, 2009: 173).
The minister argued that the Joint Ministerial Regulation was a valid legal instrument (Soefyanto, 2009: 178), and that limitations on the right to religious freedom were permitted in certain circumstances. He emphasized that:
It must be remembered that there is no freedom that is unlimited, but all freedom is limited. Freedom may be limited by the freedom of others, by customs, by laws and by the teachings of religions.
(Soefyanto, 2009: 180)
The minister also relied on the fact that the issue of the number of people needed for a permit was debated at length by religious leaders during the drafting process, before the 90/60 requirement was finally agreed (Soefyanto, 2009: 181). On 13 February 2008, the court again agreed with the government, although it offered little reasoning for its decision, rejected the application and left the Joint Regulation 2006 in force.
The court’s blanket agreement with the position of the Minister of Religion in these cases suggests that it is conscious to keep in line with government policy on this issue. It must be acknowledged, however, that the applicants’ case was weak at best, given that the regulation of permits for places of worship is a common feature in many countries. Nevertheless, the court could have considered whether particular provisions, such as the requirement that an application have the signatures of 90 members and 60 local residents, infringed on the right to religious freedom.
Conclusion
The Depok Church Permit Case highlights the persistent fears of Islamists concerning the construction of churches, which is considered to be an act of proselytization or an attempt to gain new converts. This had led to a trend in local authorities using conflict over places of worship as opportunities for political gain in the highly competitive political atmosphere since the downfall of Soeharto in 1998. Capitalizing on local fears of ‘Christianization’ in Muslim-majority areas, local authorities are responding to the concerted campaigns against churches by radical Islamic groups by cancelling church permits.
This chapter has demonstrated the pressure some religious minorities face to build places of worship – both Muslims and Christians, depending on the context. The number of churches that have faced resistance in the first ten years of democracy now exceeds the number attacked or threatened during the entire 30 years of Soeharto’s New Order. The nature of the opposition to churches, however, has changed over time. From the late 1990s up until the early 2000s, large numbers of churches were violently attacked in one day, as occurred in 1996 in Situbondo and Tasikmalaya. Since 2001, it is more common for radical Islamic groups to wage specific campaigns of threats and violence against individual churches.
Violent attacks on churches were particularly acute in 2004 to 2005 and led to calls for reform, from Christians and Muslims. The introduction of the Joint Regulation in 2006 made two major changes to the system to obtain a permit for a place of worship that are favorable to religious majorities in an area. The first major change was the establishment of the Inter-religious Harmony Forum, which only added another layer of bureaucracy to the already difficult process to obtain a permit. Although it allows for the participation of religious leaders in the application process, decisions can be easily manipulated by the religious majority. The second change is the requirement that an applicant must obtain the signatures of 90 congregation members and the signatures of 60 local residents. Even if satisfied, it may be subject to claims of corruption and bribery from radical Islamic groups that oppose the church.
From this examination of allegations of ‘Christianization’ leveled at churches, there are common themes in the arguments used to oppose plans by Christians to build a church. This may include that a church should not be built in a ‘Muslim area’ and that a stricter permit process is necessary as a gate-keeping function to protect the community. If there is already a church in the area, a proposal for another church may be seen as unnecessary and overlook the denominational fragmentation within the Christian community that is partly the cause of multiple church buildings in one area. On a more practical level, there are of course planning issues, such as traffic and parking, as well as issues of economic disparity between religious communities. Finally, the building of churches raises deep-seated fears about issues of ‘SARA,’ including ethnic differences and the fear of foreign intervention in the domestic affairs of the state.
The Depok Church Permit Case is remarkable because it is one of the first examples of a church seeking review of an administrative decision in court after its permit to build has been cancelled. It is even more significant that the Batak Church, which belongs to the largest Protestant denomination in Indonesia, actually succeeded at first instance, on appeal and in the Supreme Court. It highlights the ways in which local politics complicates the process to obtain and keep a permit, as well as the difficulties of enforcing a court decision without the active support of local authorities. Nevertheless, the move by Christian churches, such as the Batak Church, to assert their rights through democratic processes and legal institutions, particularly the courts, is significant and suggests an increase in confidence in the legal system to resolve religious disputes.
The church in the Depok Church Permit Case was successful in challenging the decision of local authorities to cancel its permit in court. Its legal victory, however, remains precarious. In November 2010, the church remained under construction, and had erected a sign with the court decisions printed on it at the front of the property. This was a way of displaying evidence of its legal victory, because progress in the construction of the building continued to be threatened by radical Islamic groups. Such cases of churches fighting to retain their permits in the face of opposition from radical Islamists and local authorities demonstrates that legal decisions may have little practical effect without the enforcement of, and respect for, court decisions by all sections of society. The role of local authorities and law enforcement agencies is crucial to this process. The unwillingness of local authorities to implement national regulations if it may compromise their political power, and the lack of protection offered to religious minorities, has the potential to undermine the processes of democracy and law reform in Indonesia.

6  Insulting a religion
Cases of blasphemy in Indonesia intersect with the politics of religious conversion, especially from Islam to Christianity, and the use of criminal law to suppress religious dissent. The criminal offence of blasphemy, inserted as article 156a into the Criminal Code in 1965, makes it a crime to dishonor or insult a religion in Indonesia; this is commonly referred to in public debates as the ‘Blasphemy Law’ (see Chapter 2). Since 1998, there has been a sharp rise in accusations of insults to Islam and ensuing court cases. This chapter primarily focuses on incidents in which Christians have been accused and/or convicted of blaspheming Islam.1 There have also been many cases in which people who identify as Muslims have been convicted for blaspheming Islam, or people who identify as Christians have been convicted for blaspheming Christianity.2 These cases involve disputes internal to a religion, rather than a dispute between religions, so I only deal with them briefly to illustrate broader trends in the application of the Blasphemy Law.
This chapter explores how fears that conversions to Christianity will destabilize social order and political power underline criminal prosecutions for blasphemy in Indonesia. Between 1998 and 2012, over 130 people were taken to court on charges of blasphemy. About half of the accused in these cases have been Christians.3 This is because cases of blasphemy often raise concerns over the limits of proselytization and efforts to convert people to Christianity. The Blasphemy Law has been used to imprison leaders of groups considered to be ‘deviant,’ to condemn converts between religions, and to silence what are considered to be unorthodox interpretations of the six official religions, primarily Islam and, to a lesser extent, Christianity. There are no legal criteria for what constitutes ‘deviancy’ in Indonesia, although the issue is usually determined by the relevant national religious council.
This chapter conducts a close analysis of the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, and places it in context by considering the history of radical Islam in Tasikmalaya and the support for regional regulations based on Islam. It also considers broader patterns in cases of blasphemy, particularly in cases where former Muslims have been prosecuted for blaspheming Islam. It concludes with an examination of the 2010 decision of the Constitutional Court, which upheld the validity of the Blasphemy Law. Overall, the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case indicates that Indonesia’s Blasphemy Law has been used like an apostasy law by Islamists to condemn and punish Muslims who have converted out of Islam to Christianity.4
Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case
One of the first cases since 1998 in which a convert from Islam to Christianity was convicted for insulting Islam was the case of Abraham Bentar Rohadi. In 2006, he was found guilty of insulting Islam under article 156a of the Criminal Code by the District Court of the City of Tasikmalaya. This case is intimately related to Abraham’s religious and family ties, and the social context of Tasikmalaya. Abraham was born into a devout Muslim family in the village of Nagarasari, Tasikmalaya. In 1982, at the age of 32, Abraham moved from Tasikmalaya to Bandung with his wife. His relatives soon lost contact with him and claim to have heard that he had passed away (Supreme Court, 2006a). In 1998, while in Bandung, Abraham converted from Islam to Christianity after a pastor helped him recover from a stroke. In 1999, he was baptized and then studied for three years at a Christian theological college.
In 2000, after an absence of 18 years, Abraham returned to visit his hometown and informed his relatives that he had changed religion and become Christian. This unwelcome news, combined with his long period of absence, placed a strain on relations with his relatives. Abraham visited his family several times in the following years. From 2003 up until the time of the trial, he was employed by a church (District Court, 2006i). The court described his role as an Ivenjalis or Penginjil, that is, a Christian ‘Evangelist’ (District Court, 2006a). Meanwhile, the situation grew increasingly tense between him and his family.
Finally, on 6 March 2006, his brother-in-law Usman Suryadi and his nephew Asep Aris went to the home of Agun Gunawan, the head of neighborhood 13 (rukun warga, RW), to report Abraham on allegations of insulting Islam several times over the past few years. Agun then coordinated a meeting with the local leader of the Indonesia Council of Ulama (MUI) and a local ustad (Islamic teacher) Latief Amien, and all agreed that Abraham should be reported to the police (District Court, 2006a: 1). It is unlikely to have been a coincidence that just two days later, on 8 March 2006, Abraham was attacked in Tasikmalaya by a large crowd of radical Muslims and severely beaten. The crowd also set fire to his van. It was Abraham, however, and not the perpetrators of the attack, who was arrested by police, partly for his own safety. He was subsequently charged with the criminal offence of insulting Islam.
On 17 May 2006, the trial began. The primary charge brought against him was that, sometime between 2001 and 2006, he blasphemed Islam (District Court, 2006i: 3–4). He was accused of having stated that:
The religion of Islam is a religion of darkness. The Prophet Muhammad is not the final prophet, rather the final prophet is Isa [Jesus]. The Prophet Muhammad is a liar … All followers of Islam are barbaric …
(District Court, 2006a: 19)
No date, time or place at which this statement was alleged to have been said was specified by the Prosecution. The Prosecution further alleged that he defamed Islam in a separate incident in December 2004, while speaking at the Pentecostal Church of Tasikmalaya. He was quoted as saying that:
The religion of Islam is a religion of darkness and its teachings cannot be understood. After a person converts to Christianity, their wealth will increase and they will have a house, a car, rice fields and land.
(District Court, 2006a: 4)
This suggested that Abraham may follow a Christian theology that upholds the so-called ‘prosperity doctrine,’ a belief particularly among some wealthy churches that God will bless His followers with material possessions. Abraham admitted to having said the two statements above, although he later claimed that he made this admission under force. He nevertheless apologized at the court hearing if his words had offended anyone.
The physical evidence brought before the court included two Bibles, a car, some religious books and two VCDs on Christianity. Abraham admitted the books were his but claimed they were for his personal study only. The Prosecution asserted that these books had been left by Abraham at his brother-in-law’s house with the intention of converting him, and claimed this contravened the prohibition on spreading religious teachings through the distribution of pamphlets or publications.5 This argument suggests that a breach of the Ministerial Regulation on Proselytization can be relied on as evidence to convict converts who insult Islam.
Only two short court hearings took place before Abraham was sentenced to four and a half years in prison. Local churches in the area did not wish to condone his behavior and so most were not present at the trial. The only pastor who attended the trial out of concern for Abraham’s safety was Pastor Andreas Setiawan, who described Abraham as ‘immature’ in his faith and quick to denigrate other religions such as Islam.6
In May 2006, Abraham lodged an appeal with the Bandung High Court. The court acknowledged that the issue in this case was really between family members and reduced his sentence to three years and six months (High District Court, 2006b). In September 2006, Abraham lodged an appeal with the Supreme Court (Supreme Court, 2006c). The appeal was rejected by the Supreme Court, although it did acknowledge that the case also involved a dispute over inheritance between family members (Supreme Court, 2006a). On 27 April 2008, Abraham was released on parole after serving two years in prison.
Historical trends in court cases
During the New Order (1966–98), article 156a of the Criminal Code was relied on to prosecute fewer than ten cases in court.7 All cases brought to court during this time related to disputes within Islam, rather than disputes between religions. There was one conviction in 1968, although in the 20 years that followed (1969 to 1990) there were no known convictions for blasphemy, perhaps because of the extent to which Soeharto controlled public or political expressions of religion. Since 1990, there are two main types of cases that have been prosecuted under article 156a of the Criminal Code.
In the first type of case, some people were convicted for being responsible for publications that were considered to have insulted Islam, such as occurred in the Monitor Case in 1990. On 15 October 1990, an article featured in Monitor, a popular magazine owned by the Catholic publishing company Gramedia. It included a survey of the most inspiring figures, with the Prophet Muhammad ranked 11th (Rumadi, 2007: 23–24). This led to protests organized by MUI, the Indonesian Islamic Preaching Council (Dewan Dakwah Islamiyah Indonesia), the Indonesian Committee for Global Muslim Solidarity (Komite Indonesia untuk Solidaritas Dunia Islam), the Muslim Students Association (Himpunan Mahasiswa Islam) and the youth wing of Muhammadiyah, which considered it offensive that the Prophet Muhammad was only listed 11th (Hefner, 2000: 161). One week later, on 23 October, the tabloid was banned and the Minister for Information cancelled its publishing license. Hefner (2000: 176–77) argues that evidence suggests the real protagonist was Din Syamsuddin (the current chairperson of Muhammadiyah 2009–14), who mobilized the Muslim community against Arswendo. The editor of the magazine, Arswendo Atmowiloto, a Catholic, was brought to trial for allowing the article to appear in the magazine. On 25 October 1990, he was found guilty of breaching article 156a of the Criminal Code and jailed for five years by the Central Jakarta District Court (Steenbrink, 2006: 148).8 He appealed to the provincial High Court and then to the Supreme Court, where his sentence was reduced by six months.9 A report of the Attorney-General noted the Monitor Case as one example where ‘repressive action’ was necessary (Kejaksaan Agung, 1995: ix). Aside from religious sentiments, there were also political tensions underlying this case. According to Hefner:
The message the government sought to send through the Monitor Affair was that the New Order’s old alliance with Javanists was over … henceforth the president would act as a champion of conservative Muslim interests against Javanists, Christians, and other minorities.
(Hefner, 2000: 161)
Aside from convictions of people who were held responsible for publications deemed insulting, the second type of case involved Muslims who were accused of blaspheming Islam because they make jokes about Islam using Javanese plesetan, that is, parody, a play on words or pun. Plesetan comes from the Indonesian word terpleset, literally to slip or fall. The first recorded case of this kind is the Sastra Case. On 8 August 1968, an anonymous short story was published in Sastra magazine titled ‘Langit Makin Mendung,’ ‘The Sky Darkens’ (Dahlan and Hermani, 2004). This short story caused controversy because of its imaginative depictions of God, the prophets, the Archangel Gabriel and Islamic religious leaders, which were perceived as disrespectful and an insult to Islam.10 In addition, the story was written in a style known as sembarangan, which is when an author presents serious matters – in this case Islam – in ‘a casual and careless manner’ (Morgan, 1974: ii). Large protests against the publication of the story were held in North Sumatra and Jakarta. The Ministry of Religion issued a press release that condemned the publication (Morgan, 1974: i). The editor of Sastra magazine, Hans Bague Jassin, a Muslim, refused to divulge the true identity of the author. Although some suspected that the short story was written by Jassin himself, the author of the story was referred to by the pseudonym ‘Kipanjikusmin,’ reportedly a Muslim who had received a Catholic education (Rumadi, 2007:20). Due to the anonymity of the author and Jassin’s unwillingness to name the author, Jassin was brought to court for allowing the article to be published in his magazine. Jassin was sentenced to one year in jail for breach of article 156a of the Criminal Code.
The next case, the Salatiga Case, did not occur until 1992. Djoni Purwoto, 22, and Sugiri Cahyono, 29, were accused of insulting Islam for the jokes they made about verses from the Qur’an. They transformed the Arabic phrase ‘Kun fa-yakun,’ ‘[God’s command is] Be and it is’ (Q 36:82) into the Indonesian phrase ‘Tak ada singkong ada sukun telor ayam lebih kecil dari telur kalkun’ (there is no cassava but there is breadfruit, a chicken egg is smaller than a turkey egg). This was a play on the sound of the syllable ‘kun’ in Arabic and Indonesian. Similarly, they exchanged the Arabic phrase ‘Amin, ya Robbal Alamin,’ ‘Amen, Lord of the Universe’ to the Indonesian phrase which sounds similar ‘Amin, ya gombal alamin,’ ‘Amen, thanks be to God the liar.’ Although intended as a joke, these puns were considered by some to be offensive and insulting to Muslims, and the students were put on trial under article 156a of the Criminal Code. Purwoto was sentenced to four years’ jail and Cahyono to three and a half years’ jail by the District Court of Salatiga, Central Java (Aqsha et al., 1995: 171–72; HRW, 1994a).
In 1992, a second plesetan case occurred in Yogyakarta. Two students of Gadjah Mada University, R. Bambang Wahyu Nirbito, 29, and Ambar Widi, 26, were prosecuted for insulting Islam in the District Court of Yogyakarta (High District Court, 1992). They were accused of making disrespectful jokes about Islam while hosting a concert. Nirbito was quoted as saying ‘Ayat kursi disebut ayat Kursi dengen mebelnys,’ ‘The Throne Verse is known as the Chair verse with furniture.’ Nirbito’s quip was considered offensive because it trivialized one of the most important verses of the Qur’an, reducing it to a matter of household furniture. Ayat kursi (Q 2:225), known as the Throne Verse, is one of the most well-known verses in the Qur’an. According to Federspiel (1995: 25–26), it is ‘a popular verse often recited by Muslims in times of danger and sorrow’ because God does not rest and is available to help with problems. Other interpretations of this verse promote the belief that if it is read before you sleep, God will send two angels to watch over you until the morning.11 Widi was quoted as replying ‘Surat Ar Rachman ayat 16 bunyinya Walaa Taqrabuzina artinya jangan kamu mendekati zina,’ ‘Ar-Rachman verse 16 says Walaa Taqrabuzina meaning do not commit adultery.’ This was a reference to Q 55:16 (‘Then which of the favors of your Lord will you deny?’) and Q 17:32 (‘Do not draw near to fornication, for it is an indecency, and its way is evil’). Although this statement appears to be against adultery, the Prosecution asserted that it was offensive because it was a play on the word bunyi which means ‘sound/noise’ but can also mean ‘the contents of’ or ‘meaning.’ The Prosecution argued that the implied meaning was ‘nikmatilah zina sepuas-puasnya dan dengan ucapan,’ ‘enjoy adultery as much as possible and with moans and groans.’ Two days after the concert, on 4 May, a demonstration was held demanding that the two students be prosecuted. At the trial, Nirbito admitted that he often performed plesetan, but he did not usually use verses from the Qur’an. The two students were sentenced under article 156a of the Criminal Code to two and a half years’ jail for their performance; their appeal was unsuccessful (High District Court, 1992).
Another incident was the Permadi Case in 1995.12 Permadi Satrio Wiwoho was a well-known Javanese mystic, a supporter of Megawati, lawyer and commentator on public affairs from Solo, Central Java. He was accused of not only insulting President Soeharto and Golkar, the state-sponsored political party, but of slandering the Prophet Muhammad (Hefner, 2000: 176). These allegations were initially raised in a statement from Din Syamsuddin13 published in a newspaper. In addition, the head of MUI KH Hasan Basri, who had ties to DDII, declared that Permadi had insulted Islam. When questioned about his statement, Hasan admitted he had not actually listened to the recording in which Permadi had allegedly insulted the Prophet, but had relied on a transcript provided to him by Din Syamsuddin, which raised questions about whether Din Syamsuddin had tampered with the recording to fabricate evidence against Permadi (Hefner, 2000: 178). Nevertheless, in August 1995, Permadi was tried in court under article 156a of the Criminal Code. He was convicted and sentenced to eight months’ jail.
Aside from cases of blasphemy through the media, plesetan performances or public speeches, other convictions for insulting Islam have led to violence against Chinese Christians, as occurred in the Saleh Case. In September 1996, Saleh, a young Muslim student from Situbondo (East Java), was reported by KH Achmad Zaini, the leader of Nurul Hikam pesantren, to the police for declaring that Muhammad was not the final prophet and therefore for promoting beliefs that ‘deviated’ from Islam (Rumadi, 2007: 25–37). When the police arrested Saleh, it was partly for his own protection (Purdey, 2006: 44). At the court hearing, rumors spread that Saleh had fled to a nearby church, Bukti Sion Indonesian Bethel Church. The crowds who had turned out to protest against Saleh then rushed to the nearby church and burnt it down, along with 23 other churches, some Christian schools and a number of Chinese shops. Five people were also killed. On 3 October, Saleh was convicted under article 156a of the Criminal Code and received a five-year jail term (Steenbrink, 2006: 149). Although this was essentially a case of blasphemy internal to Islam, it led to violent attacks against Christians. The perpetrators of this attack were never prosecuted.
In January 1997, the Tjio Kim Tjoan Case occurred in Rengasdengklok, West Java, although with racial rather than religious overtones. Early one morning, a Chinese Indonesian couple, Tjio Kim Tjoan and his wife Cik Gue, asked a group of young Muslims to reduce the level of noise of the call to prayer from the mosque. During his confrontation with the youths, Tjio Kim Tjoan was accused of insulting them by calling them ‘dogs,’ although he denied this. A dispute broke out and as news of the alleged blasphemous incident spread a crowd of Muslim youths began to rally, destroying churches, Chinese-Indonesian shops, a Confucian hall and a Buddhist temple. Complaints were made against Tjio Kim Tjoan to the police. At the trial, 600 military and police officers guarded the courtroom due to the presence of radical Islamic groups calling for the conviction of Tjio Kim Tjoan. On 7 April 1997, the Karawang District Court sentenced Kim under article 156a of the Criminal Code to three and a half years in jail for insulting Islam (ISAI, 1997: 45–46).
Article 156a of the Criminal Code was therefore only used to prosecute those who have insulted Islam a handful of times under Soeharto, mostly after 1990. These cases were marked by political undertones, and the volatile political atmosphere under the New Order. There has been an increase in blasphemy convictions since 1998, and several trends can be discerned in these cases.
Characteristics of cases post-1998
There has been a marked increase in the number of cases of insulting a religion that have been taken to the courts. From 1998 to 2012, over 50 people who identified as Muslim have been convicted for insulting Islam. In the majority of cases taken to court the accused are found guilty.14 These cases evince a pattern of small religious minorities being targeted by Islamists for a wide range of actions classified as an ‘offence against religion,’ without the same political undertones as the cases that took place under Soeharto’s New Order.
The first noticeable trend is that the groups or individuals targeted for blaspheming Islam are usually small, local groups with no international networks or support, which makes them easy targets. One example of this is the Lia Eden community, also known as Salamullah or God’s Kingdom of Eden (Tahta Suci Kerajaan Eden). This community began in 1995 under the leadership of Lia Eden, formerly known as Lia Aminuddin, who claims to have had an encounter with the Archangel Gabriel. The movement is based in Jakarta and had only about 50 members in 2005 (Rumadi, 2007: 54–57).15 Three leaders from this group have been convicted for blaspheming Islam under article 156a of the Criminal Code.
In the first case in 2006, Lia Eden was accused of claiming that she herself is the Archangel Gabriel and receives revelations from God, which she declared in letters sent to MUI and the Ministry of Religion (District Court, 2006d). She proclaimed one of her followers, Muhammad Abdul Rachman, as the reincarnation of the Prophet Muhammad. She also taught that it is permissible to pray in two languages and to eat pork. There were also allegations that Lia Eden had abused a child by threatening to burn the child’s mouth in 2005. In the same trial, Muhammad Abdul Rachman was also convicted for claiming to be the Prophet Muhammad (District Court, 2006g).
Then in 2009, Lia Eden was convicted (for the second time) for communicating the revelations she received in a letter sent to all major government departments and Islamic organizations in Indonesia, including the president. In the letter, she argued that Islam as a religion should be dissolved, that all religions should unite and that they should all pray in one direction (but not to Mecca) (District Court, 2009b: 3). One of her followers, Wahyu Andito Putro Wibisono, was also convicted under article 156a of the Criminal Code because of his role in distributing the letters (District Court, 2009a).
One of the reasons these individuals were convicted is because the Lia Eden community has very few followers, it is only based in Jakarta and has no international networks. Other accused, such as Elldein Osvaldo Lubis of Pondok Ungu Permai, Bekasi, do not even have any followers (Republika, 3 January 2009). By contrast, the followers of other groups such as Ahmadiyah have not yet been accused of blasphemy in court because it is one of the few religious minorities in Indonesia that is a global movement with international networks.
A second feature of many cases is that these groups or individuals are usually targeted by religious leaders from hardline groups that have issued a fatwa against the individual or group and then report the case to the police. MUI16 has often issued fatawa against minority groups in an attempt to suppress deviant teachings that present a challenge to Islamic doctrine and to its authority as religious leaders. At the local level, MUI have issued fatawa against groups that have resulted in the conviction of members of those groups.17 For example, in 1997, a fatwa against Lia Eden and her teachings was issued by the national Ministry of Religion (2003: 114–27).
This also occurred in the ‘YKNCA Case’ (Drug and Cancer Foundation, Yayasan Kanker dan Narkoba Cahaya Alam). A fatwa was issued by MUI against the leader, Ardhi Husein, of a healing center in Probolinggo known as YKNCA, and some of its members, and then a case was later brought to court (District Court, 2005c). A book published by the center, ‘Piercing the Darkness Towards the Light 2,’ ‘Menembus Gelap Menuju Terang 2’ (MGMT 2), was considered to be insulting to Islam because its interpretation of verses from the Qur’an was considered to be problematic. For example, the book claimed there were still prophets (implying that Muhammad was not the last prophet), that Satan has more faith than humans, that Islam is only for Arabs, and that to enter heaven you do not have to be Muslim (District Court, 2005c: 12). They were sentenced to three years’ jail for breach of article 156a of the Criminal Code.
The court has often relied on the fatwa of MUI as justification for its decision. The reliance by the prosecution of fatawa as evidence in court is difficult for the court to deny without questioning the credibility of the Islamic leaders. Of course, as discussed in Chapter 3, a fatwa from MUI is not legally binding in Indonesia, so the extent to which it is followed varies. In the Whistling Case, for example, the MUI of Polewali (South Sulawesi) declared whistling deviant and then reported Sumardin Tappaya, a high school Islamic religious teacher, to the police for insulting Islam because he whistled during Friday prayers (MUI, 2006). He was later sentenced to six months in prison for breach of article 156a of the Criminal Code. In addition to fatawa, in 2007, MUI issued guidelines on how to determine whether religious teachings are ‘deviant,’ for example, if a group disputes the authenticity of the Qur’an or changes religious rituals set down by syariah.18 The guidelines have been widely circulated to the Office of the Public Prosecutor, the Ministry of Religion and local government at the provincial and local levels.
The third characteristic of these cases is that allegations of blasphemy are often easily proven because the definition of blasphemy under the Criminal Code in Indonesia is broad and has been interpreted widely by the courts. It can include any act, belief, or utterance vaguely considered to be insulting to a religion. It must be noted that it is rare for the decisions of the District Courts (Pengadilan Negeri), the lowest court in the Indonesian hierarchy, to contain detailed explanations or reasoning for its decisions in any cases that it decides. Instead, the court usually refers briefly to the three elements of the offence, as contained in article 156a of the Criminal Code, and then examines whether it has been satisfied in a case. The first element, ‘a person,’ simply emphasizes that only an individual can be convicted for blasphemy and not an organization.
