Second, the act must have been done ‘deliberately in public.’ However, the emphasis is not on whether the act was intentional, but rather whether it was done ‘in public.’ ‘Public’ has been is defined as ‘a place that is easy for people (the public) to see and to be visited by many people’ (District Court 2006d: 54). The act must occur in a place that ‘can easily be seen by the public,’ or at least is not ‘closed to the public’ (District Court 2006f: 39). The third element is that the person must be found to ‘express feelings or behave in a manner that involves hatred, misuse or insulting of a religion followed in Indonesia.’ This has been interpreted to include any act, belief, or utterance vaguely considered to be insulting to a religion.
Behavior that has been determined to be insulting to Islam by a court includes claims that a person is the Prophet Muhammad or a prophet (after the Prophet Muhammad) and has received revelations from God. As mentioned earlier, Abdul Rahman, a member of the Lia Eden community, was charged with blasphemy because he believed he was the reincarnation of the Prophet Muhammad (District Court, 2006f, 2006g, 2008e). Another example is Ahmad Mushaddeq, the leader of Al-Qiyadah al-Islamiyah, who claimed to be a prophet after the Prophet Muhammad and taught his followers that it was not necessary to pray five times a day or make the pilgrimage to Mecca (High District Court, 2008). He was sentenced to four years’ jail for breach of article 156a of the Criminal Code.
Other behavior considered to be insulting to Islam is the refusal of groups that claim to be within Islam to practice one of the five pillars of Islam19 or other teachings considered fundamental to Islam. For example, in 2009, Agus Imam Solichin, leader of Satrio Piningit Weteng Buwono, a group that claimed to be ‘Islamic,’ was sentenced under article 156a of the Criminal Code to two and a half years by the South Jakarta District Court because he taught his group that it was not necessary to pray five times per day or to fast (Jakarta Post, 30 July 2009). A group is particularly vulnerable if it publishes its teachings, with many groups convicted on the basis of such evidence. For example, in the Lia Eden Case (No. 2), over 83 brochures, books and fatawa produced by the Eden community were used as evidence against Lia Eden (District Court, 2009a).
Finally, another key aspect of these cases is that the accused are often forced to ‘repent’ of the act or opinion considered ‘deviant’ and convert to Islam. The generally accepted view in Islam is that repentance of blasphemy is acceptable, though it must be done willingly and cannot be forced (Saeed and Saeed, 2004: 54). Often, however, the accused are pressured or coerced to ‘return’ to Islam. Even if they do, they are usually still found guilty of blasphemy in court. This occurred in the case of Yusman Roy, a Muslim accused of praying incorrectly for promoting bilingual prayer in Indonesian and Arabic.20 Although bilingual prayer is not prohibited in the Qur’an, it is a contentious issue among the different schools of Islam (mazhab). In Indonesia, the hadith by Bukhari, ‘Pray as you have seen me praying’ is relied on to support the view that prayer must be conducted in Arabic.21 Returning to the issue of conversion, Yusman Roy was strongly ‘encouraged’ by the head of Nahdlatul Ulama (NU), Hasyim Muzadi, to return to the teachings of the Qur’an (Republika, 9 May 2005).22 In the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, Abraham claimed that while he was in custody he had been visited several times by representatives of MUI who tried to force him to say the syahadat (creed) and return to Islam. There were also calls for Lia Eden to return to Islam (Republika, 6 June 2009).
The pressure to repent and return to Islam has come from Islamic organizations such as MUI, NU or Muhammadiyah.23 An example of this was the case of Ahmad Mushaddeq, whose community was reported to have ‘repented’ and ‘returned’ to Islam (Republika, 28 February 2008). In December 2007, a two-day discussion was held between government officials and religious leaders, including from NU, with Mushaddeq. After those two days, Ahmad had ‘repented.’ A similar incident took place with members of Al-Qiyadah al-Islamiyah in other areas such as Gresik (West Java) and Makassar (South Sulawesi), who were reported to have signed statements of confession to confirm they had returned to the true teachings of Islam (Constitutional Court, 2010c: 19). Aside from these cases, there has also been a large number of Christians who have been accused and/or convicted of insulting Islam.
Christians and converts to Christianity: insulting Islam
Under the New Order, there were several highly controversial cases of public religious conversions from Islam to Christianity that led to accusations of blasphemy. Between 1998 and 2012, there were over 60 Christians convicted under article 156a of the Criminal Code for insulting Islam or Christianity. I discuss these cases in detail here because these controversies have shaped relations between Muslims and Christians in Indonesia.
Muslim converts to Christianity
Two converts to Christianity who generated national public controversy were Jusuf Roni (1974)24 and Hamran Ambrie (1982). The accusations of blasphemy were intimately tied to the accused’s conversion from Islam to Christianity. The decision to convert was perceived as a threat by the Muslim community because both men previously held well-respected positions within the Muslim community and it was feared that it could lead to more conversions to Christianity. These cases are of concern to many Islamists as they are remembered with a sense of shame and embarrassment.
The first well-known case in Indonesia of a Muslim who converted to Christianity being imprisoned for behavior that could have been classified as insulting Islam under the Blasphemy Law was Jusuf Roni.25 Jusuf was born into a pious Muslim family in Palembang, South Sumatra, and as a child attended a pesantren, Islamic boarding school. He claims to have been actively involved in the Muslim community while growing up, including as a member of the Islamic Dakwah Mission and the Islamic Youth Organization (Roni, 2006: 11). His brother Kemas A. Roni was a well-known Islamic religious teacher in Palembang (Roni, 2006: 10). From 1971–72, Yusuf Roni undertook comparative religious studies in Bandung, West Java, seeking to find the weaknesses in the Christian faith. Jusuf Roni claims that he then converted from Islam to Christianity in his twenties as a result of his studies, not because of the efforts of any particular individual or church.26 In April 1973, he was baptized and soon after that began sharing his story occasionally in churches in Bandung (West Java), Surabaya (East Java), Kalimantan and Jakarta (Roni, 2006: 23, 30–31).
Word of his conversion spread quickly and many Muslims were shocked by his speech ‘The story of how I chose Christ as my Savior’ (Gatra, 5 May 2001). Yusuf Roni earned a reputation as a pastor who explained and criticized Islam from the perspective of Christianity. This was insulting to Islamists who were angered by his critical depiction of Islam. As a result of the tension and public controversy his teachings generated, on 15 June 1974, he was arrested in Malang by the East Java Special Regional Implementation of Operational Command (Kopkamtib).27 He had only been in Malang since January of that year to undertake further biblical studies at the Indonesian Evangelical Institute (Roni, 2006: 31). He was placed under indefinite detention in a military detention house (rumah tahanan militer). Three weeks later, the government issued a warrant authorizing Operation Commando, a division of public order and safety of the local government of East Java, to arrest him.28
Jusuf Roni spent five years in detention without trial before his case proceeded to court. He was convicted for intentionally inciting hostility in the community and received a six-year jail term.29 On 4 April 1980, just several months after the court decision was handed down, Jusuf Roni was released from jail as his sentence had been served (Letter of Release, 1980). On his release from jail, Jusuf Roni was warned not to preach a sermon, conduct religious ceremonies or publish his work. At Christmas, he was only permitted to attend a church that was tightly monitored by the government.
Since 1998, Jusuf Roni has experienced greater freedom of expression and assembly. In 2001, he founded Gereja Protestan Indonesia (Indonesian Protestant Church), an independent denomination he describes as ‘Judeo-Christianity.’ He also established a theological training center, the Jusuf Roni Center, and has published his work.30 His church, however, is still unable to obtain a permit to build a theological school. It purchased a block of land in Jakarta over 25 years ago, but has been unable to obtain the necessary permission from the community and local authorities. Further, Jusuf Roni has faced criticism from both Muslims and some Christians. For example, the leader of the Christian Peace and Prosperous Party (PDS), Ruyadi Hutasoit, felt that ‘his [Jusuf Roni’s] sermons are very vulgar [kasar] and insulting to Islam.’31 Theophilus Bela, chairperson of the Jakarta Christian Communication Forum, labeled cases like Yusuf Roni as ‘very inflammatory’ and perceived by Muslims as an ‘attack’ on Islam.32 These opinions are a reflection of the broad diversity of approaches within Christianity in Indonesia.
In the 1970s and 1980s, Hamran Ambrie was also accused of blasphemy, although, unlike Jusuf Roni, he died before he was ever imprisoned for or convicted of an offence. Hamran Ambrie was born on 13 January 1921 into a Muslim family in Amuntai, South Kalimantan. As a child, he attended a school run by Muhammadiyah. He was an active member of Muhammadiyah and a preacher of Islam. In 1947, he was chosen to be the chairperson of the Kalimantan Muslim Congress in Amuntai, together with KH Idham Chalid.33 In 1950–51, he served in the army in Banjarmasin as an Islamic religious leader. He later worked also a journalist and writer, with articles published in Islamic magazines such as Mingguan Adil in Solo, Mingguan Risalah Jihad in Jakarta, and Mingguan Anti-Komunis in Bandung. He supported an anti-Christian movement in Muara Teweh (Central Kalimantan) and, up until 1962, sympathized with groups that aimed to establish an Islamic state in Indonesia.34
In 1962, he claimed to have come to know Jesus through the verse al-Ma’idah,‘The Table’ (Q 5:68), which refers to the need to observe the Torah and the gospels. He began to study the Bible and explore issues such as the doctrine of the Trinity and Jesus’ death on the cross, visiting pastors with his questions. Like Jusuf Roni, Hamran also claimed that he converted to Christianity as a result of his own study, not because of the influence of any church or person. From 1961 until 1964, he was involved in several religious practices: praying according to Islam and attending the mosque every Friday; going to an Adventist Church every Saturday; and going to a church every Sunday. On 26 December 1969, Hamran Ambrie and his wife were baptized.
His conversion attracted significant controversy after his Muslim friend and journalist, H Arsyad Maran, wrote an article in the newspaper Harian Utama in Banjarmasin entitled ‘A Prominent Figure of the Movement becomes a Christian.’ In response, on 6 May 1972, Hamran wrote an open letter titled ‘Statement of Confession.’ Islamists reacted strongly to his confession, and challenged and condemned his decision. Despite the public backlash, he continued to publish and lecture, while also working at the Bible Society. By 1979, he was regularly invited to participate in public debates and speaking engagements on issues of Muslim-Christian relations in Indonesia.
His publications, however, raised concerns for the chairperson of the national MUI Hasbullah Bakry, who strongly disagreed with his interpretation of the Qur’an. The Development and Research Commission of MUI conducted an investigation of Hamran Ambrie’s publications (MUI, 1995: 27). Then, in 1982, Hasbullah Bakry reported Hamran Ambrie to the Attorney-General, Ismail Saleh. As a result, on 11 May 1982, the Attorney-General banned the distribution and publication of 19 books written by Hamran because the books addressed and challenged issues within Islam from a Christian perspective.35
This decision was based on the Attorney-General’s power to ban books under the Subversion Law, the same law under which Jusuf Roni was found guilty.36 Letters submitted to the Attorney-General by several churches, including the national Protestant council and by Hamran Ambrie himself, failed to elicit any response from the Attorney-General.37 In addition to the national ban, some provincial authorities also banned his books due to outcries from some religious leaders. For example, on 18 October 1985, a book written by Hamran Ambrie titled The Doctrine of God’s Oneness and Polytheism was banned by the provincial Ministry of Religion in Central Java. These bans were a public condemnation and statement by the government that his teachings ‘deviated’ from Christianity and insulted Islam. He was also controversial within the Christian community because he developed a theology that insisted on using the term ‘Yahweh’ instead of ‘Allah’ for God, to distinguish converts from their Islamic past.
Despite opposition from parts of the Muslim and Christian community, in 1987, Hamran Ambrie and fellow convert Suradi established the Nehemiah Christian Center in Jakarta, a biblical college to further their particular Christian theology. This was Hamran Ambrie’s last act, however, as he died later that year before criminal allegations of blasphemy could ever be brought against him. It was not until after 1998 that some of Hamran Ambrie’s students were convicted for blasphemy in court.
Cases like Jusuf Roni and Hamran Ambrie are perceived as threats by Islamists because they defy the typical stereotypes of why people convert. It is often assumed that Muslims convert to Christianity because of financial or material assistance, through the coercive means of religious groups, or through marriage (Saeed and Saeed, 2004: 110). In both these cases, it appears that Jusuf Roni and Hamran Ambrie made the decision to convert to Christianity over an extended period of time as a result of their own studies. Neither of these cases led to a conviction of blasphemy, although Jusuf Roni was imprisoned under the Subversion Law. Nevertheless, these cases created widespread controversy within both the Muslim and Christian communities. Muslims took issue with these converts as apostates and with their apologetic writings about Islam from a Christian perspective. The Christian community have also not welcomed these converts because of differences in opinion over the name of God and the use of the term Allah. This debate has gained greater prominence since 1998 in several more cases of conversions to Christianity.
Names of God: Allah, Tuhan or Yahweh?
Since 1998, several former students of Hamran Ambrie have also been accused of insulting Islam and have created controversy within the Christian community over the use of the word Allah, God. The first is the Suradi Case, which concerned a former student of Hamran Ambrie and co-founder of the Nehemiah Christian Center known only by the name Suradi. In 1987, he became the leader of the Nehemiah Christian Center after Hamran Ambrie passed away. He changed his name to Suradi ben Abraham or Eliezer ben Abraham because he believed that all Christians are descendants of Abraham.
In 1990, Suradi published five tracts titled ‘What is God’s Name?’ He published a Bible that replaced some Indonesian words with the original Hebrew word, such as replacing Tuhan (God) with Yahweh, Allah (God) to Elohim, Yesus (Jesus) to Yesua, and so on. He was also involved in the publication of a bulletin, books and cassette tapes that were perceived to be insulting to Islam. According to Duta Pranowo, general secretary of the Indonesian Bible Society, Suradi also published a version of the Bible in which he changed Allah, the word used for God in the most common translation of the Bible in Indonesian, to Elohim.38 He also published several inflammatory books that criticized the key tenets of Islam, claiming that the revelation received by Muhammad was from Satan and that the Qur’an is not a holy book. In the February 1991 edition of Gema Nehemia, he published the article ‘Jesus Christ was Called Isa Almasih in the Qur’an.’ The head of the national MUI, Hasan Basri, considered Suradi’s claims to be insulting to Islam and reported him to the police (Gatra, 5 May 2001).
Shortly after this, the Bandung Ulama Community Forum issued a death fatwa against Suradi and another member of the Nehemiah Christian Center, H.A. Poernama Winangun.39 The fatwa was issued against Winangun (ben Abraham), alias H. Amos, because he wrote a book in which he claimed that the prophet Isa (Jesus) is the same as God, that Muhammad raped a girl, and that Muslims worship the Ka’bah40 as an object rather than God (Gatra, 21 March 2005). As a result of the threat posed by the death fatwa, it is rumored that Suradi fled to America and so the complaints against him never reached the courts. The Nehemiah Christian Center also closed shortly after the death fatwa was issued.
Suradi’s publications, however, were not only considered insulting by Islamists, but met with resistance by many from the Christian community because of the issue of the name of God. In Indonesia, the Arabic word for God, Allah, is widely used and accepted by both Muslims and Christians (Steenbrink, 2003: 1). The reason for controversy in this case is that Suradi was convinced that Allah should be deleted from the Bible because it is the Arabic word used by Muslims for God and in his opinion is therefore not suitable to be used by Christians. Many churches strongly disagreed with his claims.
Tjen (2008), a linguist and consultant for the Indonesian Bible Society, highlights the use of the name Yahweh or other terms for God instead of Allah in four main movements in Indonesia spanning from the 1970s to 2000s. These movements include a wide range of Christian religious leaders, many of whom established their own church denomination. These churches generally promote the idea of ‘going back to the Hebrew roots’ of Christianity, and some of them even sing songs or pray in Hebrew during services. This idea, that Christians should not use the word Allah for God, is usually espoused by converts from Islam who feel the need to distance themselves from their Islamic past and find a way to distinguish between what they see as the God of Christianity and the God of Islam.
The debate within the Christian community over the use over the term Allah is highlighted in a court case initiated by Yeremiah Leonard, a theological student, against the Indonesian Bible Society (Lembaga Alkitab Indonesia). The Bible Institute originates from the British and Foreign Bible Society set up in Indonesia in 1814 and the Dutch Bible Society set up in 1816 (Sairin, 1996b: 244). It was officially formed in 1954 to print translations of the Bible in both Indonesian and local dialects for the Catholic and Protestant community in Indonesia. The Indonesian translation of the Bible has long used the word Allah.41
In this application lodged with the court in 2007, Yeremiah accused the Indonesian Bible Society of deceiving the Christian community by changing the name of Yahweh to Allah. He argued that the word Yahweh should not be translated as Allah but Tuhan, demanded that the Indonesian Bible Society stop printing versions of the Bible that use the word Allah for God, and asked for an official apology from the Indonesian Bible Society. The claim, however, was made without reference to any law or breach of an offence, and his lawyer was found not to be qualified according to Law 18/2003 on the Legal Profession.42 The application was dismissed by the court on these grounds.43
Aside from the issue of the name of God, there have been a number of other cases of high-profile conversions since Suradi. In 2001, a student of Suradi’s, Endang Yesaya, who converted to Christianity at the age of 44, was also accused of blaspheming Islam by the Muslim Communication Forum (Forum Komunikasi Jemaat Muslim) of Cicurug, West Java. He persisted with Suradi’s use of the word Yahweh. Moreover, his publications were perceived as a challenge to Islam because he often used quotations from the Qur’an to justify or explain the ‘truth’ of Christianity. In 2001, Endang was convicted by a court under article 156a of the Criminal Code and sentenced to four years’ jail (Gatra, 5 May 2001). He, along with others such as Jusuf Roni, was listed on the website of the Anti-Apostasy Forum (FAKTA), a radical Islamic group, as a one of the ‘founding members of the apostasy movement’ in Indonesia.44
In another case, in 2001, Pastor Ahmad Wilson from Solo (Central Java) was accused of insulting Islam for declaring on a local radio station that ‘Before he became Muslim, the Prophet Muhammad was a Christian.’ The Solo Islamic Youth Front reported Ahmad Wilson to the police, and he was later sentenced by a court under article 156a of the Criminal Code to five years’ jail for blaspheming Islam (Gatra, 5 May 2001). Other controversial public converts from Islam to Christianity include Edhie Sapto, a convert from Madura, and one of his followers and fellow converts Yosua Adhie, a convert from East Java (see ICG, 2010: 3, 14).
There are also some high-profile converts from Christianity to Islam in Indonesia,45although none have been prosecuted for blaspheming Christianity. One example of a high-profile convert is Roger Maramis, now known as Yusuf Syahbudin Maramis (son of the former Minister of Finance Alexander Andris Maramis), who converted from Christianity to Islam in the 1980s after claiming to have received a vision from God (Republika, 1 September 2002). Another example is Irena Handoko, a former nun and Chinese Indonesian who went public with her conversion to Islam and has written articles in Islamic newspapers. Her conversion not only caused concern in Catholic circles,46 but also in the Muslim community, with groups such as ARIMATEA, which aims to prevent apostasy, casting doubt on the validity of her conversion (Sabili, 1 July 2009). There is no evidence that Christian converts to Islam have been convicted of blaspheming Islam, and only a small number of Christians accused of blasphemy are ever taken to court.
These instances of conversion indicate the public controversy surrounding conversions from Islam to Christianity, or vice versa. Converts are often disowned by the church because of its concern to maintain good relations with the Muslim community. The rejection of the use of the term Allah by some converts to Christianity has been the cause of tension within the Christian community, because they perceive those who use the term Allah as ‘non-Christian.’ Converts to Christianity also face threats and pressure to return to Islam from hardliners. There have also been accusations that Christians who are intending to proselytize Muslims are insulting Islam.
Evangelical Christians insulting Islam: the Malang Case
The largest number of people accused of blasphemy in one case was the Malang Case, in which 41 Christians were brought to court. The incident concerned a Christian prayer meeting in late December 2006, held at the Asida Hotel in Malang, East Java. The event was organized by the East Java, Bali and East Nusa Tenggara branch of the Indonesian Students Service Organization (Lembaga Pelayanan Mahasiswa Indonesia, LPMI). LPMI is an Evangelical Christian organization that is linked to the international, American-based Christian organization ‘Campus Crusade for Christ.’ LPMI began in Indonesia in 1968 and it is a member of the national Evangelical council, the Communion of Pentecostal Churches in Indonesia (Persekutuan Gereja-gereja Pantekosta di Indonesia, PGLII).47 There were about 100 Christians at this event. Some of those who attended wore ‘traditional’ Indonesian Muslim clothing, including headscarves for the women, while the men wore sarongs and kopiah, a flat, oval traditional hat. This event was recorded on video, and the video came to the attention of Muslim leaders in the area.
Reports are mixed as to the contents of the video, with several versions of the video in circulation and claims by LPMI that the original video recording of the event had been tampered with. In some versions of the tape, participants prayed for the conversion of Muslim political leaders and made statements that were insulting to Islam. For example, one participant was quoted as saying that the teachings of the Qur’an are deviant and that they are the cause of ‘radicalism’ and hatred (Supreme Court, 2008a: 3). In addition, the participants placed a Qur’an on the ground and stepped on it, which is considered to be highly offensive to Muslims. This is because the verse ‘only those who are clean can touch the Qur’an’ is interpreted literally by some Islamists, while others emphasize the need for respect of the true meaning of the Qur’an (Q 56:77–79). It is also based on other hadith that encourage Muslims to place the Qur’an in the highest place in a room.
In 2007, due to the circulation of the video, the leader of the Batu branch of Muhammadiyah and the executive secretary of MUI, Muhammad Nidzhom Hidaytullah, submitted a complaint to the police. In April 2007, 41 Christians, including 36 men and five women, were detained by police in Malang, East Java, on allegations of insulting Islam. Another 60 Christians, wanted for the same alleged crime, are believed to have fled to the provinces of Bali and West Nusa Tenggara.
As news of the arrests spread, both local and national Christian organizations were quick to condemn the actions of LPMI. For example, on 5 April 2007, the three largest national Protestant and Evangelical organizations, the national Protestant council (PGI), PGLII and Communion of Pentecostal Churches in Indonesia (PGPI), issued a media release that condemned the actions of the Christians in the Malang Case (Media Release, 2007). These organizations declared that the events that occurred in Malang were inconsistent with the values of the Protestant Church in Indonesia and condemned the incident for its potential to incite issues of ‘SARA’ (ethnicity, religion, race and inter-group relations), particularly religious tensions. The media statement also expressed hope that this incident would not damage inter-religious relations in Indonesia (Media Release, 2007). This is also evidence of the extent to which Christian organizations went to distance themselves from the accused, as well as from the practices of Evangelical organizations, in order to contain the tension and maintain good relations with the Muslim community.
In May 2007, a meeting of religious leaders from all faiths of the General Communication Forum (Forum Komunikasi Umum) took place at the MUI office. Protestant leaders condemned the video and branded it as the actions of a small minority of Christians outside of the mainstream church. Church leaders offered apologies to the Muslim community for the behavior of the Christians at the LPMI meeting. This meeting was followed by a service in which 3,000 Christians from several different churches applauded a line of Christian speakers who apologized to Muslims for the incident (Asia Sentinel, 22 May 2007). Muslim and Christian leaders therefore worked hard to contain the incident, to the effect that this case is not well known in Indonesia outside of Malang. Nevertheless, the case still proceeded to court.
During the trial, the Prosecution’s arguments were supported by the presence of radical Islamic groups Islamic Defenders Front (FPI), Hizbut Tahrir Indonesia (HTI) and the Indonesian Mujahidin Council (MMI). On 6 September 2007, the District Court of Malang sentenced the 41 Christians under article 156a of the Criminal Code to five years in prison for blasphemy (District Court, 2007a). In November 2007, two of the 41 found guilty appealed to the High Court of East Java in Surabaya and their sentence was reduced from five years to three years and six months (High District Court, 2007). In 2008, two of the accused appealed again to the Supreme Court. The accused alleged that the video relied on as evidence by the court of first instance had been tampered with (Supreme Court, 2008a: 11). The Supreme Court decided that it could not rule on the issue of the validity of evidence, because the court at cassation can only deal with matters of law, not matters of fact. The court also highlighted the fact that this incident ‘had the potential to cause SARA conflict and seriously insult, defame and hurt the feelings of the Muslim community’ (p.4). On 3 April 2008, the court therefore refused the request for cassation.
The Malang Case demonstrates the remarkable influence and management skills of religious leaders, both Muslim and Christian, who were able to contain this case and ensure it did not continue to inflame inter-religious relations. There have been several other cases of Christians convicted for insulting Islam since then. For example, in 2010, Abraham Felix Grady, a 16-year-old Christian high school student from Bekasi, was convicted for stepping on the Qur’an after the photo of his actions circulated on the internet (ICG, 2010). In 2011, a Christian man, Antonius Bawengan, was convicted for five years under article 156a of the Criminal Code in the Temanggung District Court (Central Java) for distributing pamphlets that contained very explicit and vulgar criticisms of the teachings of Islam (District Court, 2011). There have also been some cases of allegations of insulting Christianity within the Christian community.
Intra-group disputes
There have only been three prosecutions for insulting Christianity since 1998. In all cases involving allegations that Christianity has been insulted, the accused claimed to be ‘Christian’ but was considered to be ‘deviant’ according to the teachings of Christianity. The first case is the Pondok Nabi Case. Mangapin Sibuea, 59, was a Protestant pastor for over 16 years, before forming the Pondok Nabi sect in 1999 which claimed about 280 followers. He taught that the world would end on 10 November 2003. There were allegations of abuse and reports that some children from this group had tried to kill themselves (Rumadi, 2007: 37–44). In 2004, he was convicted as the leader of the Pondok Nabi sect under article 156a of the Criminal Code and sentenced to two years’ jail for blaspheming Christianity by the District Court of Bale Endah, Bandung.
In the second case, the leader and six followers of the Christian sect known as Zion City of Allah, which is a splinter group of the Timor Evangelical Church, were accused of insulting Christianity (Jakarta Post, 4 June 2009; 6 June 2009). The allegations were based on claims that the group instructed its followers not to take communion and forbade wedding ceremonies in church. Although originally reported as a complaint filed by the Timor Evangelical Church, the leaders of the church claimed they had not filed a complaint against Zion City (Constitutional Court, 2010c: 18). On 3 November 2009, the Kupang District Court sentenced Nimbrot Lasbuan and his followers to six months in prison under article 156(a) of the Criminal Code.
Another case of insulting Christianity was the Last Supper Case, although it did not reach the courts. On 8 February 2008, a small, Jakarta-based student group known as the Alliance of Christian Students and Youth (Aliansi Mahasiswa dan Pemuda Kristen Indonesia) filed a complaint with the police in Jakarta against the weekly Tempo magazine. The complaint concerned the front page of the 4–10 February 2008 issue of the magazine, which depicted a satirical version of Leonardo da Vinci’s ‘The Last Supper’ with President Soeharto having dinner with his six children, suggesting an analogy between Soeharto and Jesus (Jakarta Post, 9 February 2008; 11 February 2008).
The Alliance claimed that this picture blasphemed Christianity. Harian Komentar (6 February 2008), a newspaper based in the majority-Christian province of Manado, described Tempo’s cover as ‘certainly offensive,’ because, in its opinion, ‘Christians consider the image of the Last Supper to be sacred.’
Representatives of eight Catholic organizations went to the magazine’s office the next day in protest. Tempo apologized to the Christian community and this was accepted by the national Protestant and Catholic councils, thus avoiding court proceedings. This is perhaps comparable to the Monitor Case in 1990, except that it is about insulting Christianity, not Islam.48 This case also mirrors the Danish cartoon controversy, which involved portrayals of the Prophet Muhammad that angered Muslims worldwide.49
The third case occurred in 2011 when Pastor Hadassah, a Christian pastor, was reported to police by former members of her church, who accused her of brainwashing children and of claiming to have the authority of God. In 2012, she was found not guilty by the Bandung District Court. I will turn now to look at the wider social context of insults to Islam as illustrated by the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case.
Contextualizing Muslim-Christian dynamics
The Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case was another case of a Christian who had converted from Islam being accused of insulting Islam. In the previous sections I explored the reasons why particular individuals or groups have been accused of, and convicted for, blasphemy. I now turn to examine the history of Muslim and Christian communities in Tasikmalaya in order to contextualize the Apostasy Case. The majority of cases brought under article 156a of the Criminal Code since 1998 were heard in courts in the province of West Java, East Java, Central Java and Jakarta. With the exception of Jakarta, the population of these provinces are over 90 per cent Muslim and a history of radical Islam. Several factors contributed to the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, including the history of support for radical Islamic groups; other cases of blasphemy in the area; and the push for local regulations based on Islamic law, which indicates support for an Islamist agenda.
The first Dutch Mission Society (Nederlandsche Zendings Vereeniging, NZV) missionaries came to Tasikmalaya in the late 1880s. In 1889, A. Muller moved to Tasikmalaya as a missionary of NZV along with several Christian families, and opened a school there (Soejana, 1997: 280). The first baptisms took place in December 1899, involving 19 people from three families, most of them Chinese (Soejana, 1997: 281). In 1900, Muller was replaced by J.H. Blinde. Once Blinde returned to Bandung in 1903, there were no longer any permanent foreign missionaries posted in Tasikmalaya, although a small, majority-Chinese Christian community had been established (Soejana, 1997: 443).50 In 2010, the city of Tasikmalaya had fewer than 800 Christians (BPS, 2010).51
In terms of the Islamic community, Muslims make up 98 per cent of the population in Tasikmalaya. Aside from being known as an area of major support for Darul Islam (as discussed in Chapter 3), Tasikmalaya is commonly referred to as the ‘City of a Thousand Pesantren.’52 This is because there is a strong emphasis on Islamic education in the area, with the city of Tasikmalaya boasting the five largest pesantren (Islamic boarding schools) in West Java.
The history of support for radical Islamic groups in Tasikmalaya is significant because blasphemy cases often proceed to court as a result of violent attacks by radical Islamists on the accused or a place of worship. Accusations of blasphemy are often used to justify such acts of violence.
One of the key instigators in the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case was Kiai Zenzen, the leader of a local pesantren, leader of a pesantren collective known as Forum Silaturahmai Pondok Pesantren, leader of the Laskar Brigade Taliban53 and of MMI. He claimed that his Taliban Brigade (which he emphasized has no links to the Taliban in Afghanistan) aimed to guard against maksiat (immoral behavior), such as drunkenness, gambling and adultery.54 He is an example of leaders who rally santri to protest for a particular cause, such as the demonstration that turned violent against Abraham just hours before he was arrested for blasphemy. Aside from the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, many other groups or individuals accused of blasphemy have had their homes or places of worship attacked. Large crowds of radical Muslims often turn up in force at the court hearings, as a means of intimidating the judiciary.
In particular, radical Islamists have also targeted places of worship. The chairperson of the Inter-religious Harmony Forum of Tasikmalaya, Uta Widjaya, admitted there are often problems over permits for churches in the area. For example, there were issues with a shop owned by a Chinese man that was being used as a place of prayer for Christians. In another incident, a church building destroyed in an earthquake met with strong protest from the community for a permit to rebuild, although this was partly caused by confusion over whether the church had obtained a permit for the original building.55
The Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case is not the only case of blasphemy that has caused tensions between Muslims and Christians in Tasikmalaya. In December 1996, a large number of churches and other buildings were burnt down in riots, known as Natal Kelabu (Ash Christmas).56 The violence in 1996 was sparked by the Saleh Case. As mentioned above, in December 1996, rumors that Saleh had fled to a nearby church after his trial for blasphemy led to the destruction of 13 churches, a Confucian temple, a Buddhist temple, 89 shops and 12 police stations, as well as factories, schools, banks, hotels and vehicles (Purdey, 2006: 58–59). During this period of conflict, many Chinese took shelter in government offices and military complexes, which fuelled suspicions of collusion between Chinese businesses and local government officials (Purdey, 2006: 63).
Although ignited by a case of blasphemy within Islam, this incident was partly the result of a history of tension between Muslims and (Chinese) Christians in Tasikmalaya (Purdey, 2006: 63). The history of anti-Chinese sentiment in Tasikmalaya is important because, in the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, the court noted that Abraham was employed by the local majority-Chinese church (District Court, 2006a: 63). The church is a Pentecostal church with about 100 members, most of whom are of Chinese descent, and it is affiliated with PGI.57 In December 1996, it was one of the many churches that were attacked. Abraham was never a member or employee of the church, but only visited the church on one occasion in December 2004 to give his testimony.
There have also been other convictions for blasphemy under article 156a of the Criminal Code in Tasikmalaya. For example, in 1997, Buki Sahidin, an Islamic religious leader, was accused of supporting a Jewish agenda because a star found on the ceiling of his mosque was similar to the Star of David, a symbol of Jewish identity. Buki had also pronounced himself the Imam Mahdi (Messiah) and it was suspected that he had links with an international network of Jews. He was jailed for five years for blasphemy against Islam under article 156a of the Criminal Code (Siegel, 2000: 37–38).
In a later example in 2008, Ishak Suhendra, the Muslim leader of the Martial Arts Institute (Perguruan Pencak Silat) Pancadaya Tasikmalaya, was convicted for insulting Islam through the teachings in his book The Reality of Religion. According to MUI, his teaching was false because, for example, he promoted a wrong interpretation of the word ‘basmalah’ (bismillah), he claimed that all religions are true, that there are only three rather than five pillars of Islam, and he proscribed 50 rakaat58 in one day, rather than the orthodox position of 17. MUI issued a fatwa against his teachings and his group, and he was later arrested and sentenced to four years’ jail under article 156a of the Criminal Code. The court hearings were attended by a large crowd of supporters from FPI, as with many other cases of blasphemy (Republika, 9 October 2008; District Court, 2008a).
In addition to cases of blasphemy, there have also been some attacks on the small Ahmadiyah population in Tasikmalaya (District Court, 2006i: 21). In December 2010, the Ahmadiyah mosque and facilities in Tasikmalaya were closed by the Coordinating Board because of intense pressure from FPI.59 They claimed that Ahmadiyah had breached the 2008 national Joint Regulation banning the activities of Ahmadiyah60 and the Joint Decision by local authorities to ban the activities of Ahmadiyah in Tasikmalaya in 2005.61 A further joint decision against the Ahmadiyah was issued in 2007 when a conference held by Ahmadiyah on 21–22 April 2007 at a mosque was considered to have breached the Joint Decision of 2005.62 There have also been rumors of ‘Christianization,’ such as in 2009 when a Christian group was reported to the police by FPI because it was suspected that they had proselytized the victims of an earthquake in Tasikmalaya (Front Pembela Islam, 2009).
Another factor is the support, largely from radical Islamic groups and ulama, for several draft religious regulations that have been proposed in Tasikmalaya since mid-2000. Supporters argued that Tasikmalaya needs perda syariah as a reminder that the foundations of the city are based on the religion of Islam.63 In the opinion of Mufti, the chairperson of the Tasikmalaya branch of Hizbut Tahrir Indonesia, ‘they [HTI] appreciate and value the efforts of the government and of the city council to accommodate the desires and aspirations of the community to implement Islamic syariah.’64 Some ulama and other members of the community established a Committee for the Implementation of Islamic Syariah in Tasikmalaya, which was supported by groups such as FPI and Kiai Zenzen’s Taliban Brigade. As early as 2001, a proposal for a local regulation based on Islam was suggested at a meeting of religious leaders and government officials. This proposal was opposed by religious leaders such as Uta Widjaya of Nahdlatul Ulama and the Inter religious Harmony Forum. In early 2009, calls from some ulama for the introduction of perda syariah in Tasikmalaya intensified (Republika, 9 February 2009; 12 February 2009).
In September 2009, several years of attempts to pass perda syariah culminated in the introduction of Regulation 12/2009 on Development Based on Islamic Syariah. Regulation 12/2009 applies to all residents of Tasikmalaya, both Muslim and non-Muslim. The first major concern addressed by the Local Regulation is maksiat, which is defined widely as any deed that causes a person to sin and must be avoided including corruption, adultery, gambling, alcohol, drugs, abortion, pornography, usury, polytheism and native healing, exploitation of women and children, and spreading deviant teachings.
The second major concern is to address the ‘problems’ raised by groups considered to be ‘deviant’ and to promote the ‘true’ teachings of Islam. It requires Muslims to uphold the ‘true’ beliefs of Islam and it condemns ‘deviant’ teachings (art. 6). It aims to protect acts of worship and obliges others to respect the religious rituals of others, such as fasting during the month of Ramadan and the pilgrimage to Mecca (art. 7). Article 9 focuses on akhlaq (morals) and states that a person who disturbs ‘public order’ must be ‘rehabilitated.’ Finally, as part of the aim of promoting the ‘true’ teachings of Islam, it addresses Islamic education, obliges every Muslim to implement dakwah activities and requires the local government to support these activities. The focus on dakwah is partly in response to Christian proselytization activities. The Regulation also addressed specific Islamic practices, such as Islamic attire.
The Regulation was justified on the basis that it was necessary because the majority of the city was Muslim and because there was a need to foster tolerance between religions. It aimed to educate the community about the teachings of Islam and about behavior that is opposed to the teachings of Islam. It also endeavored to create pious, faithful Muslims and to ensure that non-Muslims are respectful of the teachings of Islam (art. 3). The regulation does not specify any punishments such as fines or jail, unlike many other local regulations (see Crouch, 2009b), but it is strong on the idea that offenders will be ‘rehabilitated.’ In 2012, there were reports that plans were underway to establish a syariah police based on the model in Aceh (Jakarta Globe, 6 June 2012).
This new regulation, and the evidence of support for religious regulations in the city of Tasikmalaya, is an indication of the influence of radical Islamic groups in the area, which has also led to violence against churches and prosecutions for blasphemy against Islam. This examination of tensions at the local level is a reflection of broader trends in national debates on the Blasphemy Law. The most prominent example is the 2010 case for judicial review of the Blasphemy Law,65 which is discussed next.
The Constitutional Court and the Blasphemy Law
Given the concerns that the Blasphemy Law was being misused to criminalize religious difference, in 2009, a coalition of nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) and several prominent individuals lodged an application for review with the Constitutional Court. The decision of the Constitutional Court articulates several unique elements of the Indonesian state-religion relationship (Constitutional Court, 2010a). It has also influenced public debate in Indonesia, which is now focused on what kind of Blasphemy Law should be in place, rather than whether there should be a Blasphemy Law.
First, the court emphasized the distinctive Indonesian social and historical religious context. The court portrayed state-religion relations in Indonesia as distinct given the unique social and historical development of religions and beliefs. The sacredness of religion, as well as its highly ‘sensitive’ nature (p.279, 294), was emphasized by the court. It focused on the ‘Indonesianness’ (KeIndonesiaan) of the interpretation of the right to religious freedom in Indonesia, and noted the difference between the practice of religion in Indonesia from other countries, particularly the West (p.274).
Second, the court held that these distinctive features mean that Indonesia is essentially a compromise between a secular state and an Islamic state. The court noted that there was no strict separation of religion and state in Indonesia (p.275). The absence of such a strict separation allows for a relationship between state and religion that permits the state to regulate the activities of religious communities, while at the same time grants concessions to the authority of religious leaders of the recognized religions66 who have a greater influence over state policies and receive state funding. The court therefore reasoned that because Indonesia is a country with a belief in God, it is not an atheist country (p.273). This implied that the state does not recognize atheism or the right of an individual not to have a religion. The court expressly declared that there is no possibility of a campaign for ‘freedom not to have a religion’ in Indonesia, and that this distinguishes the law of Indonesia from the law in the West (p.295).
Third, the court recognized the dual nature of the right to religious freedom as both an individual right and a communal right (p.295). The communal right to religious freedom is also recognized in international law, although Indonesia differs in the permissible limitations on the right. While acknowledging the state’s international obligations under the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR), the court explained that article 28J(2) of the Indonesian Constitution (see Chapter 3) allows for permissible restrictions on the right to freedom of religion or belief (p.276).
Finally, based on art. 28J(2), the court emphasized the ability of the state to limit religious freedom on the broad grounds of ‘public order’ and ‘religious values.’ The limitation of ‘public order’ was defined widely to include matters of national stability, reflecting concerns that ‘chaos’ might erupt if the Blasphemy Law were cancelled.67 The court held that the state has the right to intervene in the convictions or beliefs of a group and prohibit teachings in the interests of public order. This supported the idea that religious ‘deviancy’ leads to social disorder which was expressed by many Islamic leaders and government officials throughout the hearing of this case.
Aside from public order, the other ground that the court implicitly used to justify the Blasphemy Law is ‘religious values.’ The court highlighted that this category is in addition to the scope of the legal limitations on religious freedom recognized in the ICCPR (p.296). Limitations based on ‘religious values’ were central to the way the court dismissed the applicants’ argument that the recognition of six official religions was an unjustified limitation on the interpretation of a ‘religion’ and therefore a breach of the rights of religious groups that were not one of the official religions. The court rejected the idea that the mention of only six religions discriminated against other religions and beliefs. The court reasoned that this did not preclude or prohibit a person from practicing another religion or belief, and therefore it did not constitute discrimination (p.290). This implied that the ‘religious values’ as determined by the religious leaders of the six recognized religions protected under the Blasphemy Law are justified by this limitation.
In contrast to the majority judgment (including one concurring opinion), there was one dissenting opinion by the only female Christian judge on the bench, Justice Maria Farida (p.308–20). In a short nine-page judgment, Justice Farida dissented on the basis that the implementation of the Blasphemy Law had resulted in the violation of the right to religious freedom in some cases. She reached this conclusion by first tracing the development of the Blasphemy Law, and reading this together with the subsequent amendments to the Constitution on human rights and laws that acknowledge the right to religious freedom. She found that the mention of only six recognized religions in the Elucidation to the Blasphemy Law discriminated against aliran (mystical beliefs) (p.317). She also implied that the Blasphemy Law amounts to state interference in religion (p.319), although she did not discuss the specific parameters of the grounds for limitations on the right to religious freedom.
The decision of the Constitutional Court in the Blasphemy Case has ongoing relevance and implications for public debate in Indonesia. The affirmation of the constitutional validity of the Blasphemy Law means that individuals may still be prosecuted for the offence of blasphemy, and the state may also issue an official legal warning to religious groups that have ‘deviated’ from the teachings of a religion.
More broadly, this case appears to have re-energized efforts by the legislature to promulgate a Draft Law on Inter-religious Harmony which would include reformulated provisions on blasphemy. The 2011 Draft Law on Inter-religious Harmony contained several provisions on blasphemy. The basic definition and offence of blasphemy (art. 52) was the same as the Blasphemy Law, but it also contained a list of prohibitions against certain behavior that could be considered to be blasphemy. This included a prohibition on using spoken or written words to threaten another religious community; to publish writings or pictures that insult another religious community; or to hold a public performance that is not according to the teachings of another religion (art. 44, 49). In comparison to the existing Blasphemy Law, this is a more detailed definition of what constitutes blasphemy. This may provide greater guidance to the courts, although the offence would still only apply to the six recognized religions and so would not prevent the criminalization of the beliefs of religious minorities.
In 2011, several civil society groups proposed alternative draft laws that would place greater emphasis on the protection of the religious freedom for all individuals, regardless of their religion or belief, or lack of belief.68 One of the key features of each of these proposals, however, was that they would retain the criminalization of blasphemy, although each sought to redefine the offence in different ways. This debate remains unresolved, and the content of the criminal offence of blasphemy in Indonesia is likely to remain the source of public controversy for some time to come.
Conclusion
The Blasphemy Law is a public order offence in Indonesia that has led to a string of prosecutions of individuals who are accused of insulting Islam. The latest attempt to revise the Blasphemy Law through the Draft Law on Inter-religious Harmony 2011 further defines the offence of blasphemy, although the draft is unlikely to be passed in its current form. Instead, the existing offences have increasingly been used by Islamists to condemn ‘deviant’ Islamic groups and to punish Christians considered to have insulted Islam by making a public scapegoat of them through criminal proceedings.
There has now been a large number of people convicted for insulting Islam. A significant proportion of these cases have involved Christians who have been accused of insulting Islam. This examination of narratives of conversion during the New Order, such as those of Yusuf Roni and Hamran Ambrie, shows that they were perceived as a threat to political power and provided an opportunity for Soeharto to strengthen his alliance with the Muslim community in the 1990s. These converts were not only rejected by the Christian community, but were also met with strong opposition from the Muslim community. Under the New Order, however, no Christians were convicted by a court for the offence in article 156a of the Criminal Code for insulting Islam. Instead, there were several highly political cases in which Muslims were convicted for insulting Islam, mostly through national publications or through plesetan, a play on words that was considered to be disrespectful to Islam.
Since 1998, conversions between Islam and Christianity and insults to religion have often been the subject of national debate. There has been a large number of people, usually the leaders of small Islamic sects, convicted for insulting Islam under article 156a of the Criminal Code because their teachings do not conform to a particular interpretation of Islam. This chapter has also shown, however, that there has been a significant number of Christians either accused of or convicted for insulting Islam under the same provision, many of whom were converts from Islam to Christianity. This includes the Malang Case in which 41 Christians were convicted for insulting Islam because of the video of a prayer meeting in which the Christians prayed ‘against’ Islam and stepped on the Qur’an. Although this case was addressed swiftly and contained by religious leaders on all sides, it demonstrates that radical Islamic groups are actively reporting cases to the police and putting pressure on law enforcement agencies to prosecute the accused.
The Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case is one example of how Indonesia’s general law on insulting religion, commonly referred to as the ‘Blasphemy Law,’ is actually used like an apostasy law. Since 1998, it has been used to shame and condemn deviants publicly within Islam as well as Muslims who have converted to Christianity. This is because of the deep concern among Islamists about conversions from Islam to Christianity, although there are no statistics to suggest this occurs often. It is also a reflection of the way a weak judiciary at the local level can be influenced by hardliners to condemn and convict converts publicly in court.

7  Conclusion
The violence and conflict between Muslims and Christians in Indonesia prior to and during the initial years of the transition to democracy, as canvassed in Chapter 1, has since been brought under control. Ongoing tensions and sporadic violence continue to surface, however, in part due to the haphazard intimidation campaigns initiated by radical Islamists against Christians and other religious minorities. This book set out to consider how Islamists have opposed Christian proselytization, the impact this has had on government policies, and the influence on the resolution of legal disputes between Muslims and Christians post-1998. These issues were explored through a consideration of the broader socio-political context in which these disputes have taken place, the strategies of resistance used by radical Islamists, and the increasing resort to legal mechanisms to legitimize their claims.
In doing so, this book has demonstrated that the cumulative effect of a range of Islamist responses – including violence against Christians, initiating law reform based on Islamic law, and pursuing prosecutions against Christians – have had a significant influence on the three branches of government, the executive, legislature and judiciary. This is due to the politicization of religion under democracy and the decentralization of power to the local level, underlined by ongoing, long-held fears that Islamists have of Christianization. This has limited the extent to which the resolution of legal disputes can accommodate religious pluralism.
Muslim–Christian tensions in context
There have always been tensions between Muslims and Christians in Indonesia, and these need to be understood in the socio-political and historical context. This tension began with the arrival of Christianity in Indonesia, and the perceived and real links between the colonial powers and Christian missionaries under colonialism. After independence in 1945, and particularly under President Soeharto (1966–98), religious communities were kept under tight control as part of attempts to manage the expression and practice of religion in order to maintain political power. From the 1950s to the 1970s, however, concerns arose because of reports that emerged of a large number of people converting to Christianity, as well as to other religions.
In 1965, President Soekarno issued a regulation that made it an offence to insult a religion, now known as the Blasphemy Law. The Blasphemy Law was a concession to the growing insistence from Islamists that religious identity must be fixed and that religious leaders need to maintain the power to protect the ‘orthodox’ teachings of their religion. This legitimized the beginning of a campaign continued by Soekarno’s successor, Soeharto, to ensure all citizens identified with a world religion, as opposed to animism, atheism or communism. The categorization of citizens into one of the six recognized religions was one of the primary ways that the state sought to control religious communities.
Related to the issue of religious identity was the debate over proselytization, which concerned whether a person who already had a religion could be the target of proselytization. The methods used to ‘win’ converts were also a point of contention. In the late 1970s, boundaries on proselytization were introduced through regulations issued by the Ministry of Religion. These regulations effectively affirmed the position of some Muslim leaders who argued that proselytization should not be aimed at a person who already has a (state-approved) religion. If proselytization towards a person who already has a religion were allowed, it was feared that this might destabilize the idea of religious identity as fixed, certain and permanent, and that this could lead to public disorder. The regulations also forbid forms of ‘deceptive’ proselytization, such as the distribution of free food or financial assistance, which are popularly seen as associated with efforts at Christianization in Indonesia. While Christian leaders agreed that the distribution of aid should not be used in a deceptive manner, they felt that banning proselytization towards people who have a religion was an unacceptable limitation on the right to proselytize freely and compromised their religious mandate to share their faith. These regulations carry no specific legal sanction, however, and have never been actively enforced by the state. The antagonism over Christianization, reflected in the development of state policies on religion, was exacerbated by the challenges of the transition to democracy and decentralization.
Prior to 1998, Indonesia was ruled by an extremely centralized and authoritarian regime that tightly controlled public and political expressions of religion. Opposition to Christian proselytization, as well as the activities of other religious minorities, has intensified since 1998 because of the increasing opportunities and freedom for all religious groups to practice and express their religion and beliefs. Radical Islamists have had a disproportionate voice in public debates on religion and law reform because they have often taken the law into their own hands with little opposition from law enforcement agencies. Government ambivalence towards radical Islamists is partly explained by the persistence of corruption and the competitive political environment, which has left political parties clamoring for the votes of the Islamic majority.
Local politics has become increasingly important due to the devolution of power to local governments. The case studies in this book illustrate that the distribution of power in the province of West Java has been influenced by local histories of Darul Islam and a radical Islamic agenda. Efforts to introduce perda syariah (Islamic religious regulations) at the local level have been supported by both Islamic-based and nationalist political parties. For example, such proposals have received support from the Islamic-based Prosperous Justice Party (Partai Keadilan Sejahtera, PKS) in Depok, and the nationalist political party Golkar in Tasikmalaya and Indramayu.
This demonstrates the highly competitive political environment, in which support for religious regulations cuts across party lines, and the largely symbolic and political nature of these laws, which are rarely enforced. Although these regulations generally only apply to Muslims, in practice there is ambivalence about their application and non-Muslim minorities may feel social pressure to comply. This has placed a strain on Muslim-Christian relations at the local level, and has been perceived with concern by Christians as well as by progressive Muslims, who agree that it is inappropriate for the state to set and enforce such standards. The anxiety felt by the Christian community is not because there are Muslim efforts to proselytize Christians, but because of the perceived social pressure on non-Muslims to conform to Islamic regulations. Similarly, attempts to introduce regulations based on Christianity in West Papua have been greeted with concern by Muslims.
As the decentralization of power to local governments has increased, this has led to the politicization of religion at the local level. This is highlighted by three contemporary issues that have been the cause of legal disputes between Muslims and Christians: child conversion and education; building new churches; and attempts to challenge and therefore insult Islam (particularly by converts to Christianity). On each of these issues, Islamists have used similar methods to oppose Christian activities.
Strategies of resistance
In all of the case studies, three general approaches were taken by radical Islamists. First, radical Islamists have used violence and threats of violence to campaign against Christianization. Local coalitions of radical Islamists have been mobilized to influence local authorities and legal proceedings by staging demonstrations at court hearings, placing pressure on the judiciary to submit to their demands. In disputes over church permits, radical Islamists have been responsible for preventing or delaying the construction of churches. In cases of blasphemy, such as the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, the person accused of blasphemy is often arrested due to vigilantism by radical Islamists and the risk of further violence. The vigilante campaigns of radical Islamic groups such as the Islamic Defenders Front have wielded disproportionate political power due to the ambivalence of the government towards religious organizations that use violence to achieve their aims.
Aside from vigilante violence, a second strategy to resist Christian proselytization has been campaigns for legislative reform based on Islamic law. The attention of Islamists has shifted from the failed attempts to reinstate the Jakarta Charter to efforts to influence the national and regional legislative agenda on key issues such as religious education and proselytization. Islamists have used legal and political processes, and institutions such as the Ministry of Religion and the Indonesian Ulama Council, to introduce national and local laws based on Islam. For example, law reform on religious instruction in favor of the religious majority has established legal obligations and placed increasing pressure on private Christian schools to provide Islamic religious education for Muslim students. Some Christian schools have refused to comply with this arrangement, which has only reinforced stereotypes that Christian schools aim to convert non-Christian students. Further, the pattern of religious-based regulations in the regions has heightened Christian fears of Islamization – that is, that Indonesia might become an Islamic state based on Islamic law. Despite the fact that this is highly unlikely to eventuate, many Christians perceive that such legal reform would only have negative consequences for religious minorities. In addition to the introduction of national laws, many Ministerial Regulations remain in place and reiterate the New Order agenda of control over religious communities, such as the regulation of places of worship. These regulations have been manipulated by Islamists to justify vigilante action in the absence of a state response.
The third central means of opposing Christianization has been the manipulation of legal processes and procedures. Radical Islamists have actively reported alleged breaches of these laws and pursued prosecutions through the Indonesian Ulama Council and other Islamic community organizations. This has placed pressure on law enforcement agencies to prosecute individuals for actions that are considered to be offensive to Islam. In the resolution of these disputes, democratic institutions, such as the courts, are intended to protect the rights of citizens and uphold the rule of law. Instead, the courts are generally weak, corrupt and easily intimidated, particularly at the regional level. There is an ongoing need to strengthen the independence of the judiciary, so that criminal cases concerning religion are decided impartially, particularly at first instance in the local courts.
Judicialization of inter-religious disputes
The three case studies located in the majority-Muslim province of West Java illustrate how inter-religious disputes have become increasingly judicialized since the introduction of democracy. Leaders from the Indonesian Ulama Council or radical Islamic groups such as the Islamic Defenders Front have lodged criminal allegations and pressured the police to investigate issues over Christian proselytization as a way of seeking legal legitimacy for their religious opinions and authority. Christian leaders have also lodged cases for judicial review of legislation or of government decisions with the courts. This demonstrates greater recourse to the courts in relation to disputes between religious communities, or between a religious community and the government, which is in striking contrast to the handful of cases brought to the courts under Soeharto.
One of the reasons for the occurrence of these cases in court is the concerted agenda of radical Islamists at the local level and the pressure they exert on the police and Attorney-General’s office to investigate and prosecute. In both the Indramayu Religious Education Case and the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, it was local religious leaders who reported the case to the Indonesian Ulama Council, who in turn lodged a complaint with the police. In the Depok Church Permit Case, it was the leader of a local mosque who formed a group specifically to counteract the construction of the church. In all three cases, there is evidence that these local groups were mobilized, encouraged and supported by radical Islamists from outside the area such as the Islamic Defenders Front or by criminal gangs such as the Betawi Brotherhood Forum. Despite this criminal undercurrent, the perpetrators of vandalism and property damage of church buildings often go unidentified and unprosecuted. In cases where the perpetrators are put on trial, court hearings have been mobbed by large numbers of protestors, which has an intimidating effect. In both the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case and the Indramayu Religious Education Case, the accused were convicted in court due to the wide interpretation given to the law and the targeted pressure exerted by radical Islamists on court proceedings.
The extent to which these disputes can be resolved in an independent manner that maintains civil relations between Muslim and Christian leaders partly depends on whether the Christians facing criminal charges are supported by respected Christian leaders and organizations, including political parties. The actions of some accused have been disowned by the wider church, such as those accused of blasphemy who are more likely to be evangelical or Pentecostal Christians or converts from Islam to Christianity. In other cases, the accused has received support from Christian organizations and political parties. In the Indramayu Religious Education Case, the three women were widely supported by the church, which maintained the view long held by Indonesian Christians that the government must not interfere in private religious education. In the Depok Church Permit Case, the church had the support of the Christian community, as evident from the extensive coverage it received in Christian media. This is partly because of the powerful position of the Batak Church within the Christian community in Indonesia, the history and influence of Batak lawyers in the legal profession, and because many churches across Indonesia identify with problems over permits. Some political parties such as the Christian-based Peace and Prosperous Party have been ardent supporters of congregations that face opposition to the construction of their church. It was also actively supportive of the three women in the Indramayu Religious Education Case.
In contrast to the first two cases, however, the actions of the accused in the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case were not supported by the church or by any Christian political parties. This is often the case in instances of blasphemy, especially when the accused has converted from Islam and may be particularly zealous in sharing their faith or insensitive in their denunciation of Islam. The primary concern of the national Protestant and Catholic councils has been to maintain good relations with the wider Muslim community by condemning such provocative behavior. The lack of support from Christian leaders, the media and political parties, combined with the influence of radical Islamists on law enforcement agencies, may be one of the reasons for the inevitability of convictions in cases of blasphemy.
The resolution of disputes has also been challenged by the emphasis placed on the differences between Muslims and Christians in terms of ethnicity. In the Depok Church Permit Case, the centrality of Batak culture and its differences from Javanese or Sundanese culture emerged. In the Indramayu Religious Education Case, Dr Rebekka’s Chinese heritage and the history of a majority Indonesian-Chinese church in Indramayu were contributing factors to the opposition in the case. In the Tasikmalaya Apostasy Case, Abraham had converted to Christianity and therefore challenged the stereotype that to be Sundanese is to be Muslim. These ethnic differences are relevant to Muslim-Christian relations because Christians are often seen by Islamists as ‘foreigners’ if ethnically Chinese, or as ‘traitors’ if they are from an ethnic group traditionally associated with Islam, such as the Sundanese.
Increasing opposition has also led to a growth in court cases as some Christian congregations seek legal remedies concerning disputes over church permits. Christians have been active in lodging complaints with non-judicial bodies such as the Ombudsman, as well as initiating court action for judicial review of government decisions. As research by Paramadina Institute (2012) has demonstrated, many church permit disputes can be resolved if a church makes the time and effort to build strong relations with the local community and obtain the support of local neighborhood leaders. In cases where good relations were not established or have broken down, and local authorities have cancelled permits, some churches have taken their cases to the courts.
The initial court victories of some Christian congregations in the Administrative Court regarding judicial review of decisions to cancel church permits provided momentum for other churches to take legal action in the face of discrimination and threats of violence from radical Islamists. This is evidence of a trend in the race to assert new-found democratic rights as religious minorities challenge laws and administrative decisions through litigation. The courts have the potential to create new checks on the conduct of lawmakers, and provide an avenue for the assertion of rights through the judicial process, which for so long have been inaccessible due to tight state control. In terms of practical outcomes, however, court decisions regarding church permits have had little effect because they may not be respected or enforced by local authorities, which are aligned with or under pressure from radical Islamists. These cases indicate the need for the national government to take a firm stance against the vigilantism of radical Islamists, reducing the risk of violent retaliation and therefore increasing the ability and willingness of local authorities to implement court decisions.
Conclusion
There will always be fears of proselytization between Muslims and Christians because a key aspect of both religions is its missionary nature. This book demonstrates how both Muslims and Christians, as the two most powerful religions in Indonesia, have used legal mechanisms and institutions to address issues of concern between the two communities. Under a democratic and decentralized system of law and governance, and in the shadow of concerns over Christianization, the rights of the religious majority have been exercised in a way that at times deprives or compromises the rights of religious minorities. The democratic revolution and shift to decentralization in Indonesia has intensified and created greater challenges for religious tolerance, acceptance of pluralism and inter-religious relations due to the greater freedoms afforded religious groups, in stark contrast to Soeharto’s previous authoritarian regime. This has created room for Islamists to exert disproportionate influence on the executive, legislature and judiciary in the absence of a strong state.
Many Islamists continue to promote the need for greater regulation of religious activities by the state in Indonesia. Due to the history of opposition between Muslims and Christians, the accommodation of Christian proselytization will remain a challenge for Indonesian Islam within the framework of democracy. For Indonesian Christians, issues of internal disunity and the need to overcome persistent stereotypes of Christianization remain ongoing concerns that must be addressed. This may require leaders from all sides to reconsider their position on key issues such as the boundaries of proselytization, the right to religious education and the tension between freedom of speech and religious defamation.
Christian proselytization, real or perceived, is intimately related to many of the legal disputes that have emerged between Muslims and Christians in democratic Indonesia. These disputes have raised critical questions about whether religious communities and individuals, particularly minorities, can have faith and trust in legal mechanisms and institutions. The case study of West Java demonstrates that the broader phenomenon of proselytization between the world religions of Islam and Christianity can lead to deep unrest and discrimination in local contexts. Proselytization and the extent to which it can, and should, be limited by law is an ongoing issue for religious communities. Islamist opposition to Christian proselytization remains a key to understanding and addressing the escalation of such legal disputes between Muslims and Christians in Indonesia.
